Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Luciano Spalletti, allenatore della Juventus, ha parlato ai microfoni di Sky Sport dopo il successo per 4-1 dei bianconeri in Europa League contro l'Omonia Nicosia.

Di seguito le dichiarazioni del tecnico della Juve dalla pancia dell'Allianz Stadium.

"All'inizio abbiamo commesso un paio di incertezze che gli avversari hanno sfruttato. Naturalmente questo complica la possibilità di esprimere al meglio le proprie qualità, perché senti la pressione e il timore di subire un contropiede o qualcosa del genere. Poi Cambiaso ha rimesso un po' a posto le cose con quel grande gol e da lì la squadra ha giocato con maggiore tranquillità".

"All'Inter do 6,5 dopo la Champions. Il risultato è quello che doveva arrivare, il 2-0 va anche stretto ma ha rischiato all'inizio, poteva essere sotto 20'. E' messa bene ora in classifica, ora viene il bello o il brutto".

"La mia caratteristica migliore penso sia il tiro, sono un attaccante mobile, riesco a legare il gioco e giocare spalle alla porta. C'é da migliorare comunque, ma con questo staff, mister e compagni posso migliorare molto. Pio Esposito? "Penso di essere un po' come lui, ma quello che ha fatto é difficile da replicare. Io sono qua per migliorare il più possibile e accrescere il valore della squadra, poi a fine stagione vedremo cosa ho seminato e vedremo cosa succederà".

AI made self-hosting fun again

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jan-Lukas ElseTech, life and everything else

I’ve been self-hosting things for many years. There were times when I was quite enthusiastic about it, constantly trying out new software and tinkering with my infrastructure. But at some point, that enthusiasm faded a bit.I never really stopped self-hosting, but I became less inclined to experiment with new things. Maintaining what I already had running was enough for me.Over the last few months, however, I’ve found myself getting increasingly excited about self-hosting again. And, somewhat ironically, AI is a big reason for that.Less frictionMost of my infrastructure configuration lives in Git repositories, mainly in the form of Docker Compose files, scripts, and other configuration files. This makes it possible to use AI coding agents to work on my infrastructure in much the same way as they would work on any other software project.Whenever I want to try a new service, adjust an existing configuration, or automate something, I can now do so with considerably less effort.In the past, I would sometimes put off certain changes simply because I didn’t feel like spending an evening reading changelogs and documentation or troubleshooting issues. It’s not that I couldn’t have done these things before. But often, the amount of effort required just wasn’t worth it to me.Now, even relatively spontaneous ideas often end up becoming reality.Trying things I always found interestingThere are also technologies that I’ve always found interesting but never felt knowledgeable enough about to really make use of them.NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

I never really stopped self-hosting, but I became less inclined to experiment with new things. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

Most of my infrastructure configuration lives in Git repositories, mainly in the form of Docker Compose files, scripts, and other configuration files. This makes it possible to use AI coding agents to work on my infrastructure in much the same way as they would work on any other software project.Whenever I want to try a new service, adjust an existing configuration, or automate something, I can now do so with considerably less effort.In the past, I would sometimes put off certain changes simply because I didn’t feel like spending an evening reading changelogs and documentation or troubleshooting issues. It’s not that I couldn’t have done these things before. But often, the amount of effort required just wasn’t worth it to me.Now, even relatively spontaneous ideas often end up becoming reality.Trying things I always found interestingThere are also technologies that I’ve always found interesting but never felt knowledgeable enough about to really make use of them.NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.Automating almost everythingSomething I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.Back to more powerful hardwareAs I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.Building my own toolsBut it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.Just because I can doesn’t mean I shouldOf course, there are downsides to all of this.Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.A somewhat ironic development?I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. Kanye West and Bianca Censori Plan to Renew Wedding Vows, Family Member Reveals

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Travel  |  Read Source →

Kanye West and Bianca Censori are apparently ready to say "I do" all over again ... with one of Bianca's relatives spilling the beans about a reported vow renewal next month.

Bianca's aunt Kelly dropped the wedding bombshell during a call to Australian radio show "Fifi, Fev & Nick" Wednesday ... revealing that several family members are preparing to travel overseas for the couple's upcoming celebration.

Kelly explained that Ye and Bianca are already married, but they're planning to exchange vows again in December ... though there's one awkward twist ... she says she wasn't invited.

Her call came after an alleged save-the-date surfaced on X earlier this month -- listing December 20 as the big day and Beverly Hills as the location -- though the invitation has not been verified.

The date would be especially fitting ... since Ye and Bianca reportedly tied the knot on December 20, 2022, in a private ceremony in Palo Alto, California, shortly after his divorce from Kim Kardashian was finalized.

And a second trip down the aisle would mark quite the turnaround for the couple. As TMZ previously reported ... Kanye and Bianca were headed for divorce in February 2025, with sources telling us both had reached out to divorce attorneys following Ye's antisemitic social media rants.

However, the two reunited that April and have been practically inseparable ever since ... frequently stepping out together and packing on the PDA.

Neither Kanye nor Bianca has publicly confirmed the reported ceremony ... but if Kelly's right, wedding bells could be ringing again in just a few weeks.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

[sorry for the delayed reply. We just finished up Part 2, should appear after the holiday.]
Thank you for your input.
typical.investor, I have to concur with StillGoing: your time period of 2010 - 2025 is too short to have probative value. You need at least a complete bear and bond market cycle. That's why we liked the post-1945, 80-year span: 36 years of bear, 40 years of bull, 4 years of renewed bear.
Agreed. I'm looking at the FRED H.15 data from April 1953 onward next—that's where the monthly series begins, and it also avoids the wartime yield-curve control era prior to the 1951 Fed–Treasury Accord.
In our thinking, the great 40-year bull 1981 - 2021 created the mother of all recency biases: of course bonds give you a coupon plus capital gain--that's just what bonds do! Not.

And of course, a bull market favors duration.
Agreed. It's why the period I looked at was actually rising rates overall.
Next, I don't think you can test a ladder against a constant maturity approach by switching from the Coleman / Ibbotson yield curve (which we used) to the Treasury HR 15 curve (which Shiller uses). I'd recommend actual purchase prices. These are available from the CRSP Treasury file--I access it through my university library subscription to WRDS.

That may be too heavy a lift--not everybody has access to a University library. But to go beyond a thoughtful contribution to the forum, to something you could publish, I think that's the threshold: buy the bonds at the ask, hold, handle the semi-annual coupon payments.
I don't intend to test further against constant maturity, but will instead look at a hold-to-maturity ladder vs a fund or sector (like 7-10y) using the same Treasury H.15 curve.

As an investor, I realistically do have a choice of using a ladder or fund. Constant-maturity methodology isn't something that really happens in the normal course of investing I think.
Next, remember that the 5-year maturity was not a mountain peak--it was the point of rapid flattening as far as return by maturity is concerned. Calculating a return on a 10-year ladder, with maturity of 5.5 years, and showing a dozen basis points of extra return, relative to exactly 5 years, does not move the ball down the field.
Viewing a 10-year ladder as simply "5.5 years of maturity" misses an essential return mechanism of individual bonds: pull-to-par.

A constant-maturity series buys a bond, sells it a month later, and buys back the original maturity. In a rising-rate regime, that forces repeated capital losses; in a falling-rate regime, it harvests continuous capital gains. Buying a bond and holding it to maturity doesn't work that way—your return is anchored by your starting yield and pull-to-par, with rate changes only affecting coupon reinvestment.

You can see how different those two mechanisms are in the 1953–2026 H.15 data (762 rolling 10-year windows). Let's strip out the shared starting yield and look at the correlation.

Correlation of deviations from starting yield (Realized 10y CAGR − Starting Yield):
10y CMT vs. a single 10y bond held to maturity: -0.279
10y CMT vs. a rolling 0–10y HTM ladder: +0.633

For a single 10-year bond held to maturity, the rate-change risk literally points in the opposite direction of 10y CMT. In the 1953–1981 bear market, 10y CMT lagged a buy-and-hold 10-year Treasury in 100% of rolling 10-year windows (by a median of -102 bps/yr); in the 1981–2020 bull market, it beat it in 100% of windows (by a median of +163 bps/yr).

That's why I think the "coupon plus capital gain" recency bias you noted is more a constant-maturity artifact than how hold-to-maturity Treasuries behave. Of course, I acknowledge that real-world bond funds that don't hold to maturity will also realize capital gains in bull markets and capital losses in bear markets, although likely to a lesser degree than CMT—which is why I'm testing hold-to-maturity ladders against funds.
Last, much of our analysis was focused on stock-bond portfolios, and the question of which Treasury best supplements stocks. The mean-variance charts, per Markowitz, assume regular rebalancing back to the indicated weights.

Ladders do not rebalance. You need Tobin (2-fund separation theorem), not Markowitz, given a ladder. Space constraints prevented us from going there.
I, too, am very much concerned with stock-bond portfolios. Where we differ is that I find it straightforward to rebalance with a rolling ladder inside a 60/40 portfolio:

When stocks drop: Maturing rungs and semiannual coupons provide natural cash flow to buy equities. If a sharp drop requires more than the maturing rung provides, selling the shortest remaining rung is functionally identical to selling a T-bill.

When stocks surge: Trimming equities funds new top rungs at the long end of the ladder.

Any temporary drift in average maturity from selling the front rung or adding to the back rung is largely self-correcting as the remaining rungs age. And because a rolling nominal ladder carries real duration and price risk, it isn't a Tobin risk-free asset—it's a fixed-income sleeve that rebalances Markowitz-style just fine.

I do again thank you for your input and look forward to your next work.

Statistics: Posted by typical.investor — Thu Nov 26, 2026 4:45 pm

6 YA Books That Are Better Than They Get Credit For

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Young adult fiction has produced some of the biggest literary phenomena of the 21st century, from The Hunger Games to Twilight, and this trend shows no signs of slowing. Even now, YA hits like Heartstopper and A Good Girl's Guide to Murder are fixtures on the bestseller lists.

However, there are also plenty of YA gems that are a little under-sung. Perhaps bigger franchises stole their limelight, or they had their moment in the sun but then mostly faded from popularity, or they received rave reviews but struggled to reach a wide, mainstream audience. With that in mind, here are a few titles that probably deserve a little more praise and attention than they get.

"Sometimes the most beautiful things are the most dangerous." This is a fast-paced, atmospheric fantasy novel. Our protagonist is 16-year-old orphan Juliette, who works tirelessly to gift her beloved older sister, Clare, a weeklong stay at The Splendor. It's a glamorous, magical hotel on a hill known for fulfilling its guests' wildest fantasies. However, Clare returns from the trip completely changed: cold and detached, unfeeling. Determined to uncover what broke their bond, Juliette checks into the hotel herself.

Inside, she meets Henri, a talented young illusionist employed by the hotel to manipulate guests' minds and memories to craft their "Signature Experience". As Juliette digs beneath the hotel's dazzling surface, Henri realizes he is directly tied to the heartache she is trying to undo. Their dual perspectives (alternating between viewpoint chapters) drive the mystery, while layered world-building and a nuanced magic system do the rest of the heavy lifting.

"This place is like the whole world and the whole world is like this place. So where could I go?" The Noughts & Crosses series ranks among the most thoughtful YA dystopian fiction of the last quarter-century, yet it's typically overshadowed by the likes of The Hunger Games. Set in an alternate society where Black people, known as Crosses, occupy positions of power while white people, called noughts, face systemic discrimination, the novel follows two childhood friends whose growing romantic feelings threaten to destroy their lives.

Sephy Hadley belongs to a wealthy, influential Cross family, while Callum McGregor is a nought struggling against the forces seeking to determine his future. The premise is provocative, but the book offers way more than just that. For starters, the characterization is top-notch. Neither protagonist is written as a perfect hero; Sephy often struggles with her own biases and blind spots, while Callum wrestles with growing bitterness and anger.

"The Winter Knights stick together, Quint." Part of the phenomenal Edge Chronicles fantasy series, The Winter Knights centers on Quint, a squire training at the prestigious Knights Academy in the floating city of Sanctaphrax. As an unusually harsh winter threatens the city's survival, our hero finds himself caught up in a mystery involving missing knights, political rivalries, and the increasingly desperate search for stormphrax, a remarkable substance capable of weighing down the levitating rock on which Sanctaphrax rests.

The book's greatest strength is its wonderfully bizarre fantasy setting, populated by strange creatures, eccentric scholars, and fantastical technologies that feel unlike those found in more conventional fantasy adventures. All of this is realized through Chris Riddell's brilliant illustrations. However, there's also a surprising amount of darkness beneath the whimsical surface. For instance, the story explores institutional corruption, jealousy, betrayal, and the dangers of ambition.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

"All I ever wanted was for her to love me and to do something meaningful with my life." An Abundance of Katherines is generally considered John Green's worst book, though it gets a worse rap than it deserves. Fundamentally, this is an eccentric but wise coming-of-age story. It's about Colin Singleton, a former child prodigy who can solve anagrams in seconds. He's also been dumped nineteen times (he's just 17, by the way), exclusively by girls named Katherine.

Devastated after his latest breakup with Katherine XIX, Colin sets off on a spontaneous road trip from Chicago with his hilarious, Judge Judy-loving best friend, Hassan. Their aimless drive lands them in the tiny, quirky town of Gutshot, Tennessee. While staying there, Colin obsesses over inventing a mathematical theorem to predict the exact breakup date of any relationship. However, he also slowly realizes that life, friendship, and love can't be reduced to a formula.

"The entire future of western civilization depends upon me making a smiley face with this ketchup." The Recruit is the opening entry in the incredibly entertaining CHERUB series, an action-packed spy saga aimed at younger teens. It revolves around James Choke, a troubled twelve-year-old whose life falls apart following his mother's sudden death. He finds himself recruited into CHERUB, a secret British intelligence organization that trains children and teenagers to undertake dangerous undercover missions.

Although the book is primarily concerned with delivering fast-paced entertainment (including fight scenes and a little romance), author Robert Muchamore brings an unexpected degree of realism to its outrageous premise. James is frequently selfish, impulsive, and unpleasant, making him a refreshingly imperfect protagonist with a real arc. The Recruit also avoids glamorizing espionage, instead leaning into its psychological pressures and myriad ethical complications.

"The time has come to arm-wrestle some demons." This one was acclaimed on release, but you don't seem to see it come up very often in the YA conversation these days. Speak focuses on Melinda Sordino, a high school freshman who becomes a social outcast after calling the police during a summer party. Too traumatized to explain what actually happened to her at that party, Melinda retreats into near-total silence. However, her art classes gradually provide an outlet for emotions she cannot otherwise express.

What follows is an honest depiction of harm and healing, one that refuses to sugarcoat anything or offer any contrived silver linings. Plus, even in the depths of depression and isolation, the protagonist's monologue is razor-sharp and observant, as well as frequently darkly funny. In short, Speak is a modern YA classic that should be more widely read.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Scientists find a new way to study some of the ocean’s most elusive microbes

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new study sheds light on the largely unexplored microbial world beneath the ocean and its potential ecological significance.

Most of the microbes living on the deep seafloor have never grown in a lab dish. On the way to the surface, a sample loses the high pressure and steady cold its microbes are adapted to.

The sudden change can kill the microbes before anyone gets to study them.

A robotic system called DISCUSS keeps both conditions in place, from the seafloor all the way to the lab bench where single microbes are picked out. With it, a team grew a bacterium that barely grows at ordinary air pressure.

The work was led by Jing-Chun Feng of Guangdong University of Technology in China. Zhengguang Liu, a researcher in the University of Manchester Department of Chemical Engineering, was one of the paper’s corresponding authors.

“Scientists have long struggled to grow many microorganisms found in nature under laboratory conditions, limiting our understanding of how they live and function,” said Liu.

The deep sea is home to a vast diversity of microbes, many of which have adapted to extreme pressure and near-freezing temperatures.

Some deep-sea microbes even produce enzymes and other molecules.

The molecules that deep-sea microbes create can be useful for medicines, new materials, food and energy.

Scientists say that less than one percent of deep-sea microbes can be grown with normal lab techniques.

“Deep-sea microbes are particularly fascinating because they have evolved to survive extreme pressure, cold and unusual chemical conditions,” Liu said.

The testing of this technology was carried out in the South China Sea at a depth of 4,300 feet (1,300 meters) in a cold seep location.

Cold seeps are areas of the seafloor where methane and other compounds emerge at temperatures similar to those of the surrounding water.

At this depth, the water pressure would be 1,900 pounds per square inch (13 megapascals), which is about 130 times the atmospheric pressure at sea level, and water temperature would be 39°F (4°C).

Before carrying out any sampling procedure, sensors detected the levels of methane, carbon dioxide, oxygen, acidity, temperature, and pressure on the seafloor.

After evaluation by the software, only those locations with scores related to methanotrophic microbes were sampled.

The sealed samplers kept more than 98% of the seafloor pressure on the way up.

In the lab, the system built a virtual copy of the seafloor conditions from the sensor readings. Researchers call that kind of copy a digital twin, and the machine used it as the target to hold.

When a sample moved from one chamber to the next, one pump pushed while another pulled at a matched rate. That kept the pressure from jumping during the handoff.

To grow one kind of microbe on its own, a microbiologist spreads cells thinly across a soft gel. Each cell that survives grows into a small dot called a colony.

DISCUSS performs this task inside a sealed, high‑pressure chamber. A robot arm spreads the cells with a needle, and a camera watches through a sapphire window.

A force sensor in the arm lets it press lightly enough not to tear the gel. In tests, the camera software recognized colonies with 94.6% accuracy.

The needle hit its target within 0.002 inches of the intended spot. Between tasks, the system sterilized the needle using light, keeping the pressure seal intact.

“From an engineering perspective, what excites me most is how intelligent sensing, modeling and automation can help us explore biological systems that were previously difficult to study,” said Liu.

The team recovered 34 microbes and grew each one on its own.

In 11 of them, a gene that scientists use to tell microbes apart differed enough from known reference strains that they may belong to groups not yet described.

The team took a close look at two of the 34. Both turned out to be strains of known species.

One belongs to Pseudomonas songnenensis, and the other, named HP_SR10, belongs to Bacillus cereus.

To test how much each strain depended on pressure, the researchers grew both for 11 days at five pressures, from ordinary air pressure to a little above the seafloor’s.

The Pseudomonas strain grew about as well at ordinary air pressure as it did at seafloor pressure. HP_SR10 didn’t. It grew best at the same pressure as the seep it came from, and poorly at ordinary air pressure.

The authors wrote that this strain can’t be recovered with conventional methods, which grow microbes at surface pressure.

The system also kept a whole community of microbes alive and working for 180 days.

At its center was a partnership between methane-eating archaea, single-celled microbes distinct from bacteria, and bacteria that use sulfate from seawater.

Pumps fed the community methane and nutrients and flushed out waste. That kept sulfide, a byproduct that holds these microbes back at high levels, below 4 millimoles per liter.

Around day 110, both partners became noticeably more common.

The authors wrote that communities like this one typically collapse in closed lab containers with no fresh supply.

Carbon made near the ocean surface can sink to the deep ocean, and scientists still don’t fully understand how deep-sea microbes change it.

Growing a wider range of these microbes under conditions close to home could help scientists study that process.

Only two of the 34 microbes were studied in detail. The study doesn’t describe what the 11 possibly new microbes do, or whether any make useful molecules.

All of the samples also came from a single seep. The authors noted that the design could be adapted for deeper water and for very hot places such as hydrothermal vents.

Next, the team plans to run many plates at once under different conditions and to track dissolved chemicals more closely as they change.

The researchers also plan to automate growing microbes in liquid instead of on gel.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Sensors.

Sarri: ‘Atalanta cannot always recover with comebacks’ after another wild night

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Maurizio Sarri warns Atalanta ‘cannot always expect to resolve games with these comebacks’ after a 3-2 away to Borac in the Conference League, while Gianluca Scamacca is ‘lacking confidence.’

La Dea really are turning into the most unpredictable team in Italy, as just days after an epic comeback from 4-2 down to hold Inter 4-4 in Serie A, they went even better in the Conference League.

Borac made the most of dismal defending to go 2-0 up in the first half-hour, while Atalanta wasted numerous scoring opportunities with Jonathan Rowe and Charles De Ketelaere literally missing open goals.

It was only after Borac went down to 10 men that they broke through with Nikola Krstovic, then as Sebastijan Herrera also saw red for hacking down Nicola Zalewski, the turnaround in stoppages from Sead Kolasinac and Davide Zappacosta.

“The truth is that we had a good performance and created many scoring opportunities, but unfortunately right now we have this defensive fragility that makes every match more complicated,” Sarri told Sky Sport Italia.

“If you look at the statistics, we scored three goals on four chances late on, but before that managed to miss two goals from a metre out. We made mistakes in our finishing and continue to have a few defensive problems too, so naturally it’s going to get tricky with those two elements.

“The team showed great character, as it has been doing quite a lot recently, so we have to keep working on the problems that are making our matches so complicated.”

Injuries ruled out Marco Carnesecchi, Odilon Kossounou and Isak Hien, while others are making their way back from long lay-offs.

“We’ve got to keep working, try to dig out some time to do work in training, which between injuries and the fixture list is tough, but we absolutely must find a way of working on the defence and attack. It’s about the movements and distribution, which certainly weren’t of the right level tonight, and it has been a repeated problem in recent matches.

“We cannot always expect to resolve games with these comebacks, we’ve got to stop them getting this complicated in the first place.”

Notably, Atalanta only managed to get on the scoresheet once Krstovic came off the bench to replace Gianluca Scamacca, while Jonathan Rowe was again misfiring.

“Before the season began, I’d have said Scamacca was perfectly suited to my style of football, because he is very physical and has technical quality to work with the team,” noted Sarri.

“The difference is that one striker right now is feeling very confident and optimistic, while the other is in a difficult momenta and slowly trying to rebuild. I have always said that the two forwards start on the same level, and I pick whoever is in the best form. Right now, Krstovic is doing better, so he plays more, but it could just be a moment in this season that will pass.”

There was tension before the game, with some Atalanta fans complaining that their buses were delayed crossing the border into Bosnia, then they were banned from visiting the city centre and corralled into the stadium by police.

“I want to thank our fans, who travelled a long way to be here and for 97 minutes gave us a big hand. Fortunately, we managed to repay them this evening,” added Sarri.

This result puts Atalanta mid-table in the Conference League, after two victories, a defeat away to Ajax and draw with Kairat Almaty.

Principles Don't Ship: Making Responsible AI an Operating Practice

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 26 • Originally published at linkedin.com

Almost every large organisation now has a set of responsible AI principles. They are usually well written. Fairness, transparency, accountability, privacy, safety, human oversight. They sit on a web page, they were approved by a committee, and they are referenced in board papers.

Then a team ships an AI feature, and nobody can point to the moment where any of those principles changed a decision. There was no test that could have failed. There was no review with the authority to stop the release. There was no metric that would have revealed a problem after launch. The principles were real, and they had no contact with the work.

This is the gap I want to address. Responsible AI is not a statement of values. It is a set of practices that run inside the delivery process, with owners, tests, and consequences. Values that never touch a release are decoration. Here is how I think about turning them into something that ships.

A principle tells you what you care about. A practice tells you what you do on a Tuesday afternoon when a release is due and something looks off.

The difference matters because AI harm rarely comes from bad intentions. It comes from ordinary teams under ordinary deadline pressure, making reasonable-sounding choices without the information or the mandate to make a different one. A team does not decide to build an unfair system. It builds a system that works well on the data it tested, ships it on time, and never measures how it performs for the people who were underrepresented in that data.

Principles do not prevent that outcome, because principles do not run. Practices do. A required evaluation step, a named reviewer, a release checklist with items that can block, a dashboard that someone is accountable for reading. These are boring, and they are the only part of responsible AI that changes what actually reaches customers.

The useful test for any responsible AI programme is simple: show me a release that was delayed, changed, or stopped because of it. If nobody can, the programme is a communications exercise.

The most productive exercise I know is to take each principle and ask one question: what would we observe if we were violating it?

Fairness becomes "the error rate for any significant user group is not more than a defined margin worse than the overall rate." Transparency becomes "a user can tell, at the point of use, that an answer was generated and what it was based on." Privacy becomes "no personal data from one customer appears in output shown to another, and we test for it." Human oversight becomes "decisions above a defined impact threshold cannot complete without a named person approving them."

Each of those statements can be checked. Some can be automated and run on every change, like any other test. Some need a periodic review with evidence. Either way, they can fail, and when they fail, something happens.

This translation is uncomfortable, because it forces choices that principles let you avoid. What margin is acceptable? Which user groups count as significant? What is the impact threshold? Those are real decisions with real trade-offs, and they belong with accountable leaders, written down, rather than left implicit. A principle nobody has translated into a threshold is a principle nobody has agreed on.

Many organisations run their responsible AI review just before release. The feature is built, the launch date is set, marketing is ready, and a review group is asked to approve it. Under that arrangement the review can only do one of two things: wave it through, or become the reason the launch slipped. Teams quickly learn to see the review as an obstacle, and reviewers quickly learn that blocking is politically expensive.

The review that actually reduces harm happens at design time, when changing direction is cheap. Before much is built, the team writes down who the system affects, what decisions it influences, what the worst plausible failure looks like, and who bears the cost of that failure. They identify the groups most likely to be poorly served and plan how to measure performance for them. They decide which actions need a human and which can be automated.

This takes an hour or two for most features, and it changes what gets built. Design choices that would be impossible to reverse at launch, like which data to train or ground on, how much autonomy to grant, and whether to show confidence to the user, become simple to adjust. The launch-week review then becomes a check that the plan was followed, which is a much easier conversation.

The single most common way AI systems harm people quietly is through averages. A system can be ninety-five percent accurate overall and seventy percent accurate for a particular group, and the overall number will look excellent on every dashboard.

This happens for unremarkable reasons. The group is smaller in the data. Their language, documents, or circumstances differ from the majority. The test set was assembled from whatever was easy to collect. Nobody intended a gap, and nobody looked for one.

The practice is straightforward: decide in advance which segments matter for this system, measure quality separately for each of them, and set a threshold for the acceptable gap. Segments might be demographic, but often the most important ones are operational. New customers versus established ones. Non-native speakers. Unusual but legitimate cases. Customers in smaller markets. Documents in less common formats.

Then report the worst segment alongside the average, every time. Leaders get used to asking about the average. The responsible question is always "and who does it work worst for, and how badly?" If the team cannot answer, the system has not been evaluated responsibly, regardless of how good the headline number is.

Responsible AI fails most often not on knowledge but on authority. The concern is raised. Everyone agrees it is a concern. The release goes ahead anyway, because nobody had the clear right to stop it and the cost of delay was visible while the cost of harm was hypothetical.

Every AI system above a modest risk level needs a named person with the authority to block or pause it, and that authority has to be real. It should be written down, known to the delivery team, and backed by senior leadership when it is used. The person does not need to be a specialist ethicist. In most organisations it works best as part of an existing accountable role: the product owner for lower-risk systems, a risk or compliance lead for higher-risk ones.

Equally important, there must be a way for anyone on the team to raise a concern without career risk. Engineers and domain experts are often the first to notice that a system behaves badly for a particular group or in a particular situation. If raising that concern feels like being difficult, they will stop raising it, and you will learn about the problem from a customer, a journalist, or a regulator.

A practical signal of health: concerns are raised regularly, most are resolved without drama, and occasionally a release is held. A programme where nothing is ever raised is not a programme with no problems. It is a programme nobody trusts.

Two practices do more for user trust than almost anything else, and both are cheap to build.

The first is disclosure at the point of use. People should know when they are interacting with an AI system, when a decision about them was shaped by one, and roughly what it was based on. This does not mean a wall of legal text. It means a clear, plain statement in the interface, and where it matters, an indication of the sources or factors behind an answer.

The second is a route to contest. When an AI system makes or influences a decision that matters to someone, they need a way to say "this is wrong" and reach a human who can review it. That route needs to be visible, reasonably quick, and capable of actually changing the outcome. It also needs to feed back into the system. Every contested decision is evidence about where the system fails, and a well-run team reads those cases the way it reads production incidents.

Organisations sometimes resist both practices out of fear that they will draw attention to the system's limits. In practice the reverse happens. Users who can see what a system is doing and can challenge it when it is wrong trust it more, and they use it more.

The last shift is the one most programmes miss entirely. Responsible AI is usually treated as something you do before launch. But AI systems change after launch, sometimes without anyone touching them. User behaviour shifts. The data shifts. Upstream models are updated. A system that was fair and safe in its first month can drift into something else by its twelfth.

So the practices have to continue. Segment-level quality is monitored in production, not just measured once. Contested decisions are reviewed regularly for patterns. The design-time harm review is revisited when the system's scope, autonomy, or user base changes. Thresholds are rechecked against reality.

This has a cost, and that cost should be planned and budgeted rather than absorbed as a surprise. A useful rule of thumb is to treat responsible AI work as part of the system's ongoing operating cost, alongside hosting and support, rather than as a one-off compliance project.

The organisations that will earn lasting trust from customers, employees, and regulators are not the ones with the best-written principles. They are the ones that can show, for any AI system they run, how each principle was tested, who had the authority to stop it, how it performs for its worst-served users, and what happened the last time something went wrong. Principles describe what you value. Practices are the only proof that you meant it.

I write a field-note series on shipping AI that actually works in production. The original of this piece, and the rest, live on my LinkedIn. Originally published on LinkedIn.
