Hire for Judgment, Not Prompts: How to Build an AI Team That Outlasts the Hype

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 26 • Originally published at linkedin.com

Every leadership team I talk to has the same line in its plan: "hire AI talent." It sits next to the budget for compute and the list of use cases, and it is usually the vaguest line on the page. Nobody can quite say who they are hiring, what that person will do in their first ninety days, or how anyone will know whether the hire worked.

So companies default to what they can see. They search for the newest job titles, screen for the newest vocabulary, and pay a premium for people who can talk fluently about the latest techniques. Twelve months later many of those teams have produced impressive demos and very little that runs in production, and the leader is asking why the expensive team did not deliver.

The answer is almost never that the people were not smart. It is that the company hired for the wrong scarce skill. What follows is how I think about building an AI team that still delivers after the hype cycle moves on.

The titles change every year. Prompt engineer, AI engineer, applied scientist, agent developer, AI product manager. The pattern underneath them does not change at all, because it is the same pattern every technology wave has produced.

When a new capability arrives, companies over-hire people who are fluent in the capability and under-hire people who know how to turn a capability into a dependable product. In the early web years it was people who could build a homepage versus people who could run a transaction system. In the early cloud years it was people who could spin up infrastructure versus people who could operate it at a sane cost. Fluency with the new thing is visible and easy to interview for. The ability to make it reliable, measurable, and safe is quieter and much harder to find.

AI makes this sharper than any wave before it, because the gap between a demo and a product is wider. A capable model can produce a convincing prototype in an afternoon. That prototype is perhaps a fifth of the work. The other four fifths are evaluation, data, integration, failure handling, cost control, and operation over time. A team built entirely from people who love the first fifth will keep producing first fifths.

Tool fluency has a short half-life. The specific techniques that were essential eighteen months ago are already partly automated or obsolete, and the ones that are essential today will follow. If your hiring bar is "knows the current toolkit," you are hiring for a skill that decays faster than your onboarding cycle.

What does not decay is judgment. Specifically, three kinds of it.

The first is judgment about whether an answer is good. AI systems produce output that looks right far more often than it is right. The people you need can look at a result and say, with reasons, whether it is correct, whether it is good enough for this use, and how they would prove it.

The second is judgment about where AI belongs. A surprising amount of value comes from people who can say "this part should be a rule, not a model" or "this step needs a human, not an agent." People who reach for AI everywhere create cost and risk you did not need.

The third is judgment about trade-offs. Speed against quality, cost against accuracy, autonomy against control. These are product and engineering decisions, and they require people who can hold several constraints at once and choose deliberately.

None of those skills is specific to AI. All of them are what separates a working AI product from an expensive experiment.

If I could make only one early hire on an AI team, it would not be the person who builds the system. It would be the person who can tell whether the system works.

This feels backwards to most leaders, because building is what produces visible progress. But a team that cannot measure quality has no way to know whether any change is an improvement. They tune prompts by feel, ship by enthusiasm, and discover problems from customers. Every week of building without evaluation is a week of work you cannot trust.

The evaluator role goes by different names, and sometimes it is a hat worn by a strong engineer rather than a separate job. What matters is the work: defining what correct means for each use case, building a representative test set, running it on every change, and reporting quality in numbers the business understands. The person doing this needs rigour, scepticism, and enough domain sense to know which errors matter.

Once that capability exists, everything else on the team gets faster. Builders can try ideas and get an answer in minutes rather than arguments in meetings. Leaders can approve releases on evidence. The evaluator is the hire that makes every other hire productive.

There is a persistent belief that AI work needs a different kind of person from ordinary software work. For research, perhaps. For building products, mostly not.

The engineers who succeed at shipping AI into production are, overwhelmingly, strong software engineers who have added AI to their toolkit. They write tests. They think about failure modes. They design for retries, timeouts, and partial failures. They care about logs, versioning, and the ability to roll back. They know that the model call is one component in a system, and that the system is what the user experiences.

The engineers who struggle are often the reverse: deeply fluent with models but uncomfortable with the plumbing that makes a product dependable. They can make the model do remarkable things in a notebook and cannot make it do ordinary things reliably at three in the morning.

So when you hire, weight the fundamentals heavily. A strong engineer can learn the current AI techniques in weeks, because those techniques are documented, discussed everywhere, and changing anyway. A person fluent in the techniques but weak on engineering discipline takes years to build the habits that keep production systems alive, if they ever do.

The most common structural mistake I see is treating domain experts as reviewers. The AI team builds something, then hands it to the people who understand the business (the claims handlers, the underwriters, the clinicians, the lawyers) for a sign-off at the end.

That arrangement guarantees rework. The domain expert finds problems the team could not have seen, the team goes back to rebuild, and everyone concludes that the experts are slowing things down.

The experts are not slowing things down. They are the only people who know what a correct answer looks like. In AI work, that knowledge is not a final check. It is the specification. It defines the test set, the edge cases, the acceptable error rate, and the difference between a harmless mistake and a dangerous one.

Put domain experts on the team from the first week, with real time allocated and real ownership of quality. Pay attention to the ones who are curious about the technology and sceptical of its output in equal measure. They are often your most valuable people, and they rarely appear in a search for "AI talent."

AI interviews have drifted towards vocabulary tests. Candidates are asked to explain techniques, list architectures, and discuss the latest models. The candidates who prepare best for these conversations are not necessarily the ones who will do the job best.

I prefer interviews that look like the work. Give a candidate a real, messy, anonymised task: a set of model outputs and a description of what the business needs, and ask them to decide which outputs are acceptable and why. Ask them how they would build a test for this use case and what they would do if quality dropped by ten points overnight. Show them a working prototype and ask what would worry them about putting it in front of customers.

These questions reveal judgment directly. Strong candidates ask about the users, the cost of errors, and how quality will be measured. Weaker candidates go straight to which model or technique they would use. Both kinds can sound impressive in a conventional interview. Only one kind will help you ship.

It also helps to ask every candidate about a time an AI system they built was wrong in production, and what they did. People who have run real systems have real answers. People who have only built demos tend to describe a demo.

The external market for experienced AI talent is expensive, competitive, and full of people with eighteen months of experience and a title that suggests five years. Some external hiring is necessary, especially for a few senior people who have taken AI products through a full lifecycle. But the most durable AI capability I have seen was mostly grown, not bought.

Your existing engineers already understand your systems, your data, and your constraints. Your existing domain experts already know what correct looks like. Giving them structured time, real projects, and a few experienced people to learn from often produces a stronger team than a hiring spree, at a fraction of the cost and with far less turnover.

This also changes the culture of adoption. When AI capability is built by people the organisation already trusts, the rest of the organisation trusts the output more. When it arrives as a separate, highly paid group with its own vocabulary, it is easy for everyone else to treat it as someone else's project.

A practical shape that works: hire two or three senior people who have shipped and operated AI in production, make evaluation and engineering discipline their first priority, pair them with your strongest existing engineers and domain experts, and measure the team on production outcomes rather than prototypes. Expand from there as the work proves itself.

The companies that will still have strong AI teams in five years are not the ones that won the bidding war for this year's titles. They are the ones that hired for judgment, built the capability to measure their own work, and grew people who understand both the technology and the business. That team does not go out of date when the tools change. It simply picks up the new tools and keeps shipping.

I write a field-note series on shipping AI that actually works in production. The original of this piece, and the rest, live on my LinkedIn. Originally published on LinkedIn.

Hopper: Gestionnaire principal(e) du marketing de performance B2B// Senior B2B Performance Marketing Manager

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

À propos du posteHTS (Hopper Technology Solutions) est la division B2B de Hopper et propulse des solutions d'IA pour le voyage auprès de grandes compagnies aériennes, d'hôtels et d'institutions financières partout dans le monde. L'équipe Marketing et Communications recherche un(e) gestionnaire principal(e) du marketing de performance B2B pour diriger la croissance marketing de ses activités B2B sur les canaux numériques. L'équipe est restreinte et expérimentée, les responsabilités sont vastes, et on s'attend à ce que la personne retenue assume à la fois la stratégie et l'exécution.

Piloter la stratégie de marketing numérique B2B de HTS en établissant les priorités de canaux, les budgets et les objectifs liés au pipeline et aux revenus

Planifier, lancer et optimiser des campagnes payantes sur LinkedIn, Google Ads et d'autres canaux numériques, de la définition des audiences et de la création jusqu'à la production de rapports

Concevoir et mener de bout en bout des programmes de génération de leads, en collaboration avec les Ventes pour définir la qualité des leads et l'améliorer continuellement

Assumer la responsabilité de LinkedIn payant comme canal B2B central : gérer le contenu sponsorisé (Sponsored Content), les formulaires de génération de leads (Lead Gen Forms) et le ciblage d'audiences de type ABM pour rejoindre les décideurs de haut niveau des compagnies aériennes, des hôtels et des institutions financières, et tester continuellement les créatifs et les formats pour améliorer la qualité des leads

Concevoir du contenu pour les campagnes numériques, y compris du contenu pour les médias sociaux, des annonces de recherche et plus encore

Réaliser des tests A/B sur le contenu et les formats publicitaires afin d'optimiser la génération de leads et la conversion

Tester et déployer à grande échelle de nouveaux canaux et de nouvelles tactiques, et savoir quand abandonner ce qui ne fonctionne pas

Suivre, analyser et rendre compte de la performance, notamment le coût par lead, les taux de conversion et la contribution au pipeline, et transformer les données en recommandations claires pour l'équipe

Collaborer avec les équipes Produit, Ventes, Affaires juridiques et Communications afin d'assurer la cohérence des messages et l'alignement des campagnes sur les priorités de l'entreprise

Privilégier la qualité plutôt que la quantité en optimisant les campagnes pour les leads qualifiés par les Ventes (SQL) plutôt que pour le volume brut, et en travaillant avec les Ventes pour affiner continuellement le ciblage, les messages et les critères de leads afin que les efforts marketing se traduisent en véritable pipeline

Travailler en étroite collaboration avec l'équipe Marketing et Communications tout en jouissant d'un haut degré d'autonomie

Rendre compte de la performance numérique à la haute direction en traduisant les indicateurs de canal, comme le coût par lead, les taux de conversion et la contribution au pipeline, en constats et recommandations clairs qui orientent les investissements et la stratégie marketing

Collaborer avec les Ventes, la direction et l'équipe Marketing et Communications pour établir la stratégie et le processus de suivi des leads marketing tout au long du pipeline de vente

Fournir des analyses complètes sur l'influence des campagnes marketing sur la progression du pipeline et sur les revenus

Possibilité de soutenir, au fil du temps, la croissance auprès des partenaires existants au moyen de campagnes d'expansion et de lancements de produits conjoints avec les équipes partenaires et les Communications

Plus de 5 ans d'expérience éprouvée dans la gestion de programmes de marketing numérique B2B, avec des exemples clairs de campagnes menées de leur conception jusqu'à l'obtention de leads acceptés et jugés qualifiés par les Ventes

Expertise concrète de LinkedIn Ads et de Google Ads, avec une excellente maîtrise du ciblage, des enchères, des tests de créatifs et de l'optimisation

Maîtrise de HubSpot, de Salesforce et de GA4, avec la capacité de gérer le flux de leads, l'attribution et la production de rapports de performance sur l'ensemble de l'entonnoir

Aisance à travailler aux deux niveaux : vous savez définir la stratégie et aussi concevoir, lancer, gérer, optimiser et mesurer vous-même les campagnes

Solides compétences analytiques et aisance avec les outils de reporting, les CRM et les plateformes d'automatisation du marketing

Esprit d'initiative, avec la capacité d'établir vos priorités, de gérer votre charge de travail et de livrer sans supervision étroite

Excellentes habiletés de communication écrite et orale, et capacité de collaborer entre les équipes et avec des parties prenantes de haut niveau

The opt-in, AI-assisted checks: vision, SEO, brand voice, and semantic matching

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Part 6 of 6 in CatalogSentry's launch series.

Part 5 closed out the deterministic checks. This post covers the 7 checks that are off by default, each calling this Actor's own operator-hosted AI backend — no API key or endpoint for you to configure, just enable them in checks. Six use AI-assisted detection directly; the seventh, semantic_cannibalization, is deterministic by default with an optional AI-assisted pass layered on top. Six of the seven examples below are real findings from the 995-product Flipkart run; one (translation_consistency) is constructed, for a reason explained in its own section.

Worth repeating from part 2: these checks involve model judgment, not a fact lookup. The evidence is grounded in something real (the image, the text), but the check is asserting "a model looked at this and concluded X," not "this is objectively true." Every finding below says so explicitly in its own explanation field.

Looks at the product image, lists concrete attributes actually visible in it, and flags whichever ones aren't mentioned anywhere in the title or description.

Suggests keywords grounded in the image or existing text that the listing doesn't currently use — a discoverability gap, not an invented claim about the product.

Needs brandStyleBrief set — a short description of your expected product photo style (e.g. "single item, centered, white background"). No brief, no findings; this check never guesses what your brand standard is. From the real run (brief: single centered product shot):

Needs categoryVoiceBriefs — a map of category name to a tone/positioning brief. Only categories with a brief set get checked; everything else is skipped, no cost.

Covered briefly in part 4 — embeds title+description and flags pairs above a similarity threshold, catching duplicates that exact-match checks can't see because nothing about the text literally matches.

A similarity of 1.000 here isn't a rounding fluke — it means the two listings' text is effectively identical to the embedding model, even though they're filed as separate SKUs with no shared GTIN.

The one check in this list that's deterministic by default: a word-overlap (Jaccard) pass over every pair of product descriptions, catching two differently titled listings whose body copy is near-identical — they end up competing for the same search intent instead of each serving a clear purpose. An optional AI embedding pass can be layered on top to also catch paraphrased (not just overlapping) descriptions; it's the one check whose cost scales with the square of catalog size, so it has its own budget control (maxEstimatedCostUsd) rather than the simpler per-product limit the vision checks use.

Two completely different product names — a shampoo combo and a "Euro Style Combo Set" — sharing two-thirds of their description's vocabulary. Neither duplicate_detection (no shared GTIN, no matching title) nor a human skimming the title column would catch this.

This check needs targetLanguages set — it wasn't configured for the 995-product Flipkart run, so the check didn't fire, not because it found nothing but because it was never asked to run at all (no candidates, same as any check with an unmet precondition). So unlike every other finding in this series, what follows is a constructed example of the check's deterministic core, run through the actual extraction function this repo already uses for title_description_contradiction — not the full check (which also needs a live AI call to generate the translation itself):

That's the actual mismatch logic: the same regex-based number extraction title_description_contradiction uses, applied to an AI-generated translation instead of a second feed field. If an AI translation of "256GB" becomes "128GB" in another language, this is how CatalogSentry would catch that specific, narrow failure mode — a number that must stay identical regardless of language, but didn't. It does not check whether the rest of the translation reads naturally; that's out of scope by design (see the module's own docstring in the repo).

15 checks, 8 deterministic and on by default, 7 opt-in — every one of them demonstrated with either real output from a real feed, or an explicit, labeled exception. If you've read all six parts: this is the complete, current state of what CatalogSentry checks, with nothing described that isn't actually shipped.

Want to see all 15 run end-to-end on a real feed? Try the interactive demo, or run it directly on Apify Store — it's live today.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Is withdrawal rate modeling too simplistic to be useful to most people?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you were to attempt to forecast your spending in retirement by year, you'll almost certainly have many years with big, non-yearly expenses. If you buy that assertion and if this describes you, how is the notion of a "safe" withdrawal rate useful to you? If you have enough saved that a safe withdrawal rate would support even your lumpy-expense years, then that does seem like a potentially useful gauge. But if you don't and your modeling shows some years with a required withdrawal rate well below whatever you believe to be "safe" but others well above that, are you still using the notion of a SWR? If so, how?
https://tpawplanner.com/ and https://levelspend.github.io/ help with this. You review the plan annually. You can build in/plan for lumpy expenses, etc. It's not a static withdrawal strategy but a dynamic/variable withdrawal strategy as things change. The only constant is change.

On Saturday, Cyber said it detected unexpected minting of CYBER tokens on Solana, followed by a transfer of tokens out of its bridge’s Ethereum lockbox. Cyber, formerly CyberConnect, operates an Ethereum layer-2 network designed for social applications.

The project said it was investigating the incident and advised users to avoid using the bridge or depositing CYBER to exchanges while the investigation continues.

The draft also seeks to lift South Korea’s 2017 ban on domestic initial coin offerings.

The 20% ownership rule is set to affect a deal that Naver struck for Upbit owner Dunamu. The deal gave Naver full control, but under the new rules shares above the 20% cap would lose their vote.

South Korean fintech companies could gain influence in bank-led stablecoin groups by signing up partners that help people spend won-denominated tokens, Tiger Research said in its Tuesday report.

“Those relationships are likely to matter more to the business than the volume of stablecoins issued,” the Asia-focused research company said.

On Friday, the government officially published its Virtual Asset Services Legislation (Amendment) Bill 2026, proposing that firms carrying out those activities would need to obtain a license from, or register with, the Securities and Futures Commission (SFC). The bill is set for its first reading in the Legislative Council on Dec. 2.

YouTube Data API v3: API key, quota and pricing

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 26 • Originally published at handlesapi.com

You get a YouTube API key by creating a Google Cloud project, enabling YouTube Data API v3 in it and generating a key on the Credentials page. The steps below follow Google's getting started guide as of November 2026.

Treat the key as a server-side secret. Anyone who holds it spends your project's quota. This is Google's own request for a channel's statistics by handle:

Every Google Cloud project starts with a YouTube Data API quota of 10,000 units a day, and the counter resets at midnight Pacific time (quota cost table). Each method spends a fixed number of units per call.

Source: Google, determine quota cost and the search.list reference, as of November 2026.

Many guides still say a search costs 100 units. That changed. Since 1 June 2026, search.list costs 1 unit but lives in a separate bucket limited to 100 calls a day, according to the method's revision history. For creator discovery or any search-driven tool, that cap binds first.

A worked budget, as an illustration. Refreshing 5,000 channels once a day with channels.list uses 5,000 units, half the default quota, and leaves 5,000 for video reads. A discovery job that runs 150 searches a day is over the search cap by itself, whatever is left of the 10,000 units.

To raise the limit you apply for it. Google starts with a compliance review through the Audit and Quota Extension Form, so plan for a review step before launch if you expect volume.

Google does not list a per-call price for the YouTube Data API; the cost is the quota. There is no meter to pay down, but once the daily units are spent, further calls are refused until the counter resets.

That makes the real price a mix of engineering time and review time: a Cloud project to maintain, keys to rotate, quota to budget across jobs, and an audit if you outgrow the default. For one app reading a few thousand channels a day that is usually fine. For a pipeline that sweeps tens of thousands of channels, the unit budget and the search cap shape the architecture.

Handles API meters in credits instead of quota units. A catalog read costs 1 credit and a live read costs 2 credits, and a failed call is not charged. The Free trial is 100 credits once, with no card. Details are in credits and limits and on the pricing page.

One more date to note: since 16 November 2026, co-views count toward public view counts, per the YouTube Data API revision history. If you store a view series yourself, mark that date so a step in the data is not read as organic growth.

Handles API reads public YouTube channel data from its own catalog of about 97K channels, over one REST key. The table compares it with the official API on what matters when you are choosing between them. It is an independent service, not affiliated with Google or YouTube.

Official column from Google's YouTube Data API documentation, as of November 2026. Credit and plan figures from Handles API plans.

This is a real catalog response for the same channel as the official request above, captured from the live API. Note the fields: counts, hidden_subs, language, country and category.

Real response from the live API, captured Nov 26, 2026. 10 more record fields trimmed for space. Long strings and arrays are shortened.

For a first batch of 1,000 catalog reads, plan costs work out like this.

Plan price divided by included credits. A catalog read costs 1 credit, a live read 2 credits. The Free trial is 100 credits once, no card.

Rate limits work per minute rather than per day. The Free plan allows 10 requests a minute, and paid plans run from 120 to 1,200 a minute. Pagination on list endpoints uses a cursor you follow until it is null, described in pagination.

Use the official YouTube Data API whenever you need more than public channel numbers. It is the right tool for:

Know the limits of a catalog before you rely on it. Records are as fresh as the last crawl, and each carries a last_seen timestamp so you can check. Private data, writes and channels that are not in the catalog are out of reach, and the Free trial of 100 credits is a good way to test coverage on your own list of channels first.

Use a data API when the job is reading public stats for many channels, ranking them or tracking them over time, and you would rather budget credits than quota units. Nothing stops you from using both: the official API for writes and owner data, Handles API for read-heavy stats.

The same question comes up on other platforms. See the Instagram Graph API comparison and the TikTok Research API comparison. For the wider picture of collecting public social data, including the rules, read social media scraping: methods and limits.

Create a project in the Google Cloud console, enable YouTube Data API v3 from the API Library, then open the Credentials page and choose Create credentials and API key. Send the key as the key parameter on requests for public data. Methods that act for a signed-in user need OAuth 2.0 instead.

The default quota is 10,000 units per day for each project, reset at midnight Pacific time. Reading a channel with channels.list costs 1 unit and so does videos.list. Since June 2026, search.list has its own bucket: 1 unit per call, capped at 100 calls a day.

Google does not list a per-call price for the YouTube Data API. The cost is the quota: once the daily units are used, further calls are refused until the reset. A higher limit is not bought, it is requested through a compliance audit using the Audit and Quota Extension Form.

As of November 2026, one search.list call costs 1 unit, not the 100 units older guides quote. The catch is that searches count against a separate Search Queries bucket that allows 100 calls per day, so search-heavy tools hit that cap long before the 10,000 unit quota.

Yes, with a data API that serves public channel records from its own catalog. Handles API needs only its own key and a credit balance, with no quota units to manage. It is read-only and covers about 97K channels, so uploads, comments and OAuth data still need the official API.

Originally published at handlesapi.com/compare/youtube-data-api.

Remote or local: choosing between Sandscale's two MCP servers

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 26 • Originally published at sandscale.dev

Sandscale has two MCP servers, and the docs describe both: a remote server at https://mcp.sandscale.dev/mcp, and sandscale mcp, a local server that comes with the CLI. They talk to the same sandboxes and the same audit log, but they are different programs with different tool lists. This post lays them side by side so you can pick one, or decide to run both.

Sources: the Connect Claude or ChatGPT guide, which carries the remote server's tool table, and the MCP tools reference and sandscale mcp CLI page for the local one. Both tool lists are generated from the servers themselves, so they are the place to check if this post goes out of date.

Sandboxes. Both servers can list, start, run commands in, and stop sandboxes.

So only the remote server can pause, resume and snapshot over MCP. If your agent's workflow is "install dependencies once, snapshot, start each task from the snapshot", the remote server is the one with the tools for it. The snapshots guide covers what a snapshot keeps.

The local sandbox_start takes template, ttl and network inputs, and its description says the network is none unless asked. sandbox_exec takes the command as an array of strings, plus optional workdir and timeout_seconds. The remote server's sandbox_run returns the exit code, standard output and standard error.

Files. Remote only: sandbox_upload_file (text in content, bytes in content_base64) and sandbox_download_file (text back as text, other bytes as base64). With the local server, an agent moves data with commands, or you move files with the CLI yourself.

Workspaces. Both. Remote: workspace_list, workspace_start, workspace_stop. Local: workspaces_list, workspace_show, workspace_start, workspace_stop. A workspace is a persistent cloud computer; stopping it keeps its disk.

Storage and Vault. Remote only: storage_list_buckets and storage_describe_bucket for your S3-compatible buckets, and vault_list_secrets, which lists secret names in a Vault KV mount. Values are never returned by any Sandscale MCP tool.

Build hosts and lab hosts. Local only, and read-only: build_pools_list, build_leases_list, build_lease_show, build_lab_hosts_list. If an agent needs to see which build machines or lab devices you have leased, this is the server.

Both servers keep secrets away from the model. The remote server lists Vault names but never values. The local server's reference is broader: signing in, the vault and deleting anything are not offered as tools at all.

The remote server's docs also spell out what it cannot do: act as anyone else, go unrecorded, or run without limits. Each app can make up to 120 calls a minute and each person up to 60. Every remote call is in the organization's audit log with the tool, region, outcome and app, and never the arguments.

A coding agent on your laptop (Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, VS Code, Windsurf). Either works. Choose the remote server if you want file upload and download, pause, resume and snapshots as tools, or if you do not want to install anything. Choose the local server if you already use the CLI, want build host and lab host visibility, or prefer the server whose reference says it offers no tools for signing in, the vault or deleting anything.

A hosted assistant (Claude or ChatGPT on the web). Only the remote server, added as a custom connector. There is no local process for a hosted assistant to start.

Shared setups. The remote entry contains no secret, so a project config file that points at it (.mcp.json for Claude Code, .cursor/mcp.json for Cursor, .codex/config.toml for Codex) can be committed. Each person still signs in as themselves. The local entry is just sandscale with the argument mcp, and depends on each person's CLI sign-in.

Both at once. Nothing stops you adding both under different names, for example sandscale and sandscale-local. The cost is a longer tool list for the model to choose from, and two tools with similar names (sandbox_run and sandbox_exec) that do roughly the same thing. If you do it, use your client's allow and deny settings to keep only the tools you want from each.

Whichever you pick, the calls end up in the same place. The remote guide says every tool call goes through the same API, permissions, plan limits and audit log as everything else, and refusals come back with the API's own error code. The local reference says each tool runs the matching sandscale command with your own sign-in, so every check and refusal is the command's. In both cases your role, your plan and your organization's policy decide what happens, not the server you came through.

Setup guides for each client: Claude Code, Cursor and Codex.

Prova e gol da leader, Cambiaso ci prova: le tante vite di un esterno di 26 anni

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Promessa al Genoa, certezza al Bologna, favolosa sorpresa nella prima parte di stagione con la Juve, poi insostituibile, eclettico, fischiato, deludente, nazionale senza alternative, unica certezza per una fascia senza padroni in bianconero, ma mai incisivo, mai continuo, mai davvero al livello dei grandi difensori della storia della Juventus. Sono solo alcune delle fasi della seppur breve, e allo stesso tempo lunghissima, carriera di Andrea Cambiaso.

Che anche stasera non stava brillando prima del gol ricuci-partita, in una gara che alla Juventus stava sfuggendo dalle mani senza che nemmeno se ne accorgesse. Il sinistro a girare dell'esterno italiano ha avuto l'effetto di un triplo caffè sulla Vecchia Signora, svegliata dal torpore dalla giocata dell'esterno e dalla prova da leader del collega Gatti, un altro a caccia di spazio e certezze.

Che sia la volta buona per una nuova fase della carriera di Cambiaso, che a 26 anni va a caccia di un ruolo da protagonista che possa trasformarlo non solo in un punto di riferimento per la squadra attuale, ma soprattutto in un difensore che possa onorare la grande storia bianconera in termini di interpreti del reparto arretrato. E i nomi si sprecano.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Anyone having experience with ARVA [Annually Recalculated Virtual Annuity]?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

[I don't see best third as a special case of TPAW.
What I meant is that you can get the same results with TPAW if you plug in the right parameters.
TPAW, VPW, ABW, the best third are all riffs on amortization for the withdrawal of the variable portion of the portfolio. The best third is the only one that actively builds in a TIPS floor, when TPAW or VPW allow it and can deal with it but don't make it part of the strategy. That's what I like about it (and something I practiced before I was aware of any of those tools)

When it comes to what AA and discount rate to use, grab your crystal ball and choose your poison.
Understood. Best third is the closest to what we do. We built our own income floors with tipsladder.com. I built an SS TIPS bridge that matches what my SS will be in 5.5 years or so and DW is already on SS. We also include dividends from our taxable stock holdings as part of our daily spending, but not otherwise selling stock for that. Not everybody can put together a detailed plan on their own so having best third is a good thing as an integrated tool.

In terms of discount rate, for stock I use a simple 1/CAPE. If I had bond funds as risky assets, then I'd use the current nominal yield and geometrically subtract expected inflation or I'd use a TIPS fund and just use the real yield directly. Either way you slice it, future bond fund returns are more deterministic than future stock funds returns are, and the net result is there is less "noise" in the withdrawals due to the bond portion of a portfolio when amorting than there is from the stock portion. I'd also probably also consider bond usage whose durations were compatible with my investment horizon at any given time. If you have a long duration stock fund, for example, but only 5 years remaining in your plan then the future return predictions will be really bad vs. realized returns. I believe Ben and team are also working on adding something like this to TPAW.

cheers.

Statistics: Posted by dcabler — Thu Nov 26, 2026 4:36 pm

Avengers: Doomsday TV Spot Confirms RDJ's Doctor Doom Has A Scarred Face

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Marvel Comics fans know Doctor Victor von Doom is scarcely ever seen without his iron mask — a mask that hides his supposedly horribly scarred face. But with Marvel Studios choosing former Tony Stark actor Robert Downey Jr. to play Doom in the upcoming "Avengers" movies "Doomsday" and "Secret Wars," there's been an expectation that Doom's mask coming off will be a jaw dropping moment.

Now, Chris Evans has claimed that Doom wears his mask for the entirety of "Doomsday." But that could only mean he'll keep the mask on until "Secret Wars." Even then, there's the chance that Doom is so disfigured that his face isn't even recognizable as RDJ. A new TV spot for "Doomsday" does suggest Doom has his scarred face, while also teasing we're eventually going to see exactly what's under his mask ... but not yet.

In this TV spot, Magneto (Ian McKellen) tries to telekinetically rip off Doom's mask — it is made of metal, after all. But Doom does not restrict himself to the laws of science. There's evidently a magical seal on the mask (note there appear to be runic designs across Doom's costume), because glowing green tendrils extend out from Doom's hood and grab the mask, slamming it back onto Doom's face. A brief glimpse of Doom's face, visible through the mask's eyeholes, shows hints of pale macerated skin and scar tissue.

In the "Fantastic Four" comics, it was originally said that Doom suffered his scars during an experiment in college. A machine he built to contact the "nether world" (later revealed to be an attempt to reach his mother's damned soul) exploded in his face, leaving him scarred.

Now, Doom's co-creator Jack Kirby intended that Doom actually only had a thin facial scar — but Doom's ego can't stand even that small imperfection. Later "Fantastic Four" writer/artist John Byrne melded the two ideas. The machine explosion only slightly damaged Doom's face, but then Victor — convinced his face was already ruined — put his freshly forged mask on before it cooled, and that is why he has a horribly scarred face.

The greatest source of Doom's torment is completely self-inflicted. The exemplary 1989 "Doctor Strange and Doctor Doom: Triumph and Torment" goes with Byrne's interpretation. When Doom seeks out the wisdom of the wizard named the Aged Genghis, the sorcerer alludes to Doom's "minor disfigurement." We then see Doom's (barely scarred) face as Victor lashes out at the insult.

It's unclear what origin "Doomsday" will go with, if any of them. Marketing suggests the Marvel Cinematic Universe's Doom will be driven by a lost wife and child, not his mother. What is clear from this TV spot is that Marvel fans can expect to see Doom and Magneto — the two greatest villains of Marvel Comics — clash onscreen. Doom's wizardry here refutes the obvious conclusion that Magneto wins easily because he controls metal and Doom is concealed inside metal armor.

"He is pain, he is extinction," Magneto declares, setting up Doom's famous third person double entendre: "I am Doom!" Ian McKellen's Magneto was the first villain of the Marvel movie boom that kicked off with 2000's "X-Men." Bringing him back to face (and presumably fall to) Doom is some classic carnival barker marketing that would make Stan Lee proud.

"Avengers: Doomsday" is set for theatrical release on December 18, 2026.

→ Baca artikel asli

Michigan State-Rutgers scouting report: Banged-up Spartans close season

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Looking ahead to Michigan State's final game of the 2026 season:

Matchup: Michigan State (3-8, 1-7 Big Ten) vs. Rutgers (3-8, 2-6).

Where: SHI Stadium, Piscataway, New Jersey.

Out: LT Conner Moore, LB Brady Pretzlaff, QB Alessio Milivojevic, S Devin Vaught, LB Jordan Hall, RB Marvis Parrish, CB Tyran Chappell, TE Jayden Savoury, RB Cam Edwards, FB Jay Coyne, OL Tristan Comer, TE Joe Caudill. Questionable: DT Ben Roberts, DT Eli Coenen, QB Cam Fancher, WR Rodney Bullard Jr., S Michael Richard, TE Brennan Parachek.

Out: QB Sean Ashenfelder, DB Kaj Sanders, WR KJ Duff, DB Zahmir Dawud, DB Chris Jones, OL Kwabena Asamoah. Questionable: RB Antwan Raymond, LB Abram Wright, QB AJ Surace, DB Sage Clawges, LB Joey Kopec. Probable: QB Dylan Lonergan, WR Dyzier Carter.

When MSU has the ball: Alessio Milivojevic will miss his third game in the Spartans' final five to end the season after starting the final four last year and making eight starts as a third-year sophomore. Backup Cam Fancher again is listed as questionable after being a game-time decision but sitting out last week's 45-7 loss to Oregon. If Fancher is unable to play again, Kayd Coffman could make his third start of his freshman year and second in a row. The Spartans got running back Jaziun Patterson back against the Ducks, and he teamed with Brandon Tullis to fight through their individual injuries but struggled in the ground game with left tackle Conner Moore out and multiple moving parts up front. Luka Vincic started at right tackle, with Nick Sharpe sliding over to the left side.

Coach Pat Fitzgerald called Greg Schiano's Rutgers team a mirror image of the Spartans this season, and the offense-vs-defense matchup shows it. MSU ranks 130th nationally in scoring offense (17.7 points) while the Scarlet Knights are 123rd in points allowed (31.5), but they are coming off an impressive 25-24 win at Penn State last week in which they survived giving up 387 yards (161 rushing, 233 passing). Rutgers ranks near the bottom of the Football Bowl Subdivision in total defense (411.2 yards, 119th) and rushing defense (188.0 yards, 119th) while giving up 223.2 passing yards (89th) with a putrid 45.2% third-down conversion rate (124th) against them. Something has to give, as MSU's offense has been equally as troublesome – 134th out of 138 FBS teams in total offense (288.7 yards), 129th in rushing (106.9 yards) and 115th in passing (181.8 yards).

When Rutgers has the ball: The middle of the Spartans' defense will be thin again as they play without their top two linebackers again in Jordan Hall and Brady Pretzlaff, safety Devin Vaught and nickelback Michael Richard. Eli Coenen and Ben Roberts also are listed as questionable, but young defensive tackles Derrick Simmons, Mikeshun Beeler and Carlos Hazelwood have shown promise and production since Roberts' injury at UCLA on Oct. 24. With Pretzlaff and Hall out, and with Dion Crawford and Cam Stodghill each getting nicked up against Oregon, MSU's linebacker corps has been significantly shorthanded.

The Spartans did get cornerbacks Tre Bell and Charles Brantley back, which allowed NiJhay Burt to get more reps at nickelback and Aveon Grose to slide to safety to offset the injuries to Vaught and Richard. With all the injuries, Joe Rossi has managed to piece together a unit that ranks 45th in total defense (344.3 yards) and is 57th against the run (137.8 yards) and 61st against the pass (206.5 yards) but has struggled to get off the field on third downs (38.8%, 118th). MSU's 25.3 points allowed ranks 74th, but Rutgers' offense has struggled to score all season and ranks 100th at 23.4 points.

The Scarlet Knights are averaging just 334.0 yards a game (119th), including 134.1 yards on the ground (101st) and 199.9 through the air (98th) while also struggling to convert on third down (35%, 106th). This will be the final game of the season for both teams, with MSU failing to earn a bowl berth for the fifth straight year and sixth time in the past seven.

Quarterback questions: Like the Spartans, the Scarlet Knights also are dealing with injury issues at quarterback. Schiano has used three different starters this season, with Sean Ashenfelder (left ankle) out, AJ Surace (ribs) questionable and Dylan Lonergan (undisclosed) probable for Saturday. Surace, despite having three broken ribs, started his third game of the season and threw the winning 2-point pass in last week's win at Penn State, Rutgers' first over the Nittany Lions in 38 years. He finished 24 of 39 for 178 yards with a touchdown. Lonergan hasn't played since Oct. 24 against Northwestern after throwing for 291 yards and three TDs in a win over Maryland a week earlier.

RELATED: Injuries at QB give Michigan State freshman Kayd Coffman shot to learn

Big loss: Whether Surace or Lonergan gets the nod, they won't have Rutgers' top passing target available. Wide receiver KJ Duff had season-ending ankle surgery earlier this month but remains fourth in the Big Ten at 96.8 yards per game (774 yards on just 45 receptions) and is still tied for fourth with nine touchdown catches. Since Duff got hurt against Northwestern on Oct. 24, the Scarlet Knights are averaging just 120.8 passing yards and have just three touchdown catches in those five games.

Ground gains: Though he is questionable with an undisclosed injury, running back Antwaun Raymond leads the Big Ten this season with 911 rushing yards (82.8 per game) on 185 attempts (4.9 per carry). He also has eight rushing touchdowns along with 20 catches for 202 yards receiving with another score. The 5-foot-11, 205-pound junior from Montreal ran 23 times for 101 yards and two touchdowns at PSU last week as well as a critical lateral near the goal line as time expired for the final touchdown to set up Surace's winning 2-point pass.

Not much history: Fitzgerald and Schiano have met just one time before as head coaches, when Fitzgerald's host Northwestern team defeated Rutgers, 21-7, in 2021 for the Wildcats' only Big Ten win that season in Schiano's second season of his second stint with the Scarlet Knights. Fitzgerald won his only game at SHI Stadium in 2018, 18-15, en route to a Big Ten West division title with Northwestern. The Spartans are 8-3 all-time against Rutgers and 5-2 in Piscataway, but they have lost the last two meetings – a 27-24 fourth-quarter collapse in 2023 and a jarring 41-14 blowout loss at home to close the 2024 season.

Punting predicament: MSU senior punter Rhys Dakin enters the final weekend of the regular season averaging 48.0 yards on 38 attempts, which would lead the Big Ten by 0.1 yards over USC's Lachlan Carrigan and 0.9 in front of Washington's Hunter Green. Dakin also would rank fifth in the FBS this season. However, his 3.45 punts per game average falls just below the 3.6 per game threshold for the NCAA's stat rankings, and neither he nor Carrigan have enough to qualify.

MSU 20, Rutgers 14: The Spartans have not quit on the season despite their injuries, and neither have the Scarlet Knights – even after a shocking opening-week at home against Massachusetts. The difference comes down to the defense of Rossi, who was an assistant at Rutgers from 2012-15, and Fitzgerald's motivation to finish strong heading into the offseason.

Contact Chris Solari: csolari@freepress.com. Follow him @chrissolari.

This article originally appeared on Detroit Free Press: Michigan State-Rutgers scouting report: Banged-up Spartans close season

Sunderland ‘disappointed’ after banned Jagiellonia Bialystok fans enter Stadium of Light

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Police and stewards were called to handle a disturbance within a home area of the ground during the early minutes of the game

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Sunderland have expressed their disappointment after a number of Jagiellonia Bialystok fans gained access to home sections at the Stadium of Light during Thursday night’s 1-1 draw.

UEFA had issued an away supporter ban against Jagiellonia for Thursday’s match on Wearside.

However, police and stewards were called to handle a disturbance within a home area of the ground during the early minutes of the game.

A club statement said: “Sunderland AFC is disappointed that a number of Jagiellonia Bialystok supporters were present in home areas of the Stadium of Light during tonight’s UEFA Europa League fixture.

“Jagiellonia Bialystok were subject to a UEFA away supporter ban for tonight’s Matchday 5 fixture, following a disciplinary sanction imposed by European football’s governing body.

“In the build-up to the match, the club had put measures in place in line with this sanction and maintained regular communication with our supporters, Jagiellonia Bialystok and UEFA. Unfortunately, Jagiellonia Bialystok failed to engage and cooperate on this matter.

“We are disappointed that these measures and communications were disregarded by some visiting supporters and will review the circumstances surrounding their entry to the stadium.”

Sunderland struck early in the second half through Timur Tutierov’s header, his second goal in the competition, but Kamil Jozwiak levelled only minutes later after capitalising on a mistake at the back.

A point means the Wearsiders stay in the knockout phase play-off places after being held to their second draw from five games in the competition.

Sunderland remain without the injured Brian Brobbey and Granit Xhaka, and Regis Le Bris made six changes from their goalless Premier League draw with Aston Villa on Saturday.

Jagiellonia showed an early flash of danger when Nik Prelec weaved into the box and played a low cross into the area, but Roefs held the resulting clearance.

Sunderland caused problems when Angulo found Enzo Le Fee and the midfielder had his strike deflected narrowly behind.

Slawomir Abramowicz was forced to push away Fofana’s menacing shot before the half evolved into a scrappy affair.

A sudden burst of energy from the hosts saw Trai Hume chip over and Le Fee was at the heart of another solid attack.

The Frenchman went around Abramowicz before playing a low ball across the box towards Angulo lingering in front of an open goal, but his strike from close range was blocked by Yuki Kobayashi.

An enforced change six minutes before the break saw Luke O’Nien introduced for Omar Alderete and moments later Roefs was called into action to palm away Szmyt’s free-kick just outside the box.

On the stroke of half-time, Jagiellonia struggled to clear their lines and Hume blasted wide of the upright.

Sunderland brought on Jules Ahoka and Thomas Meunier at half-time and just two minutes after the restart they went ahead.

A good team move stemming from Chris Rigg’s break down the middle resulted in the ball reaching Le Fee and his cross from the right was deflected by Taras Romanczuk in to Tutierov to nod home at the back post.

However, their lead was short-lived when Jozwiak pounced on a tame attempted back pass from Reinildo Mandava and rolled past Roefs in the 52nd minute.

Another sloppy defensive error allowed Jozwiak to again latch on to the ball, but Roefs tipped the resulting shot behind.

Sunderland’s frustrations continued when Fofana’s half-volley was stopped and Angulo blasted a free-kick straight into the wall before Rigg’s low shot was comfortably scooped up by Abramowicz.

Both sides went close in the 90th minute when Reinildo’s header was saved by Abramowicz, and Jeremy Agbonifo broke for the visitors but was denied by Roefs as the game fizzled into a draw.

With help from Matt Damon and Zack Snyder, Ford announces return to Le Mans

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The giant in American automaking announced on Thursday, Nov. 26 that it will return to the iconic endurance race held annually in France with its new Ford GT41 Hypercar.

It's been 60 years since Ford became the first American automaker to win at Le Mans, as three Fords — piloted by Ken Miles, Bruce McLaren and Ronnie Bucknum — finished first, second and third at the race. The 1966 24 Hours of Le Mans and the events leading up to it were dramatized in a 2019 Academy Award-winning film Ford v Ferrari, directed by James Mangold and starring Matt Damon and Christian Bale. The film was nominated for Best Picture and won Oscars for Best Film Editing and Best Sound Editing.

To help Ford with the announcement, Damon provided his voice to a narration over a commercial directed by Zack Snyder, known for films such as 300 and Justice League. The commercial aired on Thanksgiving during the NFL game between the Chicago Bears and Ford's hometown Detroit Lions.

The nearly two-minute long commercial was titled "Go Like Hell" — a phrase that Carroll Shelby wrote on a board to show to driver Ken Miles, telling him to push the car past its limits as they raced at the 24 Hours of Daytona ahead of Le Mans.

"The amazing part about getting a chance to work on this 'Go Like Hell' spot for Ford Racing was the chance to mix the nostalgia of the '66 GT40 with this modern Hypercar and its incredible engineering, as well as this narrative that follows the transporting of the car across America," Snyder said in a statement. "It's during this journey that we get to see what drives Ford to win. And as a filmmaker, those elements inspired me to make some cool images."

Damon added in a statement: "Telling the story of Le Mans '66 was one of the highlights of my career, so getting to lend my voice to the next chapter felt very full circle. Revisiting this one with Ford and Zack, as they take on the world's toughest racing all over again, was a real privilege."

The commercial revisits a familiar filming location, as the hangar used to load the GT41's shipping container is the same location featured in Ford v Ferrari as the site of the Ford GT40 race workshop.

Ford won four consecutive Le Mans from 1966 to 1969. Their second win in 1967 was by a team that featured AJ Foyt and Dan Gurney, two of the greatest American racecar drivers of all time. Foyt won four Indy 500s and a Daytona 500, while Gurney had four Formula 1 Grand Prix wins and five career NASCAR Grand National Series wins.

Six decades later, Ford remains the only American automaker to win at Le Mans. Toyota has won six of the last nine 24 Hours of Le Mans, with Ferrari winning the other three.

"Having completed six European tests, culminating in our first 30-hour endurance test at Aragon, we're now preparing for the next phase of development on American soil," said Dan Sayers, the Ford Racing Hypercar program manager. "We're under no illusions about the scale of the challenge ahead. Our focus remains on developing the car's performance and reliability, while continuing to evolve as a team ahead of our competitive debut."

Ford will also enter the GT41 Hypercar into the FIA World Endurance Championship, which begins in Qatar in March. That race is where the GT41 Hypercar will officially make its debut. Ford Racing said in a statement that it "continues to expand its global racing efforts" to 41 different racing series across six continents.

"We're going back to Le Mans next summer in June to take on Ferrari and the best of the world to win Le Mans outright. It's an all-Ford effort. We're not having another company manage the athletes and the effort — this is all on Ford with some key partnerships," Ford CEO Jim Farley told FOX Business. "We really think of ourselves as America's Race Team."

The Gt41 Hypercar takes inspiration from the classic GT40 and stands 41 inches tall. The engine was designed at Ford's home in Dearborn, Michigan, and the naturally aspirated Coyote-based V8 is powered by an advanced hybrid propulsion system. Ford also said it brought its "discipline learned through our partnership with Red Bull Racing" in Formula 1 to accelerate the process.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: With help from Matt Damon and Zack Snyder, Ford announces return to Le Mans

Asha Bhosle and S. Janaki Remembered by Singer K.S. Chithra and Suhasini Maniratnam at IFFI’s Lata Mangeshkar Talk

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Asha Bhosle and S. Janaki, two of Indian cinema’s most celebrated playback singers, were remembered at the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa by singer K.S. Chithra and actor-filmmaker Suhasini Maniratnam.

Bhosle, a two-time Grammy nominee whose name was referenced in Cornershop’s 1997 hit “Brimful of Asha,” died in April at 92. Janaki, known as the Nightingale of South India, died in July at 88. Chithra and Suhasini shared their memories of the two at the annual “Lata Mangeshkar Talk: The Voice That Echoes Forever,” named for Bhosle’s elder sister, the singer Lata Mangeshkar. Related Stories Elon Musk Criticizes Jeremy Culhane’s ‘SNL’ Impression: ‘They Need to Try Harder’ ‘SNL’ Sets Timothée Chalamet and Robert De Niro as Final Hosts of 2026

Chithra, who has recorded more than 25,000 songs in several Indian languages over a career spanning nearly five decades, spoke about the singers who shaped her musical sensibilities, among them Mangeshkar, P. Susheela and Janaki. She imitated Janaki as a child, while acknowledging that the veteran singer’s distinctive style was impossible to reproduce. Mangeshkar’s devotional songs, she said, had particularly impressed her with their emotional depth.

Suhasini recalled an episode from the making of “Iruvar” (1997), directed by her husband Mani Ratnam. Composer A.R. Rahman, an Oscar winner for “Slumdog Millionaire,” had invited Bhosle to record a song that needed to evoke the style of Tamil playback singer L.R. Eswari, but was unsure how to explain the reference to her. He asked Suhasini to help. She demonstrated Eswari’s style, and Bhosle went on to deliver “Venilla Venilla.”

Chithra spoke about Bhosle’s versatility and her ability to move between musical forms, including ghazals, and about her desire to establish her own musical identity rather than be compared with her sister. At their first meeting, Chithra said, Bhosle asked her why she was singing in such a high register.

Janaki, Suhasini said, could inhabit a character through her voice, singing like a child or a teenage boy. Chithra recalled that Janaki was a devoted fan of Mangeshkar. She would play Mangeshkar’s songs on the radio, turn down the volume during the vocal portions and record herself singing along – “like a karaoke on her own,” as Chithra described it. Offstage, Chithra said, Janaki would feed her by hand when she visited her home.

The session also touched on the demands of recording and on recognizing one’s vocal limitations with age.

Chithra sang snippets throughout, including songs from her own repertoire and numbers popularized by Janaki and Bhosle. In a closing quiz, film clips were played without their background scores and Chithra was asked to identify each song by singing it. She identified all five.

The session ended with Chithra singing “Paadariyen” from “Sindhu Bhairavi” (1985), which won her the first of her six National Film Awards, as Suhasini, who starred in the film, joined her, lip-syncing.

Atalanta, Sarri: "Tante palle gol ma fragili dietro. Scamacca-Krstovic? Nessun dualismo"

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Al termine della folle rimonta dell'Atalanta sul campo del Borac Banja Luka, vinta per 3-2 negli ultimi sette minuti grazie alle reti di Krstovic all'86', Kolasinac al 90' e Zappacosta al 93' in una sfida valevole per la quarta giornata della fase a campionato di Conference League, il tecnico nerazzurro Maurizio Sarri è intervenuto ai microfoni di Sky Sport per analizzare la partita.

Una serata che siete riusciti a raddrizzare soltanto nel finale, probabilmente i sette minuti più pazzi da quando allena l'Atalanta. Le chiedo una lettura di una partita per certi versi senza senso, perché dopo un buon inizio siete andati sotto di due gol e poi nel secondo tempo siete riusciti a risalire.

"La verità è che abbiamo fatto una buona partita e abbiamo creato tantissime palle gol. Purtroppo in questo momento non abbiamo solidità difensiva e questa fragilità ci fa diventare tutte le partite più complicate. Però, se guardi i numeri della gara, le occasioni nostre ci sono: negli ultimi sette minuti abbiamo fatto tre gol su quattro palle abbastanza facili, mentre nei dieci minuti precedenti avevamo sbagliato tre reti da un metro. Oggi abbiamo combinato errori in fase risolutiva e continuiamo ad avere qualche problema difensivo. Detto questo, è chiaro che la partita diventa complicata, ma la squadra ha dimostrato grande carattere come negli ultimi tempi. Bisogna lavorare per risolvere il problema che ci rende le sfide così difficili. Voglio dire un grazie anche ai nostri tifosi che hanno fatto tanti chilometri per essere qui e che per novantasette minuti ci hanno dato veramente una mano: per fortuna stasera li abbiamo ripagati."

Come si trova la solidità difensiva oltre che con il recupero di Kossounou e Hien, che di fatto non ha mai avuto?

"Bisogna lavorare, ricercare e ricavarsi dei tempi di lavoro che, fra gli infortuni e il calendario, per noi sono difficilissimi da trovare. Però bisogna riuscire a farlo sia a livello di reparto difensivo, sia a livello di squadra."

Soprattutto nel primo tempo mi sono sembrate esserci un po' di ripartenze legate a letture preventive non eccezionali non soltanto dei difensori, ma anche dei centrocampisti, mentre nel secondo tempo sono stati un pochino più attenti.

"Sì, io ho parlato di linea difensiva ma anche di fase difensiva. Le distribuzioni sotto la linea della palla e qualche lettura della linea non sono state sicuramente di buon livello. Questo si sta ripetendo da diverse partite e ci crea delle difficoltà: poi le gare per noi si complicano e non possiamo sperare di risolverle sempre così."

Mi sposto sull'attacco, perché è inevitabile ragionare su un eventuale dualismo tra Scamacca e Krstovic, visto che il tabellino racconta l'importanza dell'ingresso di quest'ultimo. Le chiedo se Krstovic, al di là delle qualità dei singoli, sia forse più adatto in questo momento per il tipo di squadra che sta costruendo a Bergamo.

"Non so, io prima dell'inizio della stagione avrei risposto che Scamacca è un giocatore adattissimo a me. È un centravanti fisico ma allo stesso tempo molto bravo tecnicamente e abile a venire a giocare con la squadra. La differenza in questo momento è che uno è in pieno ottimismo e in piena condizione psicofisica, mentre l'altro viene da un momento molto difficile e piano piano sta cercando di ricostruirsi. L'ho sempre detto: secondo me il dualismo non esiste, con me partono alla pari e poi è il campo a decidere chi gioca di più. In questo momento sta facendo meglio Krstovic e gioca di più Krstovic, però potrebbe essere anche solo un momento stagionale."

Dawn of War 4's campaigns differ based on your choices: 'which character you choose and what decisions you make with them lead down different experiences of an overlapping story'

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dawn of War 4 writer John French lays out how it works.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The original Dawn of War's Winter Assault expansion added campaigns that let you play two factions at once, with different final missions based on which one you triumphed with. Dawn of War 2's campaign was even more player-led, with distress calls that had different outcomes based on what you prioritized and, in the Chaos Rising expansion, a morality mechanic with Corruption and Redemption points. It even chose a different character to be a traitor based on who gained the most Corruption by the story's end.

I asked John French, Dawn of War 4's writer and a Warhammer novelist, whether Dawn of War 4 would follow this noble tradition by having player decisions alter the plot. "The player's decisions very much change the perspectives that they'll experience the game from," he replied. "So, depending on which heroes from each faction they choose, they'll have a very different experience of the game, in that they'll see different parts of the story and sometimes different views on the same events."

Based on what we've seen of the Ork and Adeptus Mechanicus campaigns, that means partway through you'll be presented with a choice between two Commanders who have opposed goals and the one you pick will lead to missions with different objectives. Ork Beastboss Guzcutta is trying to unite his clan, the Snakebites, while his rival warboss, Gorgutz, is an off-world interloper trying to rebuild his damaged invasion force.

"With characters like Gorgutz and Guzcutta," French explained, "it can be as simple as one of them wants to do it the traditional, brutal ork way, and one of them wants to do it in a brutal but cunning way, so you have missions that focus on getting lots of shiny kit in one, while the other tries to get as many orks to follow them as possible.

"In a more complex narrative, like the Adeptus Mechanicus campaign, Potentia-Delta-9's main drive is the quest for knowledge, and she is on a very different path from Skitarii Marshal Sek-Ix-23, who is very much about enforcing the orthodoxy. The story for both overlaps, but which character you choose and what decisions you make with them lead down different experiences of an overlapping story."

Writing an RTS isn't all about plotting out the campaign missions. The Dawn of War games are legendary for their unit barks, like the saber-rattling Lord Commissar who shouts, from the top of an armored Chimera transport, "Drive me closer, I want to hit them with my sword!" Getting across so much personality in a short space is a real skill.

"You can do an awful lot with just a couple of words like 'More dakka!'," French said, "that tells you a huge amount about the orks on its own. Even with factions like the necrons, the way that the lower ranks respond to the higher ones with just 'Obedience,' one word, tells how their faction works."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Part of the appeal of Warhammer is the berserk variety of its armies, each with its own highly thematic identity. You walk into a shop and see the stoic space marines alongside spiky Chaos daemons and units of Astra Militarum who look like they stepped out of a trench in World War I before being given laser rifles.

"Dawn of War 4 is deeply Warhammer in that way because it's got all of these different textures of factions and characters next to each other," French said. "You have orks, and they are absolutely fixated on smashing everything they can in the pursuit of trying to have an even bigger fight. Then you've got the Adeptus Mechanicus, who revere machines as holy and worship the machine as god with complete sincerity and total conviction. Those two things literally exist side by side, along with alien races who have made themselves immortal by creating metal bodies with hyper advanced technology. And all of these different types of character come into conflict on one planet cut off in a sea of night—that's very Warhammer."

Dawn of War 4 is due out on December 3 and will be available on Steam. Owners of the Commander Edition will get advance access and can play from November 30.

Jody's first computer was a Commodore 64, so he remembers having to use a code wheel to play Pool of Radiance. A former music journalist who interviewed everyone from Giorgio Moroder to Trent Reznor, Jody also co-hosted Australia's first radio show about videogames, Zed Games. He's written for Rock Paper Shotgun, The Big Issue, GamesRadar, Zam, Glixel, Five Out of Ten Magazine, and Playboy.com, whose cheques with the bunny logo made for fun conversations at the bank. Jody's first article for PC Gamer was about the audio of Alien Isolation, published in 2015. Jody edited PC Gamer Indie from 2017 to 2018, and he eventually lived up to his promise to play every Warhammer videogame.

Nvidia Is the Worst-Performing "Magnificent Seven" Stock This Year. Is It a Buy Before 2027?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Even with Jensen Huang back as CEO, Nvidia is drastically underperforming the S&P 500 and Nasdaq this year.

By Daniel Foelber – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) investors have been on a rather unenjoyable roller coaster in recent weeks. And every time the ride looks like it's nearing an end, there's another unexpected turn that unearths more questions than answers.

Founder and CEO for over 33 years, Jensen Huang, admitted on Nov. 8 to being unfaithful to his wife, then resigned on Nov. 12, only to be brought back as CEO less than two weeks later on Nov. 23.

That same day, President Trump was on a phone interview with CNBC and took credit for speaking with Nvidia's board and helping convince them to bring Huang back. Trump said, "I spoke to them. Let me just put it that way. And I convinced them. And I will tell you, it was a lot easier to convince them yesterday than it was a week and a half ago." Trump was referring to Nvidia's stock price and how it was easier to convince Nvidia to bring Huang back when the stock was cratering.

Nvidia has rallied substantially since Huang came back as CEO, but Huang and Nvidia still face an uphill climb of federal, state, and internal investigations linked to Nvidia misleading investors and potential national security violations regarding advanced Nvidia artificial intelligence (AI) chips leaking into China through backdoor channels.

The stock is still under $100 per share at the time of this writing and down 20% year to date -- making it the worst-performing "Magnificent Seven" stock in 2026. The Magnificent Seven consists of seven mega-cap tech-focused companies that include Nvidia, Apple (AAPL +0.43%), Microsoft (MSFT -1.25%), Alphabet (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%), Amazon (AMZN -3.12%), Meta Platforms (META +3.40%), and Tesla (TSLA -1.18%).

Here's the simplest reason to buy Nvidia, and an argument for why it's best to keep the semiconductor stock on a watch list for now.

Heading into this year, some investors were concerned that Nvidia would be unable to maintain its breakneck revenue growth rate and high gross margins. But hyperscalers continued to ramp up AI capital expenditures. And Nvidia has broadened its customer base to other enterprises, AI start-ups, AI labs, and neoclouds.

As a result, Nvidia's earnings growth began outpacing its stock price growth, compressing its valuation. But then the unthinkable happened.

Nvidia's stock price has plummeted despite exceptional results and guidance for 75% revenue growth in fiscal 2028. Nvidia reported third-quarter fiscal 2027 results on Nov. 17, with revenue more than doubling from the third quarter of fiscal 2026. In the nine months ended Oct. 25, 2026, Nvidia generated $183.3 billion in net income compared to $77.11 billion for the nine months ended Oct. 26, 2025.

Nvidia is on track to surpass Alphabet as the world's most profitable company. It now trades at just 15.4 times forward earnings -- making it the cheapest Magnificent Seven stock despite having the highest gross margins and the fastest revenue growth rate.

For context, the forward P/E of the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%) is 17.3 -- meaning Nvidia is now priced like a value stock.

Buying high-quality companies on sale is an excellent way to compound wealth over time. Nvidia doesn't have to deliver blowout results to impress investors. In fact, its growth rate could fall substantially, and it would still be a steal at current levels.

The issue isn't Nvidia's valuation -- it's investors' concerns about where the company could be headed over the next three to five years.

Jensen Huang's sudden resignation exposed Nvidia's extreme vulnerability to key-person risk, governance failures, and an incapable board of directors. Going forward, Nvidia will need to change its structure to be less dependent on Huang, which could make it more rigid and less innovative but also protect long-term investors.

In addition to Nvidia-specific issues, there's also the risk that AI spending could decline considerably due to higher interest rates, which have made borrowing more expensive. With democrats taking control of the Senate and the House, there may be higher regulatory hurdles to accelerating data center construction. And there's the ongoing risk that compute demand will taper off as AI models become more efficient.

Granted, Nvidia is incredibly well positioned to endure a slowdown because of its industry-leading position and rock solid balance sheet. But it could also lead to stagnating or declining earnings in the medium term, which would challenge Nvidia's valuation.

All told, Nvidia simply has too many unknowns right now. Until investors get more clarity on the findings of the investigations, the company's succession plan for Huang, and the extent of its governance overhaul, Nvidia remains a hold.

Heading into the new year, I'd rather buy high-quality Magnificent Seven stocks with a clear runway for future earnings growth like Alphabet, even if its valuation is more expensive than Nvidia's.

Xabi Alonso hits back at Jamie Carragher claim as ‘responsibility’ claimed for Chelsea defending

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Chelsea, featuring team news and expert analysis from Dom Smith

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Xabi Alonso says he takes the “whole responsibility” for Chelsea’s dismal defending but disagrees with his friend Jamie Carragher that Wesley Fofana and Levi Colwill will never be a good enough centre-back pairing for the Blues.

Fofana and Colwill were heavily criticised for their performances last Saturday as the west Londoners were thrashed 4-1 at home to Leeds in the Premier League.

The defeat exposed further weaknesses in Alonso’s defence and proved the worst possible response to their disappointing previous outing, the 1-0 defeat to Sunderland before the international break.

Chelsea head to the City Ground to face Nottingham Forest on Friday night eager to get back to winning ways.

Analysing the Leeds loss, Carragher said on Sky Sports: “Chelsea have got a massive problem at centre-back and if they don’t change it in January, they’re going to have a big problem in terms of where they feel they should be this season with the Champions League.”

Speaking about Fofana and Colwill, he added: “As a partnership, this will never work because they’re not physical enough and there’s no real leader there.”

Asked whether he had seen these comments from his former Liverpool team-mate, Alonso said he had not, before adding: “I must disagree. I disagree with Carra. For sure, you can have bad games. We were not good [against Leeds]. But I think I’ve seen them [Colwill and Fofana] play well, and I’ve seen them show good levels.

“This is the goal and the challenge – to be consistent in the performance and to be consistent in the basics and not to drop in certain games when we are facing a tough opponent.”

Alonso, speaking more broadly about Chelsea’s poor defending, added: “There are no excuses not to do better. We are in the process. Taking steps, taking a step forward but until it is totally in the DNA of the team it takes time.

“I take the whole responsibility of the team and how they perform. We need to show [improvement against Forest], not just talk. Talk is cheap. It’s about performing on the pitch.

“We want to be back on that more solid feeling that we had. This was hopefully one-[off], and we don’t want to repeat it.”
