Jesse Plemons on the ‘Honor’ of Playing Plutarch in ‘The Hunger Games’ After Philip Seymour Hoffman and Debating Director Francis Lawrence Over the Character’s Motivations

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” might be Haymitch Abernathy’s prequel movie, but Oscar nominee Jesse Plemons was the first person cast in the film.

Plemons plays Plutarch Heavensbee, a Capitol insider, who becomes a key figure in the rebellion that sparks in the latter half of Suzanne Collins’ original trilogy of dystopian novels. In the second book, “Catching Fire,” and its 2013 film adaptation, Plutarch is introduced as the Head Gamemaker, tasked with creating the brutal trials in the Hunger Games. (The late Philip Seymour Hoffman originated the role.) At the end of that story, Katniss Everdeen (Jennifer Lawrence) learns that Plutarch was really scheming from inside the establishment to help support the rebels. Related Stories James Talarico Crashes ‘SNL’ Cold Open: ‘I’m the Guy Who’s Been Texting You Every Day Asking for $5’ ‘SNL’ Weekend Update Brutally Roasts Elon Musk Over 'Epic’ Midterms Defeat

But “Sunrise on the Reaping” takes place 25 years before those events, so casting the younger Plutarch was key.

“I’m a huge fan of Jesse’s,” franchise director Francis Lawrence tells Variety. “Had never worked with him before, and had always wanted to, and thought he would be perfect.”

Plemons, of course, is one of the blockbuster movie’s most accomplished stars — one of the star-studded ensemble’s seven Oscar nominees: Jennifer Lawrence and Kieran Culkin are winners, with Glenn Close receiving an honorary Oscar earlier this month.

“We had a lot of fun casting all these people fans are familiar with for this movie. But with [Plutarch] specifically, there was a lot more development,” Lawrence adds, sharing that he and “Hunger Games” producer Nina Jacobson spent considerable time working with Collins on Plutarch’s arc.

“He’s an infiltrator,” Lawrence explains. “He’s the guy at the beginning of the formation of the resistance, and at the same time, has to get to the place where by ‘Catching Fire’ he’s at Snow’s side and so trusted. So, it was on us to figure out how to portray Plutarch in a way that is believable. And that was fun because we were adding scenes that weren’t in the book.”

Book readers might’ve noticed a new Plutarch scene in the “Sunrise on the Reaping” movie — where Plemons appears bruised and bloodied after being brutally interrogated by President Snow (Ralph Fiennes), who’s learned of a secret plot to sabotage the 50th Hunger Games.

“That was a keystone scene for us, and we went over and over and over different approaches of it,” Lawrence says. “How can we have him be active on these two distinct paths: one is, ‘How can I keep this resistance alive, but a secret?’ And ‘How can I seem utterly loyal and faithful to Snow, who is very, very smart?’ It’s become one of my favorite scenes in the movie.”

Lawrence kicked off conversations with Plemons by sending Collins’ unpublished manuscript. The actor cracked open the book, but because he was also working on “Digger,” he hadn’t made it to the final pages before the finished script came in.

“I read the script, and then finished the book, and was surprised by how engaging it was and how complex the character was,” Plemons tells Variety over the phone from his home in Texas. “And I was really blown away by the script and the book.”

Indeed, Plutarch is a meaty character. But he also had to consider that the role was originated by Hoffman, one of his favorite actors of all time, who’d also played his father in 2012’s “The Master.” As such, Plemons knew saying yes would come with plenty of questions from reporters about how it felt to continue the late Hoffman’s “legacy” after the actor’s unexpected death in 2014. Even after filming the part, Plemons hasn’t quite ironed out how to articulate his feelings on such a lofty question.

“That’s a tough one to answer,” Plemons replies, thoughtfully, when I ask how he feels about all of that. “I’m definitely honored to have the opportunity to play this character. I wish I had a better answer for you, but it’s complicated.”

Less complicated are his feelings about the “Hunger Games” franchise’s larger message, which was a key factor in his decision to take the part.

“I find it a big accomplishment when a film or a book that has such a wide appeal is able to tackle real-world themes and issues everyone feels and relates to, that connect to all of our lives,” Plemons says.

Read on as Plemons explains how he approached Plutarch’s prequel arc, including what he garnered from Hoffman’s performance, and why he and Lawrence debated over the complex character’s true motivations.

Many things. One, just the nature of doing a prequel like this. It was interesting knowing where the character ends up and how all these relationships progress, and then realizing there was actually more space to play and create ourselves. To fill in this very large blank over the course of 25 years. And there was something, just in the scope of the amount of time that he has been fighting and infiltrating the capital, there’s a sort of double agent aspect.

There’s a sort of philosopher side to him. There’s most definitely an importance on knowledge that he’s trying to instill in Haymitch. And at the same time, what I was most interested in was his very pragmatic sense of hope, which is not at all shrouded in any sort of rose-colored idealism. It’s very rooted in the reality of how difficult and almost impossible it’s going to be, and I was just really intrigued by that on a personal level.

The other interesting aspect is that he winds up being with the good guys, the resistance. But even still, in his relationship with Haymitch, I feel like I was constantly questioning — and I feel like the audience probably will too — just how expendable he feels Haymitch is for the greater good. All the while, he’s at least, to me, growing pretty fond of him as the story goes on. So there were just a lot of complexities that I really enjoyed.

That was the first sequence I shot, which was great. It’s obvious he’s incredibly intelligent. He’s able to sort of play this role. And I really loved that sequence with Glenn [Close]. I’m assuming they’ve known each other practically all of Plutarch’s life; she’s probably like a crazy aunt in some way. And it was really fun to play with maniuplating Drusilla without her knowing it.

It’s interesting. Obviously, his agenda is to prove himself to Snow and everyone in the Capitol. And at the same time, there must be something kind of alluring about pulling this [rebellion] off. It was really interesting, and Francis definitely allowed for some space, especially in the sequence where we’re filming Haymitch saying goodbye. We brought in a little more of the book there and a little more of Plutarch handling the camera operator, being a little more hands-on than was in the scene.

That scene, because it was a new addition that wasn’t in the book, was something that I could definitely see the need for — to see where Plutarch was in all of this. Aside from seeing in the library earlier on, it’s the only time you get to see Plutarch with Snow, and it was just an absolute thrill getting to work with Ralph.

It’s such a game of intellect between the two. Plutarch is trying to gauge how much Snow knows, whether he knows that he’s involved and who he knows is involved. At the same time, he’s trying to double down and act as if he’s truly working in the interest of the Capitol. We played around a lot with that scene.

I don’t think there’s like a black-or-white, or cut-and-dry, easy way of categorizing Plutarch or his motivations. The main debate Francis and I were in was whether or not his motivations were rooted in some sort of self-centeredness. A kind of wanting to be the hero, or just wanting to stick it to the Capitol and his family, that I’m sure, as a child, he had very conflicted feelings about.

I felt his intentions were pure. The fact that he would like to stick it to the Capitol, that’s most definitely part of it. He’s most definitely entitled and privileged. But I think everyone’s given a choice, and at some point, Plutarch decided that knowledge is of the utmost importance, and I think, morally, he is very much on the side of what is right and he sees the injustice. I believe his intentions are pure. I just don’t know why you would make it all that time if your motivations were about your own self-interest.

I had a lot of conversations with some of the other actors that were playing characters that were established in the other films, and we all had our own unique challenges and thoughts about how best to capture that essence, while at the same time taking some freedom in the fact that 24-25 years is a long time. The thing that jumped out at me in the film that he was in [Hoffman first appeared in 2013’s “Catching Fire”] was that they were still most definitely fully committed to the cause, but you could see the time. You could see the weariness of all those characters.

There were little things that I was conscious of and tried to connect to his performance, but for the most part, I felt like this is a very different Plutarch. He’s beginning his journey and is incredibly ambitious, and the whole energy of his pursuit just felt very different, and all the relationships, like his relationship with Snow, are just beginning. So, it wound up feeling like there was quite a bit of room.

Most definitely. I can’t imagine where her mind will go next — no one can. But, personally, selfishly, what I would love to see is Plutarch’s teenage years. I thought a lot about that while we were shooting, but I don’t think I could play that part.

This interview has been edited and condensed for length and clarity.

All 3 The Rings Of Power Seasons, Ranked Worst To Best

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You'd need to go back to 2003 and The Return of the King to find the last universally acclaimed Tolkien adaptation. Whether it be Peter Jackson's The Hobbit movies and The War of the Rohirrim on the big screen, or Prime Video's The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power on TV, many years have passed since Middle-earth experienced any degree of critical consensus.

The Rings of Power has proven especially divisive. The mere concept of Amazon bringing the Second Age into live-action triggered controversy by the bucket load, and opinions split further thanks to a series of significant deviations from canon: Sauron and Galadriel's romance, the 'orcs aren't so bad' angle, and Tom Bombadil: Defense Against the Dark Arts teacher.

As much as it's difficult to shower The Rings of Power with undiluted adulation, it's also impossible to deny there are elements that J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay's series does very well. This, combined with each season covering distinct periods of the Second Age's timeline, means that The Rings of Power's three seasons released so far vary considerably in quality.

The Rings of Power season 4 is confirmed and, if the showrunners have their way, a fifth and final season will follow sometime thereafter. Until then, The Rings of Power is following a very distinct trend in terms of overall quality.

The Rings of Power didn't exactly put its best foot forward at the beginning. It seems strange now, but the series initially thought the best way to adapt source material almost a century old was by following a 'mystery box' format. The Rings of Power's first season banked hard on questions like, "Who is the Stranger?" and "Who is Halbrand?" Answers to these plot points were handled like huge revelations, but in reality, the actor who looked like a young Gandalf turned out to be playing Gandalf, and the character behaving like Sauron turned out to be Sauron.

Looking back, season 1 kept its cards too close to its chest, and the whole affair ended up more akin to a Lord of the Rings imitator than a genuine Second Age TV series. The Stranger stood in for Gandalf, Adar was black metal's answer to Sauron, Nori and Poppy were your new Frodo and Sam, and Arondir covered the same bases as Legolas. Were it not for the folks in Lindon and Númenor, viewers would have been forgiven for asking whether The Rings of Power was actually a licensed Middle-earth production.

The focus on ambiguity and misdirection took the place of a compelling storyline. Not only does The Rings of Power's first season bear the least resemblance to Tolkien, it's the one season where little of note happens. The Harfoots aren't as interesting as The Rings of Power wants them to be (remember Lenny Henry was a main cast member?), Gil-galad is a shadow of himself, and Númenor is still enjoying some calm before the storm.

It's not all bad, of course. Mordor gets a genuinely fascinating origin story as the Southlands transforms into the volcanic realm The Lord of the Rings fans know and love, while Keeping Up with Khazad-dûm offers some much-needed light relief among all the wistful glances and overwrought foreshadowing.

With Sauron slipping into his Annatar guise, The Rings of Power finally found what it needed most: a canon cornerstone to build from. Wisely spending much time in Eregion, The Rings of Power's second chapter spanned the creation of the Rings of Power themselves, then ended on the Siege of Eregion, one of the Second Age's most memorable battles.

The improvement was palpable. The entire angle hinged on the chemistry between Charlie Vickers' Sauron and Charles Edwards' Celebrimbor, as the pair entered a marriage of blood, fire, and metal. The Rings of Power perfectly captured the nuances of that relationship: Celebrimbor's lofty ambition, Sauron's silky manipulations, and the authentic respect forged between them as they toiled. It also helped that, after an entire first season largely without rings, The Rings of Power finally lived up to its name in season 2, adapting a recognizable story from Tolkien's lore and being more or less faithful.

Tom Bombadil randomly popping up in Rhûn was odd. You'd almost call it fan service, but without the version of Tom fans would know from the books. Galadriel's pining over Sauron also started to grate very quickly, especially after Elrond stepped into the love triangle via a misjudged kiss. And the finale still dropped the 'Gandalf' reveal like the audience still hadn't realized Daniel Weyman wasn't just playing some random wizard.

Generally speaking, however, season 2 was where The Rings of Power started to justify its existence. There was a balance between hard-hitting battle scenes and intelligent character drama, and much less effort spent on trying to keep Tolkien readers on their toes with guessing games. Númenor was a particular highlight, as the tension between Míriel and her bearded cousin boiled over into some rather excellent political backstabbing.

One of the biggest compliments that can be paid to The Rings of Power is that it's still getting better three seasons in. After season 2 ended with the Siege of Eregion, it was left for The Rings of Power's third season to explore the War of Sauron and the Elves, while also handling the Faithful vs. King's Men civil war over in Númenor. The result was Amazon's best season of expensive Middle-earth content yet and, perhaps not coincidentally, the truest to the source material.

By some distance, Sauron shone brightest. Charlie Vickers spent the entire season attempting to construct the One Ring, and his performance exemplified the physical and emotional strain of crafting Arda's most powerful artifact this side of the Silmarils. What Tolkien described of the Ring's creation would fit onto an envelope, and The Rings of Power did admirably to stay within those lines, while also adding logical color between them. It made perfect sense that Sauron needed to know more about the Three before he could control them. And the One Ring's rhythmic method of construction went right back to the biblical opening of The Silmarillion.

The Númenor material continued the good work of season 2, depicting the Faithful as an underground rebellion, and Gil-galad finally turned into a character who vaguely resembles what Tolkien created. One can question whether renaming the Nazgûl was strictly necessary, but watching the likes of Khamûl, Theo, and the two fallen kings in the early stages of their corruption proved an absolute treat. To the east, Gandalf improved immeasurably once his arc dovetailed into the main happenings in Middle-earth.

The Rings of Power isn't quite there in terms of winning over skeptics just yet. Durin's 'wife slept with brother' storyline felt a little too soap opera-y, with very real fears of Disa shouting, "We were on a break!" at one point. The coronation of Thrain contained just a little too much Coronation Street for a Middle-earth series.

It would also be fair to argue that, for a season revolving entirely around two massive wars, The Rings of Power season 3 contained very little war. Opening battle aside, you'd never know conflict was even afoot. Indeed, The Rings of Power twice pulled back from battle scenes at the last minute: once when news of Míriel's wedding prevented the Faithful charging against the King's Men, and then again when Sauron's surrender avoided a clash between his forces and the Númenor-Elf alliance. Hopefully the battle budget is being saved for season 5.

Nevertheless, The Rings of Power sails toward its fourth season with the critical winds at its back, and the gradual improvement seen since the beginning offers reason for optimism that Sauron's trip to Númenor will not disappoint.

‘Stranger Things’ Star's ‘Hellboy’ Reboot Officially Leaves Netflix in 5 Days

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Mike Mignola's monster-hunting demon Hellboy has graced the big screen four times now. The most controversial take on the character can currently be found on Netflix, but you'd better act fast, or it'll be dragged to Hell. David Harbour donned the crimson prosthetics to portray the character, presaging his current success in the Marvel Cinematic Universe.

Mignola created Hellboy in 1994 as a creator-owned character; he has gone on to become one of the greatest horror comic successes of the modern era, even after Mignola concluded his story in Hellboy in Hell. The character first graced the screen in 2004 in Guillermo del Toro's Hellboy: Ron Perlman played the title character with his customary aplomb, while Doug Jones played his aquatic ally Abe Sapien, presaging his amphibious star turn in The Shape of Water, and Selma Blair starred as Hellboy's pyrokinetic ex Liz Sherman. The trio reprised their roles in 2008's Hellboy II: The Golden Army; a third film was mooted, but del Toro moved on and Perlman was unwilling to make the film without him, and so the franchise was rebooted.

2019's Hellboy stars Stranger Things' Harbour as the title character, a demon who's been raised in the bosom of the Bureau for Paranormal Research and Defense by Professor Trevor Bruttenholm (Ian McShane, Deadwood). He teams with BPRD were-jaguar Ben Daimio (Daniel Dae Kim, Lost) and medium Alice Monaghan (Sasha Lane, Twisters) in a race against time as the porcine fairy Gruagach (Stephen Graham, Adolescence) attempts to reassemble the dismembered body of the sorceress Vivienne Nimue (Milla Jovovich, Resident Evil). Can they stop her resurrection before she brings about the End Times? You've got until November 30, when Hellboy leaves Netflix, to find out.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Hellboy was not the success the studio was hoping for with critics, fans, or audiences. The next iteration of the franchise took shape five years later, with Hellboy: The Crooked Man. An adaptation of a miniseries from Mignola and late horror comics legend Richard Corben, The Crooked Man took a stripped-down approach to the material, with a low budget and a largely unknown cast: Jack Kesy (Deadpool 2) stars as Hellboy, who finds himself in the backwoods of Appalachia as he hunts a monstrous creature. It was released in theaters internationally and direct-to-VOD in the US in 2024.

Hellboy is leaving Netflix on November 30. Stay tuned to Collider for future updates.

Prediction: These 3 Consumer Stocks Will Beat the S&P 500 Over the Next 10 Years

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I think Ferrari, Hilton, and Amer Sports have the brands, the growth runways, and the discipline to outrun the S&P 500 over the next decade.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Beating the S&P 500 sounds simple until you remember how good the index already is, since it has compounded at about 9.9% a year on a total return basis going back to 1928. So a stock that beats the index over 10 years needs more than a good quarter, it needs a business that can keep growing over the long term.

When I look for that kind of staying power in the consumer world, I aim for three things: a brand that people pay up for, a long expansion runway, and a management team that protects both. Here are three consumer stocks that I think long-term investors should consider for the decade ahead.

Ferrari (RACE +2.96%) deliberately builds fewer cars than people want, and that scarcity is the heart of its economic moat, which is a durable advantage that keeps rivals from stealing its customers.

CEO Benedetto Vigna said in the third-quarter release that deliveries "reflect our clients' ever-deepening desire to make each Ferrari uniquely their own." Those personal touches add profit to every car without adding volume.

Demand isn't the problem. Vigna said on the call, "We continue to experience a healthy demand across all geographies, with an order book that enters 2028."

The risks are a premium valuation, at roughly 36 times earnings, and the shift to electric models like the Luce, which has to win over buyers who love engines.

Hilton (HLT +0.52%) mostly doesn't own its hotels; it collects fees by managing and licensing its brands to owners through a franchise model, which is an arrangement where a business pays to use another company's brand and systems. That lets it grow without spending heavily on buildings.

CEO Chris Nassetta said in the third-quarter release, "We achieved our highest quarter ever for organic openings." He also said on the call, "Globally, Hilton is still less than 6% of supply providing a long runway for continued growth."

Travel is cyclical, though. CFO Kevin Jacobs said, "RevPAR in China declined 5.4% in the quarter," referring to revenue per available room, which is a key hotel measure of how well rooms are filled and priced. Middle East conflict is weighing on that region, too, and the stock trades near 49 times earnings.

Amer Sports (AS +2.56%) owns Arc'teryx jackets, Salomon shoes, and Wilson tennis gear, and every segment grew by double digits in its latest quarter. CEO James Zheng said, "I am very confident in the future outlook for our company"

The U.S. opportunity is what excites me most. Arc'teryx CEO Stuart Haselden noted that the brand has about as many stores in the U.S. as in Canada, which he said "makes it very apparent the potential that we have to expand in the U.S." Management also said Europe's store traffic is a challenge, and higher freight costs are nibbling at margins.

None of these companies will beat the market every year, but I think long-term investors should consider owning all three and giving their growth stories the full decade they deserve. Great brands tend to compound quietly.

What ties these three together is pricing power, which is the ability to raise prices without losing customers, and I think that matters more over a decade than any single quarter's results.

Ferrari's buyers wait years for their cars, Hilton's owners keep signing up for its brands, and Arc'teryx shoppers happily pay premium prices for gear that lasts.

There will be rough stretches, whether it's a travel slowdown, a weak luxury market, or a cold winter for outdoor sales, but investors who hold through those dips give each business time to prove its case. In the end, patience is the edge most investors give up too easily.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 777%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 199% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Each of these mattress toppers softened up our hard beds, but there's a clear winner for the ultimate in cooling side sleeper comfort (and it has a big Black Friday price cut too)

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While Saatva, Tempur-Pedic, and DreamCloud are offering the best Black Friday mattress deals we've seen this year, all three brands are also behind some of 2026's best mattress toppers. But which premium mattress topper should side sleepers buy in the Black Friday sales?

We've tested the Saatva Graphite, Tempur-Adapt + Cooling and the DreamCloud mattress toppers and they each deliver the signature luxury of their respective brands' mattresses. This comparison guide will help you choose which topper is right for you if you're a side sleeper in need of extra comfort.

For the latest price cuts, also see our guide to the best Black Friday mattress deals live.

A comfy topper from a leading sleep brand, but its retention of excess heat lets it down. Though if feel cold in bed, this could be ideal.

The 3"-thick Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper is the most popular entry in Saatva's bed topper collection. Its body-cradling foam supports all sleep positions, including side sleeping, while its anchor straps keep the topper in place. However, despite the infusion of graphite, this memory foam topper can trap body heat.

Sink-in softness, contouring that hugs your curves to relieve pressure, and all-night cooling to keep you at the right temperature for sleep.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling is the most top-rated mattress topper we've ever tested, as it uses Tempur-Pedic's propriety Tempur-Material to provide softening comfort for side sleepers and hard beds. The addition of a cooling cover prevents heat build-up, but be aware that there's no official sleep trial.

A good cooling memory foam topper to soften the area under your shoulders, hips and knees, but the lack of anchor straps is frustrating.

The DreamCloud Softer Mattress Topper is made from a 2" slab of gel memory foam that our testers found to be cushioning along the shoulders and hips, making it ideal for side sleepers. Its breathable cover complete with "cooling yarns" means the DreamCloud Topper doesn't overheat. However, there's no anchor straps to prevent shifting.

All three mattress toppers are 3" thick and use memory foam to cushion firm beds. While each works well for side sleepers, their distinct features make them better suited for different types of side sleepers.

For instance, the Saatva Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper caters to all sleep styles, making it ideal for side sleepers who sometimes switch to back or front sleeping — or side sleepers who share a bed with someone who sleeps in a different position. In contrast, the DreamCloud and Tempur-Adapt have a plush feel best suited to side sleepers only.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling Mattress Topper has a cooling cover to prevent side sleepers from overheating (the Saatva Graphite, on the other hand, is prone to heat build-up). The DreamCloud Softer Mattress Topper also sleeps cooler than the Saatva Graphite, but doesn't have the washable cover with anchor straps. Still, it's the cheapest of the three, starting at $179 (the Saatva starts at $225; the Tempur-Adapt at $259).

We test mattress toppers in key areas, particularly in temperature regulation and comfort and support. We then give each area a score out of 5.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

When testing comfort and support, our testers sleep on each topper in the three main sleep styles (back, side, and stomach) to see how comfortable and supportive it is in each position. The Saatva Graphite performed the best in this area, scoring a near-perfect 4.5/5, because its medium-firm feel supports all sleep styles.

In comparison, the Tempur-Adapt's 3.5/5 score may seem low, but that's because it's too soft to support back and front sleepers. For side sleepers only, the Tempur-Adapt + Cooling provides incredible pressure relief. The DreamCloud also lost a point for its softness, but it's still a great topper for side sleepers.

As for temperature regulation (how well a topper maintains a comfortable temperature), the Tempur-Adapt's cooling cover upgrade scored the highest points. Meanwhile, the DreamCloud's breathable cover, while not cool-to-the-touch, prevented overheating. The Saatva Graphite scored the lowest, as our lead tester found it was prone to trapping heat.

Here's a close-up look at each mattress topper, exploring their respective designs and prices, plus who we do and don't recommend them to...

The Saatva Graphite Memory Foam Mattress Topper is a 3" slab of graphite-infused memory foam that's enveloped in a breathable, antimicrobial organic cotton cover (the same material used for the covers of the best Saatva mattresses).

Despite a naturally cool cover and a graphite-infusion, our testers found the Saatva Graphite's memory foam to trap heat. However, both Saatva customers and our testers agree that the memory foam's medium-firm feel adds plenty of comfort and support to mattresses.

The Saatva Graphite (or any other Saatva toppers, for that matter) are pretty much never included in the Saatva Black Friday sales, let alone the monthly Saatva sales. That means you'll always pick up a Saatva Graphite from $225 in a twin. Extras include a generous 180-night sleep trial and a so-so one-year warranty.

The 3" Temur-Adapt + Cooling Mattress Topper is actually an upgrade of the original Tempur-Adapt Mattress Topper. We found the original's use of pressure-relieving Tempur-Material was great for side sleeping, but was also prone to heat build-up.

That's why were so pleased when the + Cooling upgrade added a cooling cover, with out lead tester impressed with the difference it made. While not a great mattress topper for those who sleep on their back or front, it's a top pick for side sleepers who need to soften hard beds.

The Tempur-Adapt + Cooling Topper is the only one out of the three toppers to be included in the Black Friday mattress topper sales. This means you'll save up to 30% on all sizes in this month's Tempur-Pedic sales, with a twin now down to $259 (was $339). Disappointingly, there's no sleep trial, but you will get a generous 10-year warranty.

DreamCloud is known for its flagship DreamCloud Classic Hybrid, a luxury hybrid mattress at a medium-range price, so it's only fitting that it would offer a bed topper at a fraction of the cost of its mattresses, too.

The DreamCloud Softer Topper is one of two DreamCloud toppers; the other is called the DreamCloud Firmer Topper, which is designed to give a (you guessed it) a firmer feel. The DreamCloud Softer Topper is made from 2" of gel-infused memory foam, which our testers say provides a contouring feel for side sleepers.

The DreamCloud Softer Topper may not be on sale for Black Friday (no surprises there, it's never in the DreamCloud sales) but that doesn't stop it from being the cheapest option. A twin is priced at $179, and comes with a 100-night sleep trial, a one-year warranty, and free shipping and returns.

For side sleepers who are looking to soften a hard bed, we'd reccomend looking at the Temour-Adapt + Cooling or the DreamCloud Softer Topper. Both have a plusher feel than the Graphite and are great at regulating temperature, though those who need a bigger comfort boost should go for the thicker Tempur-Adapt.

Those looking for the most cost-effective, on the other hand, should pick the under-$200-for-a-queen DreamCloud. Meanwhile, the Saatva Graphite is best for side sleepers who prefer a firmer feel, combination sleepers, and couples with different sleep styles.

GTA 6's Map Sparks Family Guy Character Comparisons With Peter Griffin

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The absolutely massive map of Grand Theft Auto 6 is one that players have been theorizing about for years and have still only discovered a small portion of, despite players having already racked up 80,000 years' worth of playtime in the game on PlayStation 5 alone. While these numbers are certainly impressive, the more interesting part of Leonida is how many Easter eggs and hidden details are scattered throughout the map.

Although some of these references are more obvious, like callbacks to Miami's Dexter compared to others that could take years before being fully pieced together, one comparison to a character from one other than Family Guy made by a player online has completely changed how we see the map.

While you might need to squint to see it at first, after being compared side by side from X user gardeninqueen, GTA 6's map of Leonida state really does bear a striking resemblance to Peter Griffon from Family Guy, but more specifically his standard hands-at-the-side pose.

Although it's hard to imagine Rockstar spending over a decade working on a game just to make its entire map a Family Guy gag, the post has since gone viral with countless players backing up how similar they look and saying, "It's genuinely him" or "Oh my god I see it" when overlaying the image over top of Leonida.

The funniest part is that this isn't even the first time this has happened to Rockstar either, as players also said the same thing about Grand Theft Auto 5's map looking like Homer Simpson's head. While many players also questioned how either of these comparisons were noticed in the first place, it isn't too surprising given how rabid fans have been over map speculation since GTA 6 was revealed.

Although we'll likely never get an official confirmation if the unlikely similarity was intentional or not, the fact that this has happened twice now with two American animated sitcoms feels more than a little suspicious at this point.

Microsoft-owned developer platform Github stepped in it earlier today when it posted a message on X celebrating the success of AnyPS5, an open source project that aims to let users port PS5 executables to PC, and then linked that success directly to efforts to port Grand Theft Auto 6 to PC. The message was deleted shortly after it was posted, but not before it was widely noticed and called out for being a very bad idea.

"GTA 6 is finally here, sparking one of the biggest weeks in gaming and open source on Github," the message stated (via Kotaku).

"The repo AnyPS5 has passed 58K stars since its August launch, rewriting PS5 executables to run on Windows and Linux. Does changing where games run cross a legal line? Share your take. Happy GTA VImas!"

Lest there be any misunderstanding, a video that accompanied the post made the GTA 6 connection explicit: "The project rewrites a game's executable so Windows and Linux can run it directly. Performance seems to be the main issue for users wanting to run GTA 6, with one contributor telling the project's community to 'lock in and get your agents to help' if they need to."

And here's the accompanying video from @github's GTA6 tweet https://t.co/crU9cpOWcsNovember 26, 2026

The question of whether or not this sort of thing crosses a legal line has, predictably, sparked debate among those who think they have a clear understanding of the answer. What caught most by surprise, though, is that Github—which is owned by Microsoft—would put forth the question in the first place, implicitly suggesting that it might not. GTA 6 publisher Take-Two Interactive has been pretty aggressive with its efforts to keep recompiled versions of GTA 6 from landing on PC, and I can't imagine it would too thrilled with Github—which, I must emphasize, is owned by Microsoft—telling the world that it might actually be okay to go ahead and vibe-code it up.

That point wasn't missed by redditors, one of whom described it as Github "openly taunting Sony and Rockstar." Even the AnyPS5 subreddit seemed baffled: Redditor superkickstart asked, "Do they have AI running their socials? Lol"

There's also a little bit of irony in the fact that, until earlier this week, the leading effort to get GTA 6 running on PC was based on an Xbox build of the game posted by GTA 6 leaker Cyberleeks. Beyond that, there are also some very obvious questions about how Github (owned by Microsoft) would react to AI-powered ports of, say, other software:

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

The whole thing is weird, really, because it seems like such an obviously bad idea. The only explanation I can come up with is that a social media manager either didn't think about it, didn't understand it (which would be weird in itself for someone doing social media posts for Github), or just had a big ol' brain fart. Whatever the reason, I imagine someone got a good old-fashioned talking-to about it.

I've reached out to Github and Take-Two for comment and will update if I receive a reply.

Crystal Palace 2-1 Real Sociedad: Thomas Frank claims first win as Eagles complete Europa League comeback

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Crystal Palace delivered Thomas Frank a first victory after they came back to beat 10-man Real Sociedad 2-1 in the Europa League.

Dean Henderson was kept busy in the first half, making several stops to ensure it was all to play for after the break in south London, where Frank took charge of a home game for the first time.

Mamadou Sarr broke the deadlock in the 66th minute, but the contest turned on its head when Jon Martin was sent off with a second yellow card six minutes later.

Jean-Philippe Mateta fired in the penalty before second-half substitute Dwight McNeil tapped home from Jorgen Strand Larsen’s overhead kick.

Yeremy Pino had a chance to put the game away with a stoppage-time penalty but he was denied by Unai Marrero, and the hosts had to grind out a nervy finish.

The result moves the Eagles into a play-off position, after starting the evening just outside of it, in 25th.

Frank once again elected to employ a back four, as he did in his first game in charge, their 3-2 Premier League loss at Coventry.

The first chance came after a cagey first 10 minutes when the hosts gave the ball away and it fell to Benat Turrientes, who drew Henderson into a save from distance.

Henderson was then called back into action, twice denying the ever-threatening Goncalo Guedes, who was teed up by Mikel Oyarzabal’s through ball.

Palace’s best opportunity came on the stroke of half-time via a Richards throw-in, glanced on by Axel Disasi and nodded over by Mateta.

Crystal Palace handed WSL points deduction after illegal substitution

Crystal Palace sweating over Oscar Mingueza injury as defender sent for scans

Crystal Palace stay bottom as Coventry conspire to spoil Frank debut

Winter escapes and activities to end the year in style

The hosts were lucky not to concede five minutes after the restart, Guedes once again at the heart of it when he laid the ball on a plate for Oyarzabal, who was one-on-one with Henderson about eight yards out but fired straight at the Eagles keeper.

Henderson made his fifth save of the night, reacting quickly to deny Jon Gorrotxategi, and the visitors remained in the driver’s seat as the hour approached and Frank made his first substitution, replacing Eddie Nketiah with McNeil.

The breakthrough for Sociedad was the product of a patient build-up ending in Job Ochieng’s low pass for Sarr, who tapped it home.

The sides were level six minutes later. Palace were awarded a penalty after Martin – who was shown a second yellow card – tugged down Mateta, and the France striker made no mistake, burying the ball past Unai Marrero.

McNeil’s winner was the product of another acrobatic assist from fellow substitute Strand Larsen, whose scorpion kick teed up Pino at Coventry.

This time it was an overhead kick, which was heading wide of the goal but McNeil pounced at the post to poke it home.

Palace were awarded a second penalty when Soler was charged with a handball, but Marrero was equal to it, giving the visitors a fighting chance at a last-gasp leveller.

But Henderson produced his biggest save of the night, denying Orri Oskarsson at the death to preserve a much-needed Palace win.

Spalletti: ‘Juventus back on track with Cambiaso, my job is to find solutions’

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Luciano Spalletti admits Andrea Cambiaso’s stunning goal ‘got Juventus back on track’ for a 4-1 Europa League win over Omonia Nicosia, and Jeff Ekhator provides different solutions in attack.

It was a tough test for the Bianconeri, as Neto’s howler had allowed Omonia Nicosia to take the lead from an early corner with the Lubomir Satka header.

However, it had been turned around by half-time with a Cambiaso stunner and Ekhator’s first goal in the Juve jersey, with Francisco Conceicao and Jeremie Boga rounding out the scoreline in the second half.

“Until the Cambiaso goal, it looked a lot like the Venezia game,” confessed Spalletti with a smile on Sky Sport Italia.

“We were uncertain and the opponents took advantage, we felt the pressure of a potential counter-attack and everything started to feel complicated, but Cambiaso got us back on track with that wonderful goal, and from there the team played with greater calm.”

The jeers had been starting to rain down from the stands before that equaliser sparked the comeback, but Cambiaso badly needed it after recent struggles.

“It’s not easy to find form when called in, and some of these players hadn’t started for a while, so lacking consistency is difficult. However, we had some good moves, kept an acceptable level of pressing, and entertained the crowd with four fantastic goals.”

There were curlers from Boga and Cambiaso, albeit not quite the style of Juventus icon Alessandro Del Piero, who was in the Sky Sport Italia studio as a pundit.

“The Boga goal was my favourite because Gatti won it back with that high press, and sometimes the players are a little hesitant to go that far forward, as it leaves gaps behind them. Gatti then ran to follow the move and was in a position to score himself,” noted Spalletti.

“When you stop seeking something new, that is when you get pinned back. Ekhator didn’t get enough service in the first half, because at times we consolidate possession, and slow it down with too many touches, allowing the opponents to close up. It is particularly a problem when we have a striker like Ekhator who needs space to run into.

“He still managed to find the space himself two or three times, so we have to compliment Ekhator.”

Does that mean Juventus are no longer eager to sign a physical centre-forward in January, now that they have Ekhator fully fit?

“Everyone has their own characteristics, and my job is not to complain that something is missing, but to find the solution with what I have at my disposal. The first move must be mine, to find a way to provide the right service for the characteristics in the squad,” replied Spalletti.

Juventus receive another morale-boosting victory after the 4-0 against Venezia in Serie A on Saturday, and are on track to enter the Europa League Round of 16 directly after four wins and a 2-2 draw with Stade Rennais.

They are second only to Bournemouth, who have a 100 per cent record after five rounds, and ahead of Ferencvaros on goal difference.

Bournemouth 'allowed to dream' as fans book Europa League final hotels

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Marco Rose replaced Andoni Iraola - who ended up at Liverpool - in the summer

Bournemouth are dreaming of winning the Europa League after staging a 10-man fightback to join a select band of teams to win their first five European games.

The Cherries trailed 1-0 to Iago Aspas' goal at Celta Vigo, and then went down to 10 men midway through the first half when Bafode Diakite was sent off.

But second-half goals from Alvaro Rodriguez and Rayan gave the Cherries a 10th consecutive win in all competitions, extending their club record run.

Bournemouth are only the third side to win each of their first five European matches (excluding qualifiers and including the Inter-Cities Fairs Cup), after Nuremberg in 1961-62 and Ararat in 1972-73.

They top this season's table as the only club to win five out of five - and are closing in on a top-eight spot, which would mean they skip a play-off round and go straight into the last 16.

Some fans have told the BBC they have already booked hotels in Frankfurt for the final on 26 May.

As supporters sang about reaching that game, midfielder Alex Scott was asked for his thoughts on TNT Sports.

"Of course that's our dream. When you grow up wanting to be a footballer you dream of winning trophies," Scott said.

"Where this club has come from to where it is now, full credit to everyone who has built it.

"For players, it's a dream to be on the pitch playing for this club. We have still got wins to get to qualify into the top eight, and from there all focus is on pushing forward and hopefully we can go on and win it."

The Cherries are in unprecedented times, with their biggest trophy to date being when they won the second-tier Championship in 2014-15.

In terms of cups, they won the 1983â84 Associate Members' Cup - which later became the EFL Trophy.

Head coach Marco Rose, who replaced Andoni Iraola in the summer, said: "It's always allowed to dream.

"If you get a competition like this you want to achieve the best possible thing. We know it's far away to go. Many games. Now it's about getting into the top eight to avoid more games and then we can see step by step."

As well as just winning their games, Bournemouth are firing in the goals. They have netted at least twice in each match during this 10-game winning run.

And they are the first side to score at least twice in each of their first five matches in Europe since Queens Park Rangers in 1976.

The win on Thursday was made even more impressive by the fact they contested 75% of the game with 10 men.

It came days after Manchester United seemed to implode after an early red card against Liverpool, losing 4-0.

Former England manager Glenn Hoddle said on TNT: "They have deserved this result. They've played beautifully with 10 men - probably as good as I've seen a team play defensively and with the ball.

"I've not seen a team go down to 10 men for so long and dictate a game and deserve to win the game. They were absolutely brilliant."

Cherries boss Rose told BBC Radio Solent: "What a team, what a spirit, what an energy.

"I said to Ty [Tyler Adams] sometimes it's not about tactics and formations and stuff like this. [David] Brooks did a great job when we got one player less - he played right defender like he played it for 10 years. We changed in the right moment. Everyone did his job on a really high level.

"We were just relentless and we never stopped to believe in this win and that's why big, big, big congratulations to the team.

"You feel if you're one player down that it's a difficult night, but to handle a difficult night like this, it just shows the character of the team and of the whole group."

The coach's belief in what they are doing filters through to the players.

Amine Adli, who set up the equaliser, told the BBC: "At half-time, everyone still believed we can turn the game in our way and this is what happened.

"Everyone was like 'guys we can make it, we have to believe. If we score the game can change.'"

Earlier this month, the attacking midfielder said he saw a lot of similarities to the Bayer Leverkusen team he was part of in 2023-24.

That is not a bad comparison, considering they went unbeaten to win the domestic double, as well as reaching the Europa League final.

So who can blame the Cherries for dreaming?

FreightCar America: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Management is betting on an upcoming fleet replacement cycle, leaving open the question of how quickly volume returns to support the margin story.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than FreightCar America ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, FreightCar America (RAIL +4.39%) reaffirmed its full-year revenue guidance of $370 million to $410 million despite lower quarterly railcar deliveries. The business isn't relying solely on new railcar manufacturing. Expanding aftermarket sales and internal cost cuts could position the manufacturer for profit growth when railcar demand rebounds. Three things management said stand out.

FreightCar America acquired three aftermarket suppliers within a year, the latest after quarter-end. Organic parts growth and earlier deals boosted third-quarter aftermarket revenue by 65% year over year to $10.8 million with a 44.5% gross margin. This segment generates repeatable revenue that cushions cyclical drops in new railcar orders.

The company expanded its market share in addressable new railcar orders over the trailing 12 months while protecting its pricing discipline. This commercial momentum lifted total backlog value by 28% year over year to $284.5 million.

Management completed a workforce realignment in the second quarter that delivers about $12 million in annualized structural cost savings. The savings reduce fixed overhead without cutting manufacturing capacity. This operational setup could create operating leverage for the anticipated industry replacement cycle.

Railcar storage levels are falling across North America as shippers bring railcars back into service. The manufacturer maintains flexible production lines, expands aftermarket distribution, and reduces structural overhead, aiming to capture margin gains as order volumes rise.

Commercial focus remains tied to pricing discipline and customer retention across major railroads and industrial shippers rather than chasing low-margin volume.

'The Walking Dead' Meets 'War of the Worlds' in Noah Wyle's Gritty 5-Season Sci-Fi Thriller

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If aliens took over our world tomorrow, what would America look like months down the line? That's the exact question that the Steven Spielberg-produced TNT drama Falling Skies aimed to answer, and it did just that over five stellar seasons. Something of a cross between the sci-fi aesthetics of War of the Worlds and the apocalyptic character drama of The Walking Dead, this underrated cult classic deserves a rewatch — and now that it's on Netflix, there's no better time than the present.

Part of what set Falling Skies apart from the get-go was that it didn't begin with the invasion. We don't see the military's first attempt at defending our world as the major cities across the globe, Boston in this case, are descended on by alien overlords with less than admirable intentions. In step with The Walking Dead, although the world before is referenced at first, the show doesn't take long to throw you into the "new normal" that these horrid circumstances have created. When we first meet Tom Mason (played by Noah Wyle, who did double duty on this show and The Librarians during the latter's first season), for instance, he has already lost one son to these invaders on top of his wife. Although he was a history professor, he's now been conscripted as the second-in-command of the 2nd Massachusetts militia led by Col. Dan Weaver (Will Patton). We're not privy to how all that played out, but it doesn't matter. This is where the story begins.

While the show is unashamedly science fiction in the same vein as War of the Worlds, it's structured much like The Walking Dead in practice. Each episode follows this unlikely band of militia warriors on weekly quests to find new supplies, uncover more intel, or save a captive they've recently seen enslaved by the invaders. Of course, the show becomes a bit more serialized as time goes on, but many of those early episodes are spent establishing the characters so that when tragedy does strike, it means something. As the widowed father of three boys — Hal (Drew Roy), Ben (Connor Jessup), and Matt (Maxim Knight) — Tom has more to lose than just about anyone, and yet continues to help lead the fight against the Espheni in the same ground-up style as the American Revolution.

The cast is further rounded out by pediatrician-turned-jack-of-all-trades doctor Anne Glass (Moon Bloodgood), who herself has suffered serious loss at the hands of the invaders. As Tom and Weaver take the fight to the aliens on the literal battlefield, Anne works to uncover the secrets of their biology. Her part in the war is pivotal, as learning all they can about their new enemy gives the 2nd Mass the power to work against them. And, how could we forget about Colin Cunningham's John Pope? The motorbike-style outlaw became a vital part of the show throughout the entirety of its run, often standing in stark contrast to Tom and his morally conscious methods of warfare. Often butting heads, the pair each found ways to contribute to the 2nd Mass — and thus the war — that led to some impressive developments.

Falling Skies always thrived in the visual department. While some of the CGI may look a bit hokey now, back in 2011 when the show first premiered, the alien mechs and the Espheni were crafted quite well and blended effortlessly into the torn-down world around them. The alien creatures, such as the Skitters and the Volm, the alien race led primarily by Tom's friend and ally Cochise (Doug Jones) beginning in the third season, were likewise constructed with feature-level quality, using a blend of practical effects with digital enhancements. As much as we watched Falling Skies (which was already underrated in its day) for its characters and to see how the battle against Earth's first extraterrestrial invaders would play out, there was always something magnetic about the aliens and the battle sequences that ensued.

Although one might argue that Falling Skies was a stronger show early on (and there may be some truth to that), the TNT drama ultimately concludes on its own terms. Across five seasons and 52 episodes, the post-apocalyptic sci-fi series builds toward an inevitable conclusion that pits Tom and the 2nd Mass against the overlords trying to wipe out and enslave the human race. It's a powerful (and profound) climax that feels earned, and proves to be worth the journey. Falling Skies was a summer show, airing in the three-month break between traditional television seasons, which meant that it was always welcomed back by fans with open arms. All these years later, it still packs a serious punch for folks looking for something as patriotic as it is sci-fi-flavored.

Falling Skies is available for streaming on Netflix.

Gerald R. Molen, Schindler’s List & Jurassic Park Producer, Dies at 91

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Academy Award-winning producer Gerald R. Molen has passed away.

Molen, the iconic producer behind several of Steven Spielberg’s most critically acclaimed movies, has sadly died at the age of 91. Throughout his impressive career in the entertainment industry, Molen helped bring some of the industry's most famous films to the big screen, such as Jurassic Park, Schindler’s List, and Minority Report.

According to The Hollywood Reporter, Molen died on Monday, November 23, 2026. The producer's great-niece, Rachel Lorraine Pullan, confirmed the news on social media. The cause of death has not been disclosed at the time of this article's publication.

Molen's professional relationship with Spielberg began when he served as the unit production manager on the director’s 1985 drama The Color Purple. His work on the project opened many doors for the producer, leading him into a whole new chapter of his career. Spielberg also offered him an associate producer position on the 1987 science fiction comedy Batteries Not Included.

They went on to collaborate on many movies, such as 1991’s Hook, starring Robin Williams, Dustin Hoffman, and Julia Roberts. Molen eventually became the head of production at Spielberg’s Amblin Entertainment, where he managed the productions over several of the studio's projects.

During his time at Amblin, Molen was in charge of numerous major releases throughout the 1990s, including The Flintstones, The Little Rascals, Little Giants, Casper, and Twister. He was also involved with Spielberg’s 1997 dinosaur blockbuster The Lost World: Jurassic Park.

Celtic player ratings: Camilo Durán rescues team of low marks as one starter stands out

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Colombian’s late header ends Celtic’s 23-year Portuguese hoodoo in gritty Torreense triumph

Celtic clinched a vital 2-1 Europa League victory over second-tier outfit Torreense, thanks to an 84th-minute header from substitute Camilo Duran that ended the Scottish champions' 23-and-a-half-year wait for an away win on Portuguese soil.

Played nearly 50 miles away from Torreense’s 2,431-capacity home at Estadio Manuel Marques, which fails to meet UEFA stadium criteria, the 24,000-seater Municipal Stadium in Leiria was dominated by roughly 4,000 travelling Celtic supporters, making up two-thirds of the crowd. Despite the partisan backing, Martin O’Neill’s men made remarkably hard work of overcoming a side sitting 13th in Portugal’s second division with just two league wins from their opening ten matches.

There is an old adage in European football that fortunes earned by accident are rarely retained by design. For 45 disjointed minutes, Celtic offered a textbook demonstration of the principle. Handed an early advantage through the sort of comical defending usually reserved for Scottish clubs on their continental travels, Martin O’Neill’s side promptly retreated into their shell, allowing Portuguese second-tier minnows Torreense to restore parity and seize the initiative before the interval.

Celtic’s precarious Europa League campaign was balanced on a knife-edge. Having opened their league-phase campaign with three consecutive defeats before salvaging a breathless 3-2 victory over Beşiktaş in Glasgow earlier this month, O’Neill’s men arrived in central Portugal languishing in 32nd place.

Anything less than maximum points against a Torreense side operating in the lower reaches of Liga Portugal 2, yet remarkably seated 19th in this competition with six points following last season’s improbable Taça de Portugal triumph, threatened to render Celtic’s remaining fixtures against Omonia, Marseille, and Union Saint-Gilloise an exercise in mathematical futility.

O’Neill had cautioned on the eve of the tie that his squad lacked the pedigree to indulge in complacency. Initially, however, the evening appeared set to unfold with uncharacteristic smoothness for the Scottish champions.

Restored to the European squad as an injury replacement for Mika Baur, Jota marked his return to his homeland with immediate industry down the left flank. Inside nine minutes, the winger collected possession near the by-line and whipped a low, enticing delivery into the six-yard box. With Yang Hyun-jun hovering intently at the back post, Torreense full-back Javi Vázquez panicked under pressure, slicing his attempted clearance past his own goalkeeper and into the net.

It was a moment of extreme generosity, yet Celtic proved remarkably ungrateful recipients. Rather than using their early cushion to assert authority, the visitors lapsed into a passive, defensive shell. Though Celtic commanded the majority of possession, they managed to invite pressure rather than alleviate it.

Torreense, a club steeped in modest provincial history rather than continental glamour, sensed the vulnerability. Vázquez nearly atoned for his earlier mistake in the 17th minute with a venomous, low free-kick from a narrow angle that required hasty defensive intervention. Two minutes later, the equaliser duly arrived, born of Celtic’s chronic inability to clear their lines.

Given acres of space 20 yards from goal, midfielder Álex Alfaro unleashed a fierce drive that crashed against the underside of Sam Johnstone’s crossbar. As the ball dropped invitingly inside the penalty area, Celtic’s backline froze. Kieran Tierney’s tentative, lofted clearance succeeded only in teeing up Dany Jean, and the forward exhibited admirable composure to dart past Colby Donovan and drill a low finish beyond Johnstone’s reach.

Jota remained Celtic’s sole reliable source of invention. Celtic’s solitary threat to reclaim the lead prior to the break arrived on the stroke of half-time, when the Portuguese star's deep cross was hooked back across the face of goal by Luke McCowan, only for Kasper Høgh to arrive a fraction too late to apply the finishing touch.

Jota nearly restored the lead within seconds of the restart, only for his attempted dink to be gathered by a scrambling Adriel. But as the hour mark approached, Celtic’s frustration grew.

Former Hearts striker Musa Drammeh nearly dealt a massive blow to their hopes of progressing to the knockout stages by shrugging off a feeble challenge before dragging a golden chance narrowly wide of the far post.

James Forrest took over from the impressive Jota in the closing stages and the substitution proved to be a masterstroke. With Celtic seemingly sleepwalking toward a damaging result, Forrest swung a pinpoint cross in from the right, and Duran peeled away at the back post to power a downward header past Adriel. It was the Colombian’s 15th goal of a remarkable campaign, once again rescuing his side.

McCowan missed a late one-on-one opportunity to add a touch of gloss to the scoreline in the 89th minute, seeing his tame finish saved by Adriel’s boot. However, it did not matter. For the first time since O’Neill’s side triumphed at Boavista in April 2003, Celtic left Iberian soil victorious.

Marcus is a sports journalist with more than five years of experience, specializing in Football, Formula 1 and Tennis. He was a football network writer for Reach PLC, writing for pages such as the Manchester Evening News, Liverpool Echo and Football.London.

From 2022 to 2026, Marcus worked for SPORTbible, playing a role in growing the brand’s F1 output and audience, as well as covering a wide range of sports including boxing, MMA, darts and athletics.

So far in his career, Marcus has interviewed some of the biggest names in different sports, including the likes of Max Verstappen, Trent Alexander-Arnold, Ashley Cole, Jenson Button, Fred Vasseur and Tim Henman.

A Europa League clash was halted after chaos ensued due to fans displaying a flag in support of Palestine, with local police accused of using ‘disproportionate force’.

On Thursday night, following the end of the latest Champions League matchweek, the Europa League and the Europa Conference League took centre stage.

Inside the Batumi Arena in Georgia, a Europa League clash took place between Turkish Super Lig giants Besiktas and Israeli club Hapoel Be’er-Sheva.

The match was halted by referee Morten Krogh with just 26 minutes on the clock, with the score level at 0-0. Eventually, Besiktas went on to win the match 2-1, with Junior Olaitan and Orkun Kokcu scoring.

The match was marred by the chaos that led to the temporary suspension. According to The Guardian, Besiktas fans displayed a pro-Palestinian banner inside the stadium, with local police forced to step in.

It has been reported that Turkish media claimed that ‘Georgian police ‌entered the stands and used pepper spray on Besiktas fans’. Meanwhile, it is also believed that Turkish fans chanted ‘down with Israel’.

It was also reported by Turkish media that before kick-off, Besiktas players refused to shake hands with their opponents.

Reacting to the chaos that marred the Europa League match, the Turkish football federation slammed the local police in Georgia; a statement read: “We strongly condemn the unacceptable attitudes, behaviour and actions directed at Besiktas supporters inside and outside the stadium before and during the UEFA Europa League match between our representative Besiktas and Hapoel Be’er-Sheva in Georgia.”

Meanwhile, Besiktas issued a strong statement, accusing Georgian police of using ‘disproportionate’ force against their supporters.

The statement read: “We strongly condemn the security forces for their use of disproportionate force against our fans.

“We also extend our best wishes for a speedy recovery to all our fans affected by these incidents.”

Taking to social media, former Arsenal and Real Madrid star Mesut Ozil, who played in Turkey for Fenerbahce and Istanbul Basaksehir, slammed local police and congratulated Besiktas fans for taking a stand.

Ozil wrote: “I condemn the police intervention against Beşiktaş fans in Georgia who support the Palestinian people's struggle for freedom.

“I congratulate the Beşiktaş fans for taking this meaningful stand, refusing to remain silent in the face of the injustices in Palestine and speaking to the shared conscience of humanity. I wish a speedy recovery to all the fans subjected to the intervention.”

Meanwhile, Besiktas’ rivals, Galatasaray and Fenerbahce, also issued statements of their own as they condemned the action that was taken against Turkish fans.

An official release from the police has been issued following the incident.

The Marketing & Communications team is looking for a Senior B2B Performance Marketing Manager to lead growth marketing for its B2B business across digital channels. The team is lean and senior, ownership is broad, and the expectation is that the candidate handles both strategy and execution.

Own the B2B digital marketing strategy for HTS, setting channel priorities, budgets, and targets tied to pipeline and revenue

Plan, launch, and optimize paid campaigns across LinkedIn, Google Ads, and other digital channels, from audience and creative through to reporting

Build and run lead generation programs end to end, working with Sales to define lead quality and keep improving it

Own paid LinkedIn as a core B2B channel, running Sponsored Content, Lead Gen Forms, and ABM-style audience targeting to reach senior decision-makers at airlines, hotels, and financial institutions, and continuously testing creative and formats to improve lead quality

A/B test content and ad formats to optimize for lead generation and conversion

Test and scale new channels and tactics, and know when to cut what isn’t working

Track, analyze, and report on performance, including cost per lead, conversion rates, and pipeline contribution, and turn the data into clear recommendations for the team

Partner with Product, Sales, Legal, and Communications to keep messaging consistent and campaigns aligned with business priorities

Drive quality over quantity, optimizing campaigns for sales-qualified leads (SQLs) rather than raw lead volume, and working with Sales to continuously refine targeting, messaging, and lead criteria so that marketing effort translates into real pipeline

Work closely with the Marketing & Communications team while operating with a high degree of independence

Report on digital performance to senior leadership, translating channel metrics like cost per lead, conversion rates, and pipeline contribution into clear insights and recommendations that inform marketing investment and strategy

Work with sales, leadership and marketing comms to create strategy and process for tracking marketing leads through the entire sales pipeline

Potential to support growth within existing partners over time, through expansion campaigns and joint product launches with partner teams and Comms

The Odyssey's New Release Shuts Down The Last Remaining Concern About Nolan's Masterpiece

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The last rumbling concerns surrounding Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey have been put to rest with its latest release. Christopher Nolan has spent much of his career making films that demand to be experienced on the big screen. From the rotating corridors of Inception to the spectacular aerial sequences of Dunkirk and the enormous practical effects of Oppenheimer, his film-making style has always benefited enormously from theatrical presentation.

The Odyssey is perhaps his most ambitious attempt yet to make audiences appreciate the possibilities of large-format cinema. Based on Homer's ancient Greek epic, the 2026 blockbuster follows Matt Damon's Odysseus as he attempts to return home to Ithaca following the Trojan War, confronting mythical creatures, dangerous gods, and extraordinary obstacles along the way.

Its enormous scale and extensive use of IMAX photography made seeing it in a premium theatrical format particularly rewarding, while its spectacular imagery helped distinguish it from other recent blockbusters. Naturally, bringing such an ambitious production into the home presents some challenges. Fortunately, the new 4K release addresses the biggest remaining concern about experiencing Nolan's latest masterpiece outside the cinema.

Universal released The Odyssey on 4K Ultra HD Blu-ray and digital platforms on November 17 with a 1.78:1 aspect ratio, filling a conventional widescreen television. The theatrical IMAX presentation used the taller 1.43:1 ratio, meaning the home version loses a small amount of image at the top and bottom.

Despite what some purists might assert, this makes virtually no difference to the viewing experience, with no noticeable loss of important visual information. Furthermore, the 4K transfer brings out the textures of the enormous practical sets, the detail in the costumes, and the natural beauty of the locations. The sequences involving Odysseus' ships, the vast battlefields of Troy, and the dangerous open sea still look spectacular, even without the enormous IMAX screen surrounding the audience.

Obviously, watching The Odyssey at home cannot replicate the sheer scale of a 70mm IMAX screening, particularly when Ludwig Göransson's score and the film's enormous sound effects are filling a cinema auditorium. Indeed, IMAX is designed to be as immersive as possible with sound and vision. While no home viewing experience can replicate this, The Odyssey’s sharp 4K imagery is a pretty close domestic alternative.

One of the most rewarding things about revisiting The Odyssey is having the opportunity to appreciate Christopher Nolan's storytelling without being quite so overwhelmed by the spectacle. The theatrical experience is deliberately enveloping, with enormous set pieces, elaborate action sequences, and Göransson's thunderous score commanding attention. It's part of the appeal, but it can also make it difficult to appreciate some of the smaller details on a first viewing.

At home, there's more opportunity to concentrate on the performances and the relationships driving the story. Matt Damon's Odysseus is a particularly interesting protagonist because his journey is about far more than surviving dangerous encounters. It explores loyalty, identity, and the consequences of war – which all become easier to appreciate when the immediate visual spectacle isn't quite so overwhelming.

Robert Pattinson's performance also benefits from another viewing, as does the film's elaborate treatment of Homer's mythology. The supporting characters and the various encounters along Odysseus' journey contribute to a larger story about the difficulty of returning home after becoming somebody fundamentally different.

Universal's physical release also includes 12 behind-the-scenes featurettes, covering everything from the construction of the Trojan Horse and the enormous Moroccan battlefield sets to the film's practical effects, stunts, and Göransson's score. These extras offer another reason to revisit the production, particularly for anyone interested in Nolan's commitment to large-format film-making. No matter the aspect ratio, The Odyssey is a truly extraordinary cinematic achievement.

Splitting in Borderline Personality Disorder

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 26, 2026 | Reviewed by Kaja Perina

Have you ever met a new friend who seemed to think you were too great? You can do no wrong, you’re the first person to ever truly understand them, you’re so much better than their other friends. But then you have a disagreement or miss a birthday party, and they seem to just…turn on you? Now they feel sure that you were only ever kind to them because you wanted something; you’re just like everyone else.

Idealization and devaluation like this are two sides of the same coin—splitting, which is most associated with borderline personality disorder, or BPD. But it, or behaviors that closely resemble it, can also occur in people with narcissistic traits, depression, anxiety, autism, and other conditions. In fact, we all do it to some degree; teenagers are notorious for it. And it happens in all kinds of relationships: friendship, family, professional, marriage or partnership.

Obviously, neither extreme is a complete or fair perception of you. The truth of who you are exists somewhere between the ideal and the devalued, but that liminal space isn’t something your friend has access to right now.

And within that inability to see the space between all-good and all-bad lies the definition of splitting. In all of us, certain cognitive processes go offline when we become dysregulated or lose homeostasis: You don’t communicate well when you’re hungry, you can’t appreciate the beauty of a sunset when you’re angry, and so on. Likewise, when your friend or loved one becomes triggered and splits, their ability to see the nuance of who you are goes offline.

In a way, you each experience a similar betrayal. You’re late coming home from work, and by the time you arrive, your spouse’s overactive threat detection network has them convinced you’re seeing someone else. You don’t really love them. You’ll probably leave. You’re abandoning them.

Of course that’s not what you’re doing. So, you’re likewise blindsided by their response. Your spouse is fuming because you betrayed them, and they have some choice words for you. Every positive aspect of your relationship is read back to you in a negative light. It feels like they hate you.

There is no law that says you must stay in a relationship with this dynamic. However, if you’re committed to it, then equanimity is paramount. Your key to coping is not to internalize either idealization or devaluation. Even though it feels so personal, it’s not about you. It’s about their pain and fear.

It may help to think of splitting as a halted developmental process. Children learn good and bad first, and then they come to understand nuance later. But that learning can be disrupted by a number of childhood experiences, including:

When a person’s emotional development was disrupted in this way, their brain is quick to interpret small things as threats, such as:

It’s not willful, and that’s important for any person who loves someone with BPD to know. In most cases, people with borderline personality disorder experienced feeling chronically unsafe, uncared for, or unseen during an important developmental stage. Their brain is hyper-wired to detect this type of threat and respond defensively.

You’re under no obligation to engage in any relationship that doesn’t work for you. You decide what’s okay for you, and it’s a neutral choice. No one has to be right or wrong. And if a relationship ever involves threats or physical harm, that crosses a line no diagnosis excuses. Leave the situation and get help right away.

If you’re safe and you choose to stay involved: First, know that borderline personality disorder is highly treatable. Recovery and remission are possible and even likely with the right support. And there are a few things you can do to take care of yourself and support your loved one. For you:

And to support your loved one in feeling safe and secure so that they may recover:

Learned safety can rewire the fear that leads to splitting. As people with BPD build a sense of safety in treatment and in trustworthy relationships, the space between all-good and all-bad slowly and increasingly opens up to them.

Houben, M., Claes, L., Sleuwaegen, E., Berens, A., & Vansteelandt, K. (2018). Emotional reactivity to appraisals in patients with a borderline personality disorder: A daily life study. Borderline Personality Disorder and Emotion Dysregulation, 5, 18. https://doi.org/10.1186/s40479-018-0095-7

Mitchell, A. E., Dickens, G. L., & Picchioni, M. M. (2014). Facial emotion processing in borderline personality disorder: A systematic review and meta-analysis. Neuropsychology Review, 24(2), 166–184. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11065-014-9254-9

Porter, C., Palmier-Claus, J., Branitsky, A., Mansell, W., Warwick, H., & Varese, F. (2020). Childhood adversity and borderline personality disorder: A meta-analysis. Acta Psychiatrica Scandinavica, 141(1), 6–20. https://doi.org/10.1111/acps.13118

Story, G. W., Smith, R., Moutoussis, M., Berwian, I. M., Nolte, T., Bilek, E., Siegel, J. Z., & Dolan, R. J. (2024). A social inference model of idealization and devaluation. Psychological Review, 131(3), 749–780. https://doi.org/10.1037/rev0000430

American Journal of Psychiatry, 169(5), 476–483. https://doi.org/10.1176/appi.ajp.2011.11101550
