The 5 Greatest Bands of the British Invasion, Ranked

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Throughout the 1960s, there was a huge influx of British rock and pop music into American culture. English bands were dominating the charts and the music scene was becoming more globalized than ever before. The overseas relationship was reciprocal – these British bands were reworking American blues and R&B within a rock format, infusing psychedelic and progressive rock. Concurrently, American rock music began to emulate the British sound. This cultural phenomenon changed the face of music forever.

The British Invasion is an interesting and necessary study in the way music reinvents itself – how every musician is influenced by those who came before. This time period introduced this wonderful merging of sound to an international audience, causing a dizzyingly steep learning curve that produced some of the greatest music of all time. Here are the top five most important bands of the British Invasion.

The Animals formed in 1962 in Newcastle upon Tyne. They drew inspiration from American rhythm and blues artists, getting their start with covers by iconic musicians such as Jimmy Reed and Nina Simone. They had a big hand in the genesis of folk rock with their signature song: a cover of the American folk classic “House of the Rising Sun.” Their version is raw and emotional, capturing the struggles of working-class Americans during this time. The band represented a darker and grittier side of the British Invasion canon, the opposite end of the spectrum from the British pop that was also making its way overseas.

In the latter half of the sixties, they changed their lineup and their name to Eric Burden and The Animals. During this time, they delved into the psychedelic rock space, departing from their earlier blues influences. They laid the groundwork for prog rock, influenced punk/ garage music, and inspired artists across many genres (including Bruce Springsteen, The Grateful Dead, and Rod Stewart, to name a few.)

The Kinks also formed in 1962, and their rise in popularity aligned with that of the British Invasion. They released their breakout hit “You Really Got Me” in 1964, quickly achieving international success. They were heavy hitters on the British Invasion circuit – that is, until 1966, when they were banned from touring in America. While the reason has never been explicitly made public, it is believed to be a combination of promoter disputes, chaotic behavior from the band, and visa and permit issues. Nevertheless, they continued to grow in popularity in the UK and America alike.

The Kinks’ satirical single “Sunday Afternoon” was inspired by British music hall – a form of working-class variety theater rooted in Victorian-era taverns and pubs. The track was the biggest hit in the UK during the summer of 1966. The Kinks are often regarded as the original punk band, with much of their music featuring fuzzy guitar distortion and a driving, bluesy energy. They influenced countless bands across rock subgenres, including psychedelic bands like The Doors and Jefferson Airplane, punk rock groups such as the Ramones, and Oasis and other Britpop bands.

The Who popularized many techniques and terms that would become touchstones in hard rock. Their incredible mastery of their instruments allowed them to get super creative with their recording and production. They experimented heavily with synthesizers and feedback, adding layers of texture that were unprecedented at the time. Guitarist and songwriter (and legend!) Pete Townshend had a massive impact on rock music unto himself. He popularized the use of power chords and also coined the term “power pop” in reference to the group’s sound. Townshend wrote the band’s most popular album, Tommy, which was the first time the term “rock opera” was used publicly.

Drummer Keith Moon also had a big hand in the band’s popularity and original sound. His playing was chaotic and unruly – he pushed the rhythm section out of the pocket and onto center stage. Moon was the backbone of The Who’s explosive, energetic live performances. They were the loudest band of the era, and maybe of all time – a quality that was understandably polarizing.

And now we’ve reached the greatest rock band of all time. The Rolling Stones (who are remarkably still together and actively performing) are pioneers of hard rock music. They began their career with blues covers (a common theme on this list) and were greatly influenced by American R&B artists such as Chuck Berry, Muddy Waters, and Fats Domino. The band was drawn to the sheer power and emotional expression these musicians brought to their work.

Eventually, the Stones moved on to writing and recording their own original music – tracks that would, conversely, influence the next generation of American blues and rock artists, a prime example of the circuitous and reciprocal nature of music. These songs were primarily written by Mick Jagger and Keith Richards, a collaboration that sometimes led to tension in the group. Not that it affected them much – they have countless international hits. The Stones’ expansive discography bridged the gap between rhythm and blues and hard classic rock, and also between America and the UK. Their swagger and flamboyant rebellion made them popular in youth culture on both sides of the Atlantic.

Bet you didn’t see this one coming – Beatlemania, baby! Formed in 1960, The Beatles are the most influential band in popular music. They started out with a clean-cut, boy-next-door look and sound – all pop, no grit. With time, they evolved into something much more. They began to travel and broaden their horizons, getting to know themselves and the world on a deeper level. Their lyrics grew deeper and more poetic, and their influences expanded vastly: Indian and Western classical music, pop, psychedelia, and folk were all incorporated. Their evolution as a band is something to be studied – and it has been, quite thoroughly and quite often.

The British Invasion would never have happened without The Beatles – they were the foot in the door to the American popular music market. They were the voice of youth culture and the counterculture, a living and breathing documentation of the Western world from 1960 to 1970. Their constant reinvention and unprecedented levels of success have never been matched or recreated, and likely never will.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Better Than Ronaldo and Kane: Gareth Bale Named Man Utd Icon His Greatest Teammate

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Gareth Bale had a long and distinguished career, winning some of football's biggest prizes.

With an honours list as long as his - five UEFA Champions League winners' medals, three La Liga titles, three UEFA Super Cup wins, three Club World Cup triumphs, a Copa del Rey, a Supercopa de Espana and an English League Cup - he is certainly among those who can be considered to be among the greatest football players of all time.

It was not only domestically where he showed his class either, as he guided Wales to Euro 2016, Euro 202 and then the World Cup in 2022, with the nation reaching the semi-finals of the 2016 tournament.

Wales getting to the finals of big tournaments was unheard of, with their appearance at the 2022 World Cup the nation's first since 1958. The 2016 Euros was their first ever at the UEFA tournament, with Bale at the centre of the country managing to create history.

During his career he has shared a pitch with some extraordinary players. With Real Madrid he played alongside Ballon d'Or winners Cristiano Ronaldo and Luka Modric, with whom he also played for Tottenham Hotspur, where he also shared the grass with current Ballon d'Or winner Harry Kane.

So he has a big pool of players from which to choose the greatest teammate he has ever had.

Yet none of those three, who have won the biggest and best individual prize football has to offer are who he went for. Instead, he went with one of his countrymen, one of the greatest Premier League players of all time in Ryan Giggs.

Speaking in 2016, Bale told Foot Locker: "For me, the greatest player is Ryan Giggs. He was my hero growing up and is a legend in world football. It also helps he's a fellow Welshman."

In fairness to Bale, he had not played with Kane by then, with his stint with the Bayern Munich striker coming when he was getting towards the end of his career, when he went back on loan to Spurs from Real Madrid in 2020/21.

Incredibly, Bale and Giggs only shared the pitch on three occasions. They first played together in 2006/07 during a friendly against Brazil, before then playing together in European Championship qualifiers in 2007/08 against the Republic of Ireland - which they lost 1-0 - and San Marino - during which both Giggs and Bale scored in a 3-0 win.

Bale may not have played that often with his idol, but the Manchester United legend's career speaks for itself. He won the UEFA Champions League twice and the Premier League 13 times with United, with a UEFA Super Cup and Club World Cup and four FA Cups and four League Cups thrown in there as well, making him one of the most decorated players in football history.

The admiration between the pair was mutual and when Giggs was Bale's manager in the Wales national team, he said: "To win four, to go to Real Madrid, and be so successful, he's got to be [the best].

"He's the greatest I've seen. Obviously I didn't see John Charles play and many others around that time but he's the greatest I've seen.

"Great players turn up at the right moments and make the difference, and he's done it twice now in Champions League finals."

United face a fast decision as Pochettino weighs squad fit and a Brazilian star with real Premier instant impact.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Topics Amazon Apple Black Friday Gadgets Kitchen

This year, Black Friday falls on Nov. 27. While retailers often start featuring their best markdowns on Black Friday itself, there are plenty of early deals worth shopping in the lead-up. Most retailers' official sales do start well before the big day, so shopping strategically ahead of time can save you from missing out on a deal entirely (or dealing with the chaos the day after Thanksgiving).

Cyber Monday takes place on the Monday following Thanksgiving. This year, the shopping event falls on Monday, Nov. 30. While Cyber Monday originally began as an online counterpart to in-person shopping on Black Friday, the two events have merged into an extended online shopping bonanza.

Amazon's Black Friday Week sale kicked off on Nov. 19, which means deals have been live for a week now. Amazon has an incentive to spread out the shopping (to give its delivery drivers a break), so you no longer have to wait until actual Black Friday to save. Deals will continue to go live through Nov. 30.

Amazon: The retail giant's Black Friday Week sale began on Nov. 19, with new deals dropping daily through Cyber Monday on Nov. 30.

Best Buy: The electronics store's Black Friday sale kicked off on Nov. 19 and runs through Nov. 28. The Best Buy Cyber Monday Sale Event runs for 48 hours on Nov. 29 and Nov. 30.

Walmart: The big box store's Black Friday sale runs from Nov. 23 to Nov. 29 (with deals online only from Nov. 23 to Nov. 26). It's hosting an online-only Cyber Monday sale from Nov. 30 to Dec. 2.

Target: Target's weeklong Black Friday sale started Nov. 22 and goes through Nov. 29.

The Home Depot: The Home Depot's official Black Friday sale ran from Nov. 5 to Nov. 25, wrapping up before the actual holiday. However, the home improvement store will continue to offer new deals throughout the week, and its Cyber Week sale kicks off on Nov. 30 and runs through Dec. 6.

With the day upon us, in-store Black Friday prices will be live at most retailers on Nov. 27. Opening are as follows:

Best Buy: 6 a.m. local time, close at 10 p.m. local time

Home Depot: 6 a.m. local time, closing varies by location

That basically means she puts her hair through a lot, scrolls through a lot of dating apps, and rotates through a lot of different headphones. In addition to testing out and rounding up the best products, she also covers deals for Mashable, paying an especially obsessive amount of attention to Apple deals and prices. That knowledge comes in handy when she's covering shopping holidays like Prime Day and Black Friday, which she's now done for five years at Mashable.

Timothy Beck Werth is the Tech Editor at Mashable, where he leads coverage and assignments for the Tech and Shopping verticals. Tim has over 15 years of experience as a journalist and editor, and he has particular experience covering and testing consumer technology, smart home gadgets, and men’s grooming and style products. Previously, he was the Managing Editor and then Site Director of SPY.com, a men's product review and lifestyle website. As a writer for GQ, he covered everything from bull-riding competitions to the best Legos for adults, and he’s also contributed to publications such as The Daily Beast, Gear Patrol, and The Awl.

Tim studied print journalism at the University of Southern California. He currently splits his time between Brooklyn, NY and Charleston, SC. He's currently working on his second novel, a science-fiction book.

Lauren Allain is a freelance journalist covering deals at Mashable. She graduated from Western Washington University with a B.A. in journalism and holds an M.B.A from Webster Leiden. ‘It’s very beautiful’ - Monaco applaud Hearts as Vrancken insists cruel defeat ‘doesn’t make a difference’

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wouter Vrancken has stressed Hearts’ objective remains the same despite conceding a last-minute goal that led to an agonising defeat against AS Monaco at Tynecastle.

Hearts’ hopes of reaching the knockout stage rest on winning their last two Conference League matches against Bosnian side Borac Banja Luca and Cypriot team Pafos, probably by high-scoring margins.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

The Tynecastle manager was not giving up on anything after the 3-2 loss, with United States World Cup star Folarin Balogun scoring the winning goal in the final seconds of added time.

Hearts had taken the lead after a sensational strike from Jordi Altena on 15 minutes. A penalty from Balogun levelled things before the visitors took the lead with a goal from Edam Diop five minutes before half time. Sabah Kerjota scored the goal of the night on the stroke of half-time after curling in with his left foot to leave everything to play for in the second half.

“I think the point doesn't make a difference,” said Vrancken. “There's also something that really was pleasing for me is that when it was 2-2, we still wanted to go (for the win).

"You saw also when it was a throw-in, we wanted to do it fast, we wanted to go for the win. Against a side like this, in the situation we're in, I am so really proud of this also.

"Three points would make a difference," he added. "Zero or one point doesn't make a difference towards the next games, so you have to win the next games. One point or no points doesn't change anything in the next games.”

The Belgian stressed his pride in his players and reiterated that Hearts were missing nine injured players as well as Leart Kabashi and Eduardo Ageu, who were not named in the European squad. He did, however, lament the lack of concentration that saw them concede so late in the game after 93-and-a-half minutes of lung-busting effort. He was also unhappy that Hearts were not cute enough to stop Monaco in their tracks as they broke free for the winning goal. Substitute Finlay Pollock had the chance to clip Mamadou Coulibaly’s heels before the midfielder played in Balogun. Ruthlessness might be something Hearts are lacking at this level. A reporter pointed out that it was a cruel way to lose a match.

“It did feel like this,” said Vrancken. “But at the same time, we have to learn in that situation because I think until then we were really solid about giving chances away. And I expect more from guys who come in from the side (substitutes).

“We can win it with a fresh player and in the middle we can win it with a fresh player. Or you make a foul somewhere to keep the result - I expect this. This is like battling for the team, as I call it.

“We have to do better there,” he added. “But the biggest thing, of course, is to be really proud of what performance we put on the park today, because it's against a really good side. Knowing that we have still nine injuries - I will name it - and we have a lot of players not on the European list today and still do this against that team. I'm really proud of what we did.”

He hailed the quality of his side’s goals. “You see also the confidence those guys have. This is what we wanted after the weekend, of course - that you take some confidence from the dynamics of what we did there. I think the conviction around the box was there, in both boxes actually. It's an important thing to take with us towards the next games coming, especially in this run of games.”

Hearts host Celtic, who beat them 7-1 last month, at Tynecastle on Sunday. Vrancken is firmly in the belief that his side have their mojo back following this and the 5-2 defeat to Falkirk earlier this month. Hearts have since beaten Dundee 8-0 and been edged out by Monaco in a five-goal thriller. “We have played two good games and two good performances against good sides, especially today,” he said. “And now you have the confidence. But we are still playing against a good side on Sunday with only two days' rest, so we have to manage fatigue and make sure that we are as fresh as possible again to make up for something a few weeks ago, especially because it's an at-home game.”

Monaco manager Filipe Luis, meanwhile, denied that relief was the primary emotion after leaving it so late to collect three points. “It is not relief, we compete to win,” he said. “We had an amazing opportunity to win tonight but we had a tough opponent.

“They scored two unbelievable goals,” he added. “You almost need to applaud them. When a team scores two goals like this it is almost nobody’s fault. They are two fantastic goals. Honestly, it’s very beautiful.”

He spoke admiringly of the atmosphere. “I played against Celtic (with Atletico Madrid) in 2011 I think in the Europa League,” he said. “We are lucky to have the opportunity to play these types of games in different countries. It is so intense. I am happy to have had this opportunity to be here tonight – and happy with the result.”

Should You Buy Tesla Stock Before December?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Merger talks might jolt Tesla stock by year-end, or they might now.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Tesla (TSLA -1.18%) has had a challenging year. Despite its core electric vehicle (EV) business often performing better than expected, partly due to soaring oil prices, the company's shares have significantly lagged broader equities. However, the year isn't over, and there is a chance Tesla could finish 2026 with a bang, given ongoing developments. With that said, should investors consider buying the stock before December?

Tesla's CEO, Elon Musk, now leads two publicly traded corporations, the other being Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.30%), which went public in June. Rumors of a potential merger between Tesla and SpaceX have been circulating since before SpaceX was public, but a comment Musk made during SpaceX's third-quarter earnings call added fuel to the fire.

Musk said that SpaceX and Tesla have formed independent special committees to discuss a potential merger between the two corporations. And more recently, five board directors from both companies have reportedly been discussing a potential all-stock merger that would make the combined entity worth about $3.5 trillion, with a deal possible by year-end. Importantly, this hasn't been confirmed by either company. But it's still worth figuring out the potential benefits and risks of this proposed acquisition.

Suppose SpaceX acquires Tesla at a 20% premium in an all-stock deal: SpaceX would have to issue a substantial number of new shares, thereby significantly diluting existing shareholders. Tesla's stock might rise, at least initially, while SpaceX's could decline unless investors believe the merger will generate enough synergies to justify the acquisition premium.

There are indeed some reasons to think that the combined entity could create more value than either would on its own. For instance, both Tesla and SpaceX are investing massive sums in artificial intelligence (AI). As a single entity, they could eliminate redundant investments and lower costs, while leveraging their larger size to negotiate better deals with suppliers.

Also, some of Tesla's work, including its energy infrastructure and the Optimus humanoid robot, could be of immense value to SpaceX. So, that could be good news for the business' long-term prospects, although whether the proposed combined entity will, on balance, create more value is by no means guaranteed. However, one major source of uncertainty will likely come from regulators. The deal will probably attract significant attention from lawmakers, at least some of whom may try to stop it.

Whether they succeed or not, it's hard to predict the impact on Tesla and SpaceX. And even as a combined entity, the company will be significantly exposed to regulatory risk across businesses such as space launch, satellite-based connectivity, EVs, and robotaxis.

Putting aside the acquisition talks, Tesla's own business has somewhat attractive prospects. The company's work in the robotaxi industry looks promising. Tesla launched the Cybercab, a self-driving vehicle with no steering wheel, no pedals, and no mirrors, earlier this year. Provided it can scale its Cybercab fleet and capture a decent share of the market over the next five to 10 years, the robotaxi business could meaningfully contribute to the company's revenue and profits.

And Tesla does have an advantage over other robotaxi leaders. The company has a growing number of subscriptions to its Full Self-Driving software, which it offers to EV owners for $99 per month. A growing FSD ecosystem grants the company access to more data to train and improve its software. And as Tesla has pointed out, the FSD software is becoming one of the reasons people buy Teslas. That's great news for the company's future.

But one of Tesla's problems is valuation. At 157.3x forward earnings, the market has already priced in significant success from the company's robotaxi ambition. So, I'd be skeptical about buying Tesla's shares at current levels. I'd prefer a much lower price. The potential merger with SpaceX changes very little, in my view, and actually adds significantly more uncertainty to the mix.

The combined entity (if a merger does happen) will certainly be worth serious consideration, but investors with an average tolerance for risk and volatility shouldn't buy Tesla stock right now, whether or not they expect the company to merge with SpaceX.

Lana Del Rey Says New Albums Are ‘Getting Close’ to Completion: ‘I’m Happy With Them’

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The singer-songwriter updated fans on Stove and its companion album, Spyda, while seemingly teasing cryptic lyrics.

Lana Del Rey is giving fans an update on her long-awaited new music.

In a Thanksgiving Day Instagram post on Thursday (Nov. 26), the alt-pop singer-songwriter revealed that her upcoming country album, Stove, and its companion album, Spyda, are “definitely getting close to wrapping up.”

“The albums are coming along so well. I’m happy with them,” Del Rey wrote alongside a photo gallery. “Taking time cooking but definitely gonna be out at the perfect time considering how much music is on both of them. Definitely getting close to wrapping up although did take so many unexpected turns over the years. Can’t say I saw it coming but what came out of it feels meant to be — it makes me wonder where I would even go after this.”

Although Del Rey didn’t reveal a release date for either project, she offered insight into the albums’ evolution.

“It’s an unusual coupling of albums that explain where I started when I announced everything 4 years ago to my perspective now on my internal web of people and their reactions to my happiness and physical growth and success,” she wrote.

Del Rey also offered insight into the albums’ introspective themes, referencing what may be a song titled “You’ve Got a Friend.” She described its lyrics as containing substantial “internal dialogue” about the events of the past several years, the lessons she’s learned, and whether everything happens on “God’s time.”

“Makes me feel like there’s no way the things that happen could be random – one thing that comes to mind is that little quote… You can have it all, but you can’t have it all at the same time,” she wrote.

The “Summertime Sadness” singer also revealed that the physical editions will be an important part of the projects.

“One of the more important things actually with this album is how beautiful the physical copies are, the copper plates alone took half a year to make,” she wrote.

Del Rey concluded her post with what could be a tease of new lyrics. “All because I met Happy, who would’ve thought it would make you so mad?” she wrote alongside a musical score emoji.

The singer-songwriter’s Thanksgiving post also included a message of gratitude and reflection.

“Sending love to absolutely everyone today,” she wrote alongside a video of herself hugging a tree and driving through a city filled with lit palm trees. “Thinking of the history of our country and trying to energetically honor so many of the early traditions and culture of the people who were first on this beautiful land. My heart is always with you.”

She added, “Also grateful to my family who have continued to spend time together for our gatherings, old and new. It’s the glue that keeps me together in this crazy game we call life. Hope we can find one thing to feel grateful for.”

In mid-October, Del Rey teamed up with Zach Bryan on “Lies,” featured on his eight-song EP, Young Manhood and Other Donatable Organs. She also released “First Light,” the theme song for the James Bond video game of the same name.

The artist’s upcoming albums will mark her first full-length releases since Did You Know That There’s a Tunnel Under Ocean Blvd, which debuted at No. 3 on the Billboard 200 in March 2023.

See Del Rey’s full post on Instagram here.

What Teachers Know (Or Don’t Know) About Left-Handedness

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 26, 2026 | Reviewed by Kaja Perina

All over the world, teachers play a crucial role in their pupils' learning experience. This is especially true for left-handed children, who, for a long time, have been forced to write with their right hand against their will in many countries. Thankfully, the awful practice of handedness relearning is now a thing of the past in the US and many other countries, and today, both left-handed and right-handed pupils are encouraged to write with their naturally dominant hand in school. This makes it important that teachers have some basic knowledge about handedness and also the ergonomic needs of left-handed kids in learning environments. As handedness typically is not a main focus in teacher education, it is, however, largely unknown what knowledge the average teacher has about handedness and the specific learning needs of left-handed children. Thus, more psychological research on this important topic is needed.

A new study entitled “Handedness literacy in teachers: Data from Greece, Serbia, and Belgium”, just published in the scientific journal Laterality, aimed at closing this gap in the psychological literature by focusing on teachers’ handedness literacy (Papadatou-Pastou and co-workers, 2026). Handedness literacy refers to a person’s general knowledge and understanding of handedness.

The study team, led by renowned handedness researcher Marietta Papadatou-Pastou, asked more than 800 prospective and in-service teachers to fill out a questionnaire with various questions about their knowledge of handedness and about themselves. The teachers came from three different European countries: Greece, Serbia, and Belgium.

The questionnaire the teachers had to fill out consisted of four main parts. The first part consisted of open-ended questions about left-handedness, such as “Do left-handed students differ from right-handed students?”. The teachers had to write answers to these questions. The second part of the questionnaire consisted of 18 questions quizzing the teachers about their knowledge of handedness. The third part of the questionnaire contained further questions about handedness, and the fourth part contained some questions about the teachers themselves, such as their own handedness and their age, among others.

The analysis of the data yielded several interesting results about teachers’ handedness literacy.

The analyses of the open-ended questions about handedness in the first part of the questionnaire showed that teachers generally had a supportive attitude towards left-handed pupils. The teachers wrote about different aspects of handedness, such as the underlying neurobiology or specific challenges left-handed pupils face, or their ergonomic and classroom needs. In general, they were in favor of letting children choose which hand they like to write with.

The analysis of the handedness literacy scores revealed that teachers knew some things about handedness, but also had quite a lot of knowledge gaps. As there were 18 questions and the scientists gave 1 point per correct question, the maximum score for handedness literacy was 18. Teachers from Serbia had an average score of 8.11 (45% correct answers). Teachers from Belgium were slightly better as they had an average score of 8.85 (49% correct answers), as were teachers from Greece, who had an average score of 8.90 (49% correct answers).

So, in all three countries, teachers had less than 50% correct answers- if there were a left-handedness test, they would probably fail! One thing that many teachers got wrong was that they thought that left-handed pupils were more intelligent than right-handed pupils. Also, many teachers thought that there were more left-handers in the population than the actual percentage (10.6%). Interestingly, left-handed teachers did not show higher handedness literacy than right-handed teachers.

Many of the teachers who participated in the study stated that they thought that handedness literacy in teachers needs to be improved. The results of the study show that this is actually true. This suggests that teacher education should include more scientific information on left-handedness, so that teachers can optimally educate left-handed kids.

Papadatou-Pastou, M., Papadopoulou, A. K., Kovic, V., Gerrits, R., & Vingerhoets, G. (2026). Handedness literacy in teachers: Data from Greece, Serbia, and Belgium. Laterality, 1–43. Advance online publication. https://doi.org/10.1080/1357650X.2026.2753053

Every Harry Potter Book, Ranked by Goodreads

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Harry Potter franchise is a legendary series in the world of children's literature. The series defined an entire generation of readers with its inventive wizarding world and how it matured alongside the audience, dealing with heavy themes of death, love, and corruption born of systemic prejudices. Warner Bros. adapted the books into an equally successful film series, which brought the franchise to a wider audience and has served as the baseline for future additions, such as the Fantastic Beasts prequels.

Though the last book came out in 2007, the Harry Potter saga has maintained a dedicated fanbase and continues to delight and entrap new readers. Everyone has their preferences, but the consensus tends to look more positively towards some books compared to others. Fans on Goodreads have ranked each Potter book, and while the lowest-rated might be predictable, the highest one might be a bit unsurprising.

Book two follows Harry's second year at Hogwarts School of Witchcraft and Wizardry, though it doesn't start off very well. A House Elf named Dobby tries to prevent him from going on account of a strange plot, so Harry and his friend Ronald Weasley have to fly an enchanted car to get to school. However, as the semester goes on, students are attacked by a mysterious creature that is linked to Salazar Slytherin, one of the school's four founders.

Harry Potter and the Chamber of Secrets is not a bad book, but consensus does put it as the worst in the main series. Examples of why include a repetitive structure to its predecessor, Hogwarts' staff looking incompetent as the attacks escalate, a number of plot contrivances and deus ex machina, and the fact that Albus Dumbledore would hire such an unqualified teacher as Gilderoy Lockhart to teach Defense Against the Dark Arts. That said, it has its positives as well, such as the introduction of Dobby, memorable set pieces like the Womping Willow and the titular chamber, and an expansion of the Wizarding World through new spells, monsters, and tension between pure-blood wizards and those of mixed heritage.

The one that started it all. Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone introduced the world to Harry Potter, a young boy abused by his spiteful and selfish aunt and uncle, who learns on his 11th birthday that he is a wizard. Upon entering the Wizarding World, Harry also learns that he is a celebrity because, as an infant, he survived the Dark Lord, Voldemort, and somehow brought about his downfall. Unfortunately, while he does make new friends upon entering Hogwarts, his school year is interrupted by various strange happenings linked to an item stored in the castle.

The book perfectly captures the beautiful and whimsical nature of the Wizarding World, making you just as eager as Harry to learn more about it. Rowling also does a good job of crafting an effective mystery around who is trying to get the titular stone and why. Alas, due to being the first book in the series, it does suffer from the need to establish the world and principal characters, and the plot does have a few holes/contrivances, but the rest of the series exists thanks to its foundations.

Harry's fifth year at Hogwarts is a doozy: the Ministry of Magic refuses to believe that Lord Voldemort has returned, instead thinking this is a plot by Dumbledore to seize power. Thus, Dolores Umbridge is sent to Hogwarts to keep an eye on things and ensure that Dumbledore can't use the students as his personal army. Meanwhile, Harry is struggling to process his trauma from Voldemort's return, while the Dark Lord's minions hunt for a prophecy that holds the details of his defeat.

Harry Potter and the Order of the Phoenix is the longest book in the series and is filled with lots of good and bad. The worst part is Harry's grief, which, while presented realistically, does make him frustrating to follow because of how short-tempered and aggravating he is. Fortunately, it's balanced out by Umbridge's unique brand of villainy, great backstory revelations that reveal more about the First Wizarding War, and a fantastic climax featuring rooms in the Department of Mysteries and a creative duel between Dumbledore and Voldemort.

Harry's fourth year at Hogwarts sees it host the Triwizard Tournament, a friendship-building event with two sister schools: Beauxbatons Academy of Magic and Durmstrang Institute. However, everyone is shocked when the magical Goblet of Fire that chooses the participants reveals Harry's name as well, even though he never put his name in. Due to the binding magic of the goblet, Harry has no choice but to compete, forcing him to prepare for three deadly challenges while trying to solve the mystery of how and why his name showed up in the first place.

Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire is officially when the series shifts from the whimsical adventures in magic schools to the much darker and more complex stories that dominate its later half. The tournament itself offers plenty of exciting set pieces, from fire-breathing dragons to a hedge maze full of monsters. Then, while waiting for the next challenge, the characters partake in all manner of subplots, including relationship drama, missing gold, a reporter who knows way more than she should, and Hermione's attempt to give the House Elves equal rights.

Harry's sixth year takes place during the Second Wizarding War, casting a net of uncertainty as he wonders about Voldemort's next move and how to stop him. To that end, Dumbledore takes Harry through a series of memories to decipher clues about Voldemort's powers, and hopes that Harry can get the last one they need from Professor Horace Slughorn. Meanwhile, Harry also starts keeping an eye on Draco Malfoy, whom he believes has become a Death Eater and is planning something nefarious.

Harry Potter and the Half-Blood Prince is the book that sets up everything we need to know for the endgame of the series. We get a massive deep dive into Voldemort's backstory that helps explain how he became the Dark Lord we know today; the mysteries are engaging, and the ending is one of the most shocking moments in children's literature. That said, it's also the book where Rowling tried to describe Harry's growing feelings for Ginny Weasley as a green monster roaring in his chest, and it's got a lot of awkward couple drama, which can be a bit of a turnoff.

Harry's third year of Hogwarts almost ends before it begins when he accidentally casts a spell that turns his aunt Marge into a balloon and runs away from home. While he's still allowed to go back to school, he's informed that a former servant of Voldemort's named Sirius Black has escaped from Azkaban Prison, and the horrific emotion-sucking Dementors that guard the prison are out hunting for him. Fortunately, the new Defense Against the Dark Arts Teacher, Remus Lupin, is an old friend of Harry's parents and offers to help him learn a spell to keep the Dementors at bay.

Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban is the first book that really takes a dark turn, but it still has tons of whimsical moments to balance them out. The dramatic moments are where the book shines, and a lot of time is dedicated to exploring concepts of good, evil, and how people change for better or for worse when pushed to their limits. It's also unique in that Voldemort has the least impact on this book, not appearing physically and hardly even being mentioned. Instead, it gives us the horrific manifestations of depression that are the Dementors as antagonists.

Rather than attend Hogwarts for their seventh year, Harry, Ron, and Hermione decide to hunt down Voldemort's Horcruxes in a bid to make him mortal. Unfortunately, they don't know where to find them, and Voldemort has taken over the Ministry of Magic, meaning that there is now a nationwide manhunt for Harry. What's more, Harry learns some shocking news about the early life of Albus Dumbledore, making him question if he can truly trust his old professor or not.

Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows had the monumental task of bringing this series to a close, and it's safe to say that it struck the landing. The first half of the book is the hunt for the Horcruxes, while the second half is structured around the Battle of Hogwarts, where the heroes face off against Voldemort's army of dark wizards, werewolves, giants, Dementors, and other foul creatures. Amongst it all are some fantastic explorations of legacy, death, sacrifice, and presenting long-established characters as more complex than they first appeared.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Janus International Group: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Steel costs and rate pressure are delaying the storage builds that feed Janus's volumes, and that tension is the part long-term holders must weigh.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:07PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Janus International Group ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings conference call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Janus International Group (JBI +1.34%) lowered its full-year revenue guidance to between $855 million and $865 million. A sharp slowdown in self-storage development tests whether the manufacturer can defend its margins through cost cuts and technology sales when construction activity turns sluggish. Three takeaways from management's comments show how the business is adapting.

Higher input costs and financing rates squeeze developers' margins, forcing customers to delay construction of new facilities. That pause directly cuts into delivery volumes for self-storage building components.

When top-line volume drops, structural cost reductions help protect profitability. Management cut expenses across operations and manufacturing to align its cost footprint with lower market demand.

Management is expanding its Noke Smart Entry platform, a technology-enabled access control system for self-storage facilities, to build recurring digital revenue. Adding tech content to existing sites creates recurring revenue opportunities beyond physical steel doors.

Demand remains under pressure across North American self-storage channels, and market conditions show no immediate signs of recovery. Janus relies on its broad customer distribution and technology adoption to defend market share while maintaining pricing discipline.

Disciplined capital allocation and cash generation remain central priorities while new project starts lag.

John Travolta Didn’t Recognize Himself as Alleged JFK Assassin Johnny Roselli in ‘November 1963’: ‘I Went, “Oh My God, He’s the Devil”’

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

John Travolta rarely connects with all his characters. Sometimes, he even loves to hate them.

“They say you have to find something you like,” the actor explains. “But I’ve never felt that. I think you have to like the result, pulling it off. That’s where the joy comes from. To become these people, you don’t have to identify with them or enjoy being them. You just have to be excellent at executing them.” Related Stories ‘SNL’ Sets Timothée Chalamet and Robert De Niro as Final Hosts of 2026 ‘SNL’: Druski Dons Whiteface as Elizabeth Holmes in ‘You Can See Everything’ Parody

Even so, Travolta faced one of the biggest challenges of his career playing a rather literal executioner in Roland Joffé’s “November 1963,” which opened the Torino Film Festival with its international premiere.

On paper, Johnny Roselli seems right in Travolta’s wheelhouse: a real-life wiseguy turned showbiz fixer, an Al Capone protégé whose career took him from Hollywood to Las Vegas to the Bay of Pigs, leaving a trail of bodies along the way.

“He was an odd mix,” says Travolta. “He was the guy who asked Sinatra to connect Kennedy with the mob. He hooked Kennedy up with Marilyn Monroe. He was a Hollywood icon of sorts, and had this other, darker side as a hitman. That’s complex. I’ve done dark subject matter before, but this was real. It actually happened, and that’s the difference.”

Roselli was purportedly a CIA go-between and sharpshooter. “November 1963” takes that history a step further, imagining him as a key triggerman on the Grassy Knoll, directly responsible for the killing that shook a nine-year-old boy in Englewood, New Jersey, and still haunts the actor to this day.

“It was the biggest crime of the century,” says Travolta, instantly transported back to second grade. “The announcement came at 11 a.m. Eastern Standard Time that the President had been shot, and then three hours later, at 2 p.m., they confirmed that he had passed. We all went to our homes, and all I can remember is being home with my father, glued to the television, sitting on his lap and trying to make sense of it all.”

Travolta carried that weight onto the set, where, he says, he was asked “to play a very evil man with moments of humanity.”

“The actors were all suffering because everybody who was hired was a very decent person, and we all had to play, to some degree, people who were not,” he continues. “So it was harder to go home every night. It took a glass of wine and a nice dinner to snap out of the zone.”

That feeling returned in full force when Travolta finally watched his finished performance at Torino’s opening night, prompting a kind of out-of-body experience.

“I thought, ‘Wow, I can’t believe that’s me,’” he says. “I’ve never seen myself lose my soul in a film, and [here] I was soulless. I was very proud that I could portray that, because it’s against my very essence. When I looked at it on screen, I went, ‘Oh my god, he’s the devil.’”

Written and produced by family members of Windy City crime boss Sam Giancana, “November 1963” foregrounds the Chicago Outfit’s role in the assassination plot, leaving CIA and government involvement for a possible follow-up.

“This is not history; it’s about human beings,” Travolta says. “There will be lots more to tell, but you can’t do it all at the same time.”

That deliberately incomplete picture appealed to Joffé, who translates the murky history into a mise-en-scène of skewed angles and funhouse reflections.

“That’s how history comes to us,” he says. “I’d say this story is probably 90% true, insofar as no one will ever know what really happened. What’s most important to me is whether it’s credible. And has that mindset changed? The transactional mindset, the mafia mindset, the idea that power is all that counts and loyalty has to be bought at the point of a gun. My feeling is that it doesn’t disappear. It transmutes. It may become a political party. It may become the way business acts.”

In one telling revision, the film casts slain Dallas policeman J.D. Tippit, whose murder was attributed to Lee Harvey Oswald, as a key conspirator. Joffé uses the twist to further muddy the waters while dismissing the notion of a neatly orchestrated plot.

“I reject the conspiracy theory,” he says. “If it were a conspiracy, we would have known by now, and we absolutely do not. What it was is collusion, and collusion is very different. Collusion is subtle. It happens in the shadows. It’s a nod, a wink, a knowing glance. It’s putting a gun in the right place.”

The distinction, he argues, is that no grand design is required. The CIA and organized crime had already worked together on plots to kill Fidel Castro, but that didn’t mean anyone had to convene a committee to plan Kennedy’s assassination.

“It’s not about control,” Joffé says. “It’s about the fact that life is chaotic, and the killing of the President comes out of that. We made a film about chaos.”

Manchester United, Liverpool and Tottenham have enlisted London-based media, entertainment and technology specialists, Wiggin

WoW: Forever min-maxers are upset that its 40-player PvP mode now actually requires PvP to win

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Blizzard's latest patch prevents Alterac Valley from being a glorified reputation farm.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Despite being set in the past, WoW: Forever is an attempt to capture the spirit of the old game by injecting it with new quirks that challenge new and returning players alike to approach it differently than any other version of the MMO. This is why familiar old dungeons can drop new loot, like an armor set that turns you into a snake.

Blizzard has said that its goal is to "unsolve" the game as best it can so players don't settle into the same routines they're used to. This is especially true for min-maxed strategies that threaten to suck the fun out of enjoying the game, or "the journey", as Blizzard likes to call it.

It's a lofty goal that can't be achieved without persistently fiddling with the knobs that encourage players to do one thing over another—which is the very thing that differentiates WoW: Forever from WoW Classic.

This week, Blizzard stepped in to fix a problem that was brewing in its 40-player MOBA-esque PvP mode Alterac Valley. Players were avoiding PvP altogether, turning it into a glorified reputation farm.

"We’ve seen that players in Alterac Valley were frequently completing the battleground without capturing the objectives, so we’ve made some adjustments to incentivize a focus on the objectives rather than rushing to kill the enemy faction’s general," community manager Kaivax wrote.

The patch basically broke a popular 'zerg' strategy where one side rushed to end the match as fast as possible by beefing up the NPC defenders that stood in their way. Blizzard shifted some of the reputation and honor you'd normally get from killing the generals to the other objectives so players are encouraged to engage with the whole mode.

But, as I've seen with changes like this in the past few weeks, many players are upset that they can't just spam it for the rewards, and that others have already reaped the benefits of the old strategy. There are people in Reddit threads doing the math to highlight how much efficiency has been lost (it doesn't seem like much) and questioning the point of doing Alterac Valley at all.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

While I sympathize with not wanting to do something that feels like a chore, it's a far worse problem to have an entire part of the game flattened into an AFK farm. Alterac Valley is supposed to feel like a large-scale war where you look at a big map and choose what objectives are worth fighting over as you try to weaken the enemy forces. The loot you get from maxing out your reputation with the associated factions is meant to be a reward for putting in the effort to learn what it takes to win.

The whole pitch for WoW: Forever is that it's not going to ditch everything with a new expansion every few years. Numbers can be tweaked down the line if the reputation grind is too much of a slog, but the mode should be fun to play so it's not treated like a box to check off in your character's journey. An inert PvP mode ruins the spirit of the MMO far more than a slightly longer reputation grind does.

I think this is a good example of a healthy change that shows Blizzard is committed to protecting core parts of the experience from becoming a treadmill that nobody enjoys. WoW: Forever's strengths are the ways it encourages you to participate in systems that aren't there to fill progression bars. As Harvey said in his WoW: Forever review, "Forever is proof that old-school MMO design deserves a second chance," and that requires taking a stance on what the game is and isn't meant to be.

Your Flight Takes 10 Minutes Longer Than It Used To—And That’s A Luxury Worth Paying For

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Flying takes longer than it used to – and, retirement of the Concorde notwithstanding, it’s not because planes aren’t as fast. There are more than twice as many commercial flights as there were at the time of airline deregulation, and air traffic control hasn’t been upgraded very much. There’s congested airspace and also congested airports. We also don’t build major new airports – the last one was Denver, 30 years ago. (Austin’s new 1999 airport was a converted military base, and there were others like that in the 90s.) By one estimate flights have gotten 8% longer. That means a two hour flight is 10 minutes longer than it used to be. And the single biggest factor is often tagged as strategic padding of schedules. Airlines are often chided for ‘schedule padding’ – claiming that flights take longer than they should. Often, this just reflects the reality of telling customers when they can expect to arrive – although there’s greater variance than there used to be, which isn’t great for having a gate available when you land early. Airlines do not like this! Though real airfares (even including fees) are down significantly since deregulation, longer flight times are expensive for staffing, fuel and efficient use of $50 – $100 million planes. And longer travel times historically disadvantaged an airline trying to sell tickets, because it meant their flights were displayed lower on the screen that travel agents and later passengers directly saw – which translates into fewer sales. A new study finds that airlines with more “padding” in their schedules tend to charge higher fares. That sounds like passengers paying more for a slower trip. But those extra minutes can also let United hold your plane with ConnectionSaver and get you home that night, and still arrive on time. It also means fewer delays and missed connections. So that ‘premium’ is actually a more valuable product to many consumers. I’ve argued for years that schedule padding often isn’t really padding. Sometimes an airline is finally admitting how long the trip takes. Customers can plan around an honest arrival time. There’s a new chapter by Embry-Riddle’s Jules Yimga, “The Price of Punctuality,” in The Economics of Airline Pricing. It combines 2019 flight time data with the government’s ticket price survey. It compares schedules against different percentiles of actual flight times, then examines how the resulting measures relate to fares. More padding is associated with higher fares, especially when the comparison uses the faster end of actual flight times. The relationship appears in both competitive and concentrated markets. The abstract itself allows that reliability could be a service customers value. (Interestingly, Yimga’s earlier research on schedule padding attributed higher prices primarily to higher costs rather than higher margins.) Even scheduling to the median arrival time means roughly half of trips take longer than published. It’s often better for a customer if an airline underpromises and overdelivers – or arrives ‘on time’ rather than late. Predictability matters when you’re scheduling connections or trying to arrive in time for a meeting. An airline that leaves little buffer is going to appear less reliable, so customers book earlier flights and longer layovers or even fly in the night before an important meeting. Then the buffer hasn’t disappeared, it just changes who’s scheduling it. That’s why I argued American’s investment in more realistic schedules could pay for itself. Avoiding rebooking, mishandled bags and aircraft and crews getting out of position can justify spending running an on-time schedule even before a customer chooses the airline again because the trip met expectations. American’s Dallas – Fort Worth overhaul that began in April combined longer scheduled flight times with a move from nine daily banks of flights to thirteen. Spreading flights into smaller waves reduced congestion that made some of the extra time unnecessary. One plane waiting for a gate can delay its passengers, its next departure, and other flights whose crews are stuck on board. Giving the operation room to recover can prevent that problem from cascading. In January, I covered American network planning chief Brian Znotins explaining that bookings for 40–54 minute connections at DFW were 4% below what its model predicted. Bookings for 55–84 minute connections were 8% above prediction. Customers have actually started choosing longer connections because they feel more reliable and less likely to leave them stranded. When I covered United’s media day in 2019, Greg Hart explained that 25% of its flights each day were expected to arrive ten minutes early. That gave United room to use ConnectionSaver to hold flights for connecting passengers and still arrive on time. Of course there’s such a thing as too much slack. An airline can improve its reported punctuality by moving the promised arrival later without getting anyone anywhere sooner. Excessively long connections waste customer time. And routinely beating an absurdly generous schedule doesn’t make an operation excellent. Not all padding is something customers will pay a premium for. Topics on this pageAirline schedule padding and consumer choice behaviorAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesJules O. It compares schedules against different percentiles of actual flight times, then examines how the resulting measures relate to fares.

American’s Dallas – Fort Worth overhaul that began in April combined longer scheduled flight times with a move from nine daily banks of flights to thirteen. Spreading flights into smaller waves reduced congestion that made some of the extra time unnecessary.

One plane waiting for a gate can delay its passengers, its next departure, and other flights whose crews are stuck on board. Giving the operation room to recover can prevent that problem from cascading.

In January, I covered American network planning chief Brian Znotins explaining that bookings for 40–54 minute connections at DFW were 4% below what its model predicted. Bookings for 55–84 minute connections were 8% above prediction. Customers have actually started choosing longer connections because they feel more reliable and less likely to leave them stranded.

When I covered United’s media day in 2019, Greg Hart explained that 25% of its flights each day were expected to arrive ten minutes early. That gave United room to use ConnectionSaver to hold flights for connecting passengers and still arrive on time.

Of course there’s such a thing as too much slack. An airline can improve its reported punctuality by moving the promised arrival later without getting anyone anywhere sooner. Excessively long connections waste customer time. And routinely beating an absurdly generous schedule doesn’t make an operation excellent. Not all padding is something customers will pay a premium for.

Wizards Is Only Targeting Counterfeit Cards, Not Regular MTG Proxies

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There's a fine line between a proxy and a counterfeit card, and Wizards is only interested in eradicating one of them from the game of Magic.

A wave of controversy hit Magic: The Gathering this week after Wizards of the Coast revealed it is taking legal action against a group of proxy card businesses, collectively referred to as 'PrintMTG Enterprise,' that produce and sell unofficial MTG cards directly to players on several marketplaces. The lawsuit is worth $28.5 million, and it includes strict conditions if Wizards succeeds, such as handing over all card-printing equipment used in the business.

The community was immediately up in arms over the announcement, as it felt like Wizards was taking a huge step forward in a war against proxy cards, but the reality is that your average proxy card is safe, and this is actually more about counterfeit cards. Is there a difference? Yes, and it could be a costly one for 'PrintMTG Enterprises.'

The difference between a counterfeit Magic card and a proxy is that a proxy card is clearly not a real Magic card, typically because it has a plain back or doesn't use official Magic artwork on the front. In most cases, proxy cards have a plain white back, so they can't be confused with an official Magic card.

Counterfeit cards are carbon copies of the original Magic design and are intended to deceive players who aren't all that familiar with the minor details that make a Magic card legit. This can also include using the same paper stock as official Magic cards to make them feel authentic in hand, as well as any details meant to fool the eye.

It's entirely fair for Wizards of the Coast to target any seller looking to reproduce Magic cards and sell them in a condition that could be used to defraud others, and this lawsuit shouldn't be confused with Wizards taking a stance against proxy makers or users.

If you're looking for the best Secret Lair MTG Commander deck to purchase on the secondary market, we're ranking them all from worst to best.

One of the big debates that arose from this lawsuit is why now? It's fair to say that counterfeit Magic cards have been around for a long time, so why is now the time for Wizards to take a stance and look to get them off the market?

The reality is that we'll never know why, but it's a positive move for the secondary market regardless. Proxy cards, when advertised and produced correctly, aren't mistaken for regular Magic cards, and those who buy them are 100% okay with that.

Counterfeit cards become a problem when people look on typically trusted marketplaces for an official card but end up buying a counterfeit version without knowing it, because the seller is trying to make a quick buck.

This isn't to say that the companies producing these counterfeit cards do so with the intent of deceiving players in online transactions, but that's what is happening, and it needs to stop.

I've only ever purchased one counterfeit card in my time playing Magic, and it was a copy of Tibalt's Trickery. It wasn't a very expensive card, but the copy I received was clearly fake, not to the eye, but to the touch. I reported the seller, who had ghosted me after the transaction, and I got my money back, but I wonder how many players that seller managed to fool.

On the proxy front, I only own one set of proxy cards: the Goblin Storm Secret Lair Commander deck. Due to this deck selling out incredibly fast and being scalped for insane prices online, I decided to proxy it instead, but did so from a seller who clearly stated that the backs of the cards would arrive plain white, and that the paper stock used was quite thin (as a warning for when I sleeved the deck). This was perfect, because I planned to use the deck still at my local store, and with my Commander pod, I wanted to be able to say "hey, this is a proxy, does anyone have a problem with that?" as the code of honor among most groups is that, as long as you admit to what it is, there's typically no issue playing with it.

Magic is a very expensive game to get into in most formats, so proxies let you avoid extortionate secondary market prices (especially on land for Commander right now) and still play with limited-availability stuff like Goblin Storm.

Proxies are great, and they should continue to be allowed as long as certain rules are followed. I have no issue with Wizards setting a clear boundary for what a counterfeit card is and why it isn't good for the game.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Teresa Giudice has been holding a grudge against a mystery Hollywood actress for years ... and now the woman who allegedly rubbed the "Real Housewives of New Jersey" star the wrong way has been identified as Sofía Vergara.

A source tells Entertainment Weekly the "Modern Family" actress is the unnamed celebrity Teresa calls out in her new memoir, "The Queen of New Jersey," for an encounter she considered downright rude.

According to Teresa, the two were instructed to pose for a photo while appearing with Mario Lopez in Los Angeles ... but afterward, she allegedly overheard the actress questioning why she'd been asked to take a picture with her.

Teresa says the remark caught her off guard, especially since she didn't think she'd done anything to deserve that kind of attitude.

And apparently, the awkwardness didn't end there ... Teresa recalls spotting the same actress years later at an L.A. restaurant while dining with husband Louie Ruelas.

The kicker? The actress was meeting up with a woman who had just approached Teresa for a fan photo ... something Teresa couldn't resist taking a little satisfaction in.

While Teresa kept the actress's identity under wraps in her book, she's actually called out Sofía before ... blasting her during a 2023 episode of her "Namaste B$tches" podcast and calling her the rudest woman she'd ever met over an alleged 2017 interaction.

Sounds like Teresa hasn't forgotten their run-in ... even if she left Sofía's name out of the book.

Doncaster Rovers 2 Huddersfield Town 3: 10-man Terriers emphatically end their barren league streak at bogey venue

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Certainly for the victor, if there happens to be one.

Do the business, put your feet up - well, sort of - with three points in the bag ahead of a long weekend and watch the rest of your divisional rivals sweat it out.

That was the hope for both Doncaster Rovers and Huddersfield Town in their differing third-tier missions.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

The incentive for Rovers was moving out of the drop zone up to 18th, while Town’s ambition revolved around securing the win that would draw them level with leaders Stockport County at the right end of the table.

Huddersfield were the ones smiling as they finally made hay at league level at what had previously been a bogey ground.

The first ever visitors here back on New Year’s Day 2007 - the 20th anniversary is in a month’s time - Town had not so much as scored in any of their previous four visits at league level.

They emphatically put paid to that on a night when they played with a man light for around an hour. You didn’t really guess.

For the first 34 minutes, this game simply wasn’t a contest. There were 19 places between these two rivals ahead of kick-off and it showed as Town cruised into a 2-0 lead, courtesy of Cameron Humphreys’ first goal of the campaign and a 15th strike of a goal-laden season for Joe Taylor before Bali Mumba’s Donny demons resurfaced.

In the league fixture here in February, a panicking handball saw him concede a penalty in the game-breaking moment.

In the present, he was the guilty party again - towards the same goal. He hauled down Jamie Sterry, who got on the wrong side of him following Robbie Gotts’ spotter’s badge of a pass.

Booked earlier for a sliding challenge he couldn’t complain about, Mumba was dismissed for a second yellow card—his second dismissal of the season.

Isaac Hutchinson sent the underemployed Matt Young the wrong way to make it 2-1 and out of nowhere, we had a game on.

But Town, not Rovers, grabbed the all-important fourth goal.

Unconvincing at the back, the hosts erred for a third time and Ilias Bronkhurst fired in a soothing third.

Even without an extra player, it was Town who had the wit going forward as Rovers lacked penetration and quality. Given their defensive charity, it wasn’t a good mix and it's easy to see why they are where they are in the standings.

A stoppage-time goal from home captain Owen Bailey revived Rovers, but it was not enough. Nowhere near.

Rovers, without the ineligible Alfie May under the terms of his loan, handed a first start to loanee James Collins, now finally over his long-term knee injury.

It was a psychologically important night for the veteran striker, who was injured almost exactly a year to the day on December 2, 2025 - ironically against Town. It was his last start.

Collins, who turns 36 on Tuesday, was afforded an early birthday present in the process. Sterry also returned to the Rovers’ starting line-up following illness. Brandon Hanlan dropped to the bench, with Tom Nixon absent.

Joe Low and Fletcher - lined up from the off for Town, coming in for Ibane Bowat and Bojan Radulovic.

Ahead of the game, Martin Drury had spoken about Rovers’ set-piece threat, with Low’s promotion seemingly something to do with that.

A gulf in class saw Town stroll most of the first-half, aided by the meek defensive tendencies which Rovers had avoided five days earlier at Bradford City. It eventually changed.

The promotion of Ashley Fletcher, who had delivered a fine cameo in the Trophy game here in early autumn - looked the shrewdest of moves from Drury.

He teed up Humphreys for an easy low finish for Town’s opener on five minutes after Mumba’s cross caused initial consternation as Rovers prevaricated at the back terribly.

Town mixed play up impressively and afforded themselves plenty of control and possession.

Last-ditch defending from ex-Terriers man Matty Pearson blocked Bronkhorst’s goalbound effort. A similarly alert block from Jack Senior repeated the trick to deny Low.

Town’s second was not long in coming on 21 minutes with Rovers again spooked by Fletcher, who showed adept chest control and neat footballing skills to set up Taylor, who fired home a clinical low volley as Rovers again stood and watched.

A brief half-chant of 'We turn up, why don't you' arrived from disgruntled Rovers patrons in the Black Bank.

Mumba’s indiscretion changed the complexion of the night and Thomas spurned a chance to level before the break, planting a free header over.

Ahead of the resumption, Grant McCann threw on another attack-minded player in Hakeeb Adelakun. His side saw a lot of the ball in the opening part of the second period, but couldn’t conjure a big chance against 10-man Town.

It was the visitors, who still possessed the killer instinct.

Sanches Fernandes rattled the crossbar and from the resultant corner, Rovers’ defensive deficiencies again re-surfaced.

The ball found Bronkhorst and after his initial effort was blocked, he rammed home a low drive in front of the ecstatic 1,905 Terriers supporters.

Bailey ensured there was a nervy finale for the visitors, firing home from Adelakun's low cross. But it was Town's night, deservedly.

Doncaster Rovers: Simkin; Sterry, Grehan (Ayinde 70), Pearson, Senior, Thomas (Husband 84); Gotts, Bailey, Broadbent (Adelakun HT), Hutchinson (O’Reilly 65); J Collins (Hanlan 65). Substitutes unused: Clarke, Bryne. Huddersfield Town: Young; Balker, Low, Roughan; Bronkhorst, A Collins (Ledson 69), Humphreys, Mumba, Sanches Fernandes (Bowat 84), Taylor (Radulovic 84); Fletcher (Harness 55). Substitutes unused: Alnwick, Gooch, Ashia.

Attendance: 8,376 (1,905 Huddersfield Town supporters).

Diesel tanker carrying up to 7,000 tonnes capsizes near Ibiza, killing one

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Med Emre vessel capsized near Ibiza and Spain's Clara Campoamor, a pollution control vessel, was sent to assess the environmental impact

An oil tanker carrying up to 7,000 tonnes of diesel has capsized off the coast of Ibiza, leaving one crew member dead, authorities have said.

The Med Emre, a Malta-flagged vessel, was carrying 17 crew members who were all rescued. However seven were taken to hospital and one was later pronounced dead, Balearic Islands emergency services confirmed.

The six crew receiving treatment reportedly suffered minor injuries, such as hypothermia, Spanish media reports.

The possible environmental damage is being assessed by a plane and Spain's pollution control vessel, Clara Campoamor, which is owned by the country's national maritime rescue service.

"There is still little information available about a possible fuel spill," Balearic Islands' President Marga Prohens wrote on X.

Elena Delgado, maritime captain of Valencia, told news agency AFP that a small slick that had formed around the boat had "disappeared".

"The situation is stabilised," she said, adding that further inspections would be carried out on Friday morning.

Earlier, regional emergencies director Pablo GÃ¡rriz said the tanker had not completely sunk, as had originally been reported. The vessel remains partially submerged.

The Med Emre capsized 25 nautical miles (46 km; 29 miles) from Ibiza's coastline and 57 nautical miles from Valencia. It is unclear what caused the sinking.

The chemical tanker was en route from Algeciras, in southern Spain, to Barcelona in the north-east.

Rescuers in Valencia received a distress call around 22:30 local time (21:30 GMT) on Wednesday, according to Spanish media. Minutes later, the vessel reported a leak and the crew said it was leaning at a 90-degree angle.

The injured crew members have been taken to hospitals in Valencia and Can Misses Hospital in Ibiza, President Prohens said.

Ibiza's marine environment was added to the Unesco World Heritage List in 1999. In particular, it is home to dense prairies of Posidonia oceanic seagrass, which is found only in the Mediterranean basin and plays a vital role in supporting local marine biodiversity.

In 2002, Spain experienced one of its worst environmental disasters when the Prestige oil tanker ran into trouble off Galicia's coastline. It spilled more than 60,000 tonnes of heavy fuel oil into the sea.
